Understood. Below is the complete compressed revision in plain text. I avoided Markdown formatting symbols like backticks, asterisks, tables, and code fences. Code lines are shown as plain indented text.

1. FLOATING-POINT LITERAL f SUFFIX

- 321.5 is a double by default.
- 321.5f is a float literal.
- f or F suffix tells the compiler the literal type is float.
- Example:
    float t = 321.5f;
- Interview point: Floating-point literals are double by default. The f suffix makes the literal float.

2. MULTI-CHARACTER CHARACTER CONSTANT

- char a = 'abc'; gives Clang warning: multi-character character constant.
- It also warns about conversion from int to char.
- On tested system: 'abc' becomes 6382179.
- 6382179 converted to char becomes 99.
- 99 is 'c'. Output was c.
- 'abc' is not a normal character literal.
- Normal character literal has one character: char a = 'a';
- Multi-character constants have implementation-dependent behavior.
- Do not say 'abc' always gives the last character.
- Interview trap: A multi-character character constant is permitted in C++, but its value is implementation-defined. Converting it to char can produce implementation-dependent results.

3. sizeof(pointer) VS STRING SIZE

- Example:
    const char* b = "abcd";
    std::cout << b << std::endl;
    std::cout << sizeof(b) << std::endl;
- Output:
    abcd
    8
- b is a pointer to the first character of the string literal.
- sizeof(b) gives pointer size, not string size.
- On 64-bit Apple Silicon, pointer is 8 bytes.
- sizeof("abcd") is 5 because string literal contains 'a','b','c','d','\0'.
- Key distinction:
    sizeof(b) gives pointer size, usually 8
    sizeof("abcd") gives character array size, 5

4. const PRACTICAL USE

- Example:
    const float PI = 3.141526f;
    PI = -42; // error
- Once PI is const, value cannot be modified after initialization.
- Clang error: cannot assign to variable 'PI' with const-qualified type 'const float'
- Use const for values that should remain unchanged:
    const float PI = 3.141526f;
    const int MAX_USERS = 100;
    const int DAYS_IN_WEEK = 7;
- This prevents accidental modification.
- Modifying const is a compile-time error, not merely a warning.
- Both commands fail:
    clang++ -std=c++17 const.cpp -o const
    clang++ -Werror -std=c++17 const.cpp -o const
- -Werror is not responsible for this particular error.
- Interview point: const enforces that an object cannot be modified after initialization. Attempting to modify it results in a compile-time error.

5. COMPILER FLAGS OBSERVED

-std=c++17
- Compiles according to C++17 standard.
- Example: clang++ -std=c++17 file.cpp -o file

-Werror
- Treats compiler warnings as errors.
- Example: clang++ -Werror file.cpp -o file

Important distinction:
- Warning plus -Werror becomes an error.
- Actual language error is an error regardless of -Werror.
- -Werror does not create every error. It only promotes warnings to errors.

6. BLOCK SCOPE AND LIFETIME

- Curly braces { } can create their own scope, even without if, for, while, or function.
- Example:
    {
        int x = 7;
    }
    std::cout << x; // error
- x is declared inside inner block, so accessible only within that block.
- After closing brace, x is no longer accessible.
- Compiler error: use of undeclared identifier 'x'
- Outer scope variable can generally be accessed from inner scope:
    int x = 10;
    {
        std::cout << x; // OK
    }
- Inner scope variable cannot be accessed from outer scope:
    {
        int x = 10;
    }
    std::cout << x; // error
- Scope means where variable is accessible.
- Lifetime means how long variable exists during execution.
- For local x, lifetime ends when execution leaves block.
- Interview point: Scope determines where a variable can be accessed in source code. A variable declared inside a block has block scope and cannot be accessed outside that block.

7. std::iota()

- Header: #include <numeric>
- Fills a range with consecutive values.
- Example:
    int ids[100];
    std::iota(std::begin(ids), std::end(ids), 0);
- Produces 0,1,2,...,99.
- General form: std::iota(first, last, starting_value);
- Values increase by 1 for each element.

8. std::array::at() PERFORMS BOUNDS CHECKING

- Example: std::array<int, 10> arr;
- Valid indexes are 0 through 9.
- arr.at(5) valid.
- arr.at(9) valid.
- arr.at(200) throws std::out_of_range.
- Invalid index does not necessarily cause compilation error. Check happens at runtime.
- If exception not caught, program terminates:
    libc++abi: terminating due to uncaught exception
- at() vs operator[]:
    arr.at(i); // bounds-checked
    arr[i]; // no bounds checking
- at() throws std::out_of_range for invalid index.
- operator[] invalid access results in undefined behavior.
- Interview trap: Do not say std::array automatically prevents out-of-bounds access. It provides two access mechanisms with different behavior.
- Practical takeaway: Use at() when runtime bounds checking and safer access are important. Use operator[] when index is known valid.

9. RANGE-BASED FOR WITH A REFERENCE

- Example:
    for (int& x : arr) {
        std::cout << x << std::endl;
    }
- int& makes x a reference to the actual element.
- Modifying x modifies original array element:
    for (int& x : arr) {
        x = 100;
    }
- For read-only access, use const reference:
    for (const int& x : arr)

10. std::fill()

- Header: #include <algorithm>
- Fills every element in a range with same value.
- Example:
    std::fill(std::begin(arr2), std::end(arr2), 1024);
- Result: every element becomes 1024.
- Pattern: std::fill(begin, end, value);
- Include the header that officially provides functionality, not relying on indirect inclusion.
- Useful when entire range needs to be initialized or reset to same value.

11. TRAILING RETURN TYPE SYNTAX

- Normal syntax: float add(float, float);
- Trailing return type: auto add(float, float) -> float;
- Both declare same function.
- General form: auto function_name(parameters) -> return_type;
- Example:
    auto multiply(int a, int b) -> int {
        return a * b;
    }
- The auto in auto add(float, float) -> float; does not mean compiler is deducing return type. Return type is explicitly specified by -> float.
- Trailing return types are useful for complex function declarations, especially when return type depends on parameters.

12. ADDRESS-OF &x AND &(x)

- &x and &(x) produce same address.
- Parentheses around expression do not change meaning of address-of operator.

13. char* SPECIAL std::cout BEHAVIOR

- For char c = 'g'; expression &c has type char*.
- When char* is sent to std::cout, it is treated as pointer to C-style null-terminated string, not simply printing address.
- std::cout << &c; may print characters from that memory location until '\0'.
- Experiment: &c -> g, &d -> hg, &e -> ihg.
- These are not valid strings in program; behavior comes from reading memory as C-string and should not be relied upon.
- To force pointer-address printing: std::cout << (void*)&c;

14. FUNCTION POINTER VS OBJECT POINTER WITH cout

- For void foo() {}, &foo is a function pointer.
- Directly sending it to std::cout produced 1 because non-null function pointer converts to bool true, printed as 1.
- Explicitly converting to void* on tested platform produced address like 0x1000bc4e8.
- Function-pointer-to-void* conversion is not portable ISO C++.
- Interview trap: Do not assume every pointer behaves identically with std::cout.
- char* is interpreted as C-string by ostream.
- void* is printed as address.
- function pointer is different pointer category.
- Type of pointer determines which operator<< overload/conversion is selected.

15. REFERENCES AS ALIASES

- int x = 21; int& y = x;
- y is reference alias to x, not separate int object.
- Changes through either name affect same object:
    y = 100; also changes x.
- References must be initialized:
    int& z; // invalid
    int& z = x; // valid
- Normal reference does not have uninitialized/null state like pointer.

Reference to pointer:
- int* f = nullptr;
- int*& w = f;
- f is pointer to int.
- w is reference to f, which is pointer to int.
- Modifying w modifies f:
    w = new int(10); changes pointer stored in f.
- Useful when function needs to modify caller's pointer.

typeid and references:
- Although int& y = x; declares y as reference, typeid(y) reports type of referenced object, int.
- int*& w = f; typeid(w) reports underlying pointer type.
- typeid(...).name() output like i or Pi is implementation/ABI dependent.

Interview trap:
- int* is pointer to int.
- int& is reference to int.
- int*& is reference to pointer to int.
- Last one useful when function needs to change caller's pointer itself.

16. const AS TYPE QUALIFIER

- const is type qualifier, not standalone type.
- const variable must have explicit type:
    const int x = 43; // valid
    const x = 43; // invalid
- const prevents modification through that declaration:
    const int x = 43;
    x = 21; // compile-time error
- Checking const-qualification at compile time:
    #include <type_traits>
    std::is_const_v<int> // false / 0
    std::is_const_v<const int> // true / 1
- decltype(x) retrieves declared type:
    const int x = 43;
    std::is_const<decltype(x)>::value // true
- C++17 shorthand:
    std::is_const<T>::value
    std::is_const_v<T>
- Interview point: const qualifies an existing type; it does not replace the type itself.

17. DYNAMIC MEMORY WITH new AND delete

- new dynamically allocates object on heap:
    int* p = new int;
- new[] dynamically allocates array:
    int* ids = new int[n];
- Correct deallocation pairing:
    new -> delete
    new[] -> delete[]
- Examples:
    int* p = new int;
    delete p;
    int* arr = new int[100];
    delete[] arr;
- Using correct delete form is essential.
- Memory leak: if dynamically allocated memory not released:
    int* arr = new int[100000];
    // delete[] arr; forgotten
- For 100000 integers, assuming 4-byte int: 100000 * 4 = 400000 bytes.
- Valgrind reported: definitely lost: 400,000 bytes in 1 blocks.
- When delete[] arr restored: definitely lost: 0 bytes.
- -g debug information:
    clang++ -Wall -std=c++17 -g dynamic.cpp -o dynamic
- Adds debug info, helps debugging tools associate runtime info with source locations.
- Memory leak tools:
    macOS leaks can detect leaked allocations.
    Valgrind Memcheck reports categories: definitely lost, indirectly lost, possibly lost, still reachable.
- Important: Process memory footprint is not same as leaked memory. Process may have large physical footprint while reporting zero leaks.
- Interview trap: new pairs with delete, new[] pairs with delete[]. Do not use delete for memory allocated with new[].

18. std::vector: sizeof() VS size() VS capacity()

- Vector has two parts conceptually:
    1. Small vector object stored directly as variable.
    2. Dynamically allocated element storage, usually on heap.
- std::vector<int> v;
- sizeof(v) measures size of vector object itself, not dynamically allocated elements.
- On tested 64-bit libc++ environment: sizeof(v) == 24.
- It remained 24 even after adding more elements.
- v.size() returns number of elements currently stored.
- Example: v.push_back(1); repeated 6 times; v.size() is 6. After five more insertions, v.size() is 11.
- v.capacity() returns number of elements for which vector currently has allocated storage.
- Capacity can be greater than size because vectors allocate extra space for efficient push_back.
- Key distinction:
    sizeof(v) -> size of vector object itself
    v.size() -> number of elements currently stored
    v.capacity() -> allocated element capacity
- Interview trap: sizeof(std::vector<int>) does NOT tell how much memory vector's elements are using.
- Exact size/layout of std::vector is implementation-dependent. 24 bytes is observation, not universal guarantee.

19. ARRAY PARAMETER -> POINTER ADJUSTMENT

- When C-style array is used as function parameter:
    void print(const int arr[])
- It is adjusted to pointer parameter:
    void print(const int* arr)
- Therefore sizeof(arr) returns size of pointer, not size of original array.
- Example:
    int array[] = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5};
    print(array);
- Inside print(): sizeof(arr) is 8 bytes on typical 64-bit system.
- Original array contains 5 integers, but size info not available through arr inside function.
- Key rule: Array arguments decay to pointers when passed to functions.
- If C-style array function needs element count, size commonly passed separately:
    void print(const int arr[], int size);
- Interview trap: sizeof(array) in caller can give entire array size, but sizeof(arr) inside function gives pointer size when arr is array parameter.

20. POINTER SAFETY: THREE CRITICAL BUGS

1. Dereferencing nullptr
- int* ptr = nullptr;
- *ptr = 100; // error
- nullptr represents null pointer value.
- Dereferencing attempts to access invalid/null address, commonly segmentation fault or access violation.
- Debuggers like LLDB can show pointer value as 0x0.

2. Dangling pointer
- Returning address of local variable is unsafe:
    int* returnMemory() {
        int result = 91;
        return &result; // error
    }
- result is local object whose lifetime ends when function returns.
- Returned pointer becomes dangling pointer.
- Dereferencing is undefined behavior, even if particular run prints old value.

3. Double delete
- int* x = new int;
- delete x;
- delete x; // error
- First delete releases allocated memory. Second attempts to release same allocation again, resulting in undefined behavior and commonly double-free.
- After manual deletion, defensive pattern:
    delete x;
    x = nullptr;
- Deleting a null pointer is safe.

Debugging tool:
- clang++ -g -fsanitize=address -std=c++17 file.cpp -o file
- -g includes debugging information.
- -fsanitize=address enables AddressSanitizer (ASan), which detects use-after-free, double-free, buffer overflows, etc.

Interview traps:
- nullptr is not valid memory to dereference.
- Dangling pointer can contain seemingly valid address; problem is pointed-to object's lifetime has ended.
- Undefined behavior may appear to work during one run and fail during another.
- Compiler warning does not necessarily mean program will fail to compile. Dangling-pointer example compiled but produced warning.

21. FUNCTION POINTERS AND std::function

- Function pointer stores address of function and can select which function executes at runtime.
- int (*op)(int, int);
- Means op can point to function that accepts two int arguments and returns int.
- Example:
    op = add;
    op(2, 2);
- Function can change at runtime:
    if (choice == 1) op = add;
    else op = multiply;
- Useful for runtime-selectable operations, callbacks, dispatch tables, etc.
- Function pointer type alias:
    typedef int (*Operation)(int, int);
    Operation op;
- Modern C++ alternative:
    using Operation = int (*)(int, int);
- std::function:
    std::function<int(int, int)> op;
- std::function is general-purpose callable wrapper. It can store functions, function pointers, lambdas, and other callable objects.
- Example:
    op = add;
    std::cout << op(2, 3);
- Empty std::function: default-constructed contains no callable:
    std::function<int(int,int)> op;
- Calling it directly: op(2, 3); throws std::bad_function_call.
- Can be checked before calling:
    if (op) std::cout << op(2, 3);
- Comparison:
    Function pointer: lightweight; can point to compatible functions; can be null; calling null pointer is undefined behavior.
    std::function: general callable wrapper; can store functions, lambdas, function objects; can be empty; calling empty throws std::bad_function_call.
- Interview point: Both provide runtime polymorphism over operations, but std::function is more general while raw function pointer is simpler and typically has less abstraction/overhead.

22. LVALUE REFERENCES

- Non-const lvalue reference can bind to lvalue:
    int a = 10;
    int& x = a; // OK
- It cannot bind to temporary/rvalue:
    int& x = 12; // error
- const lvalue reference:
    const int& x = 12; // OK
- const lvalue reference can bind to both lvalues and rvalues/temporaries.
- Temporary's lifetime is extended to lifetime of reference in direct-initialization case.
- Rvalue reference:
    int&& x = 12; // OK
- && is rvalue reference and is designed to bind to rvalues.
- Comparison:
    int& can bind lvalue, not rvalue.
    const int& can bind lvalue and rvalue.
    int&& can bind rvalue, not lvalue.
- Compiler experiment: int& x = 12; gives error: non-const lvalue reference cannot bind to a temporary.
- But const int& x = 12; compiles.
- Interview point: Non-const lvalue reference T& cannot bind to rvalue/temporary, while const T& and T&& can.

23. RVALUE REFERENCES

- Rvalue reference declared using &&, can bind to temporary/rvalue objects.
- int&& x = 12; // OK
- Here 12 is rvalue and x is rvalue reference.
- Lvalue reference vs rvalue reference:
    int a = 10;
    int& x = a; // OK lvalue reference
    int&& y = 12; // OK rvalue reference
- Comparison:
    int& can bind lvalue, not rvalue.
    const int& can bind lvalue and rvalue.
    int&& can bind rvalue, not lvalue.
- Compiler experiment: int& x = 12; gives Clang error: non-const lvalue reference to type 'int' cannot bind to a temporary.
- Because 12 is rvalue/temporary and int& can only bind to lvalue.
- But int&& x = 12; compiles because int&& is rvalue reference.
- Rvalue reference with std::string:
    std::string s1 = "Mike";
    std::string s2 = "This is really a very long value";
    std::string&& x = s1 + s2;
- s1 + s2 produces temporary std::string object.
- x is reference/alias to temporary std::string.
- x is not a new string object.
- Temporary lifetime: when temporary directly bound to rvalue reference, temporary's lifetime extended to lifetime of x.
- Assembly showed calls related to std::operator+ and std::basic_string::~basic_string(), demonstrating temporary std::string created and eventually destroyed.
- Practical use: rvalue references are important for move semantics, move constructors, move assignment operators, avoiding unnecessary copies, efficient handling of temporary objects, perfect forwarding.
- Interview trap: Do not say references cannot bind to temporaries.
- Precise rule: Non-const lvalue reference T& cannot bind to rvalue/temporary, while const T& and T&& can.

24. MOVE SEMANTICS

- Move semantics allow C++ to transfer/steal resources from one object to another instead of expensive copy.
- Example:
    std::string myString = "copy construct me";
    std::string newValue;
    newValue = std::move(myString);
- std::move(myString) converts myString into rvalue expression, allowing move assignment operator to be selected.
- std::move() does not actually move data by itself.
- It essentially performs a cast that allows object to be treated as rvalue:
    newValue = static_cast<std::string&&>(myString);
- So: std::move(myString) -> treat myString as rvalue -> move assignment operator -> resources transferred to newValue.
- Example output:
    myString:
    newValue: copy construct me
- Contents transferred to newValue.
- Moved-from object: after newValue = std::move(myString); myString is still valid object, but value is valid but unspecified.
- For std::string, commonly observed to become empty:
    myString -> valid, but contents unspecified
    newValue -> "copy construct me"
- Do not assume every moved-from object will necessarily become empty.
- Copy vs move:
    Copy: std::string newValue = myString; both objects retain own resources/value.
    Move: std::string newValue = std::move(myString); resources transferred. Source remains valid but previous value should not be relied upon.
- Important: std::string&& x = s1 + s2; and newValue = std::move(myString); both involve rvalue references/rvalue expressions. std::move() specifically tells C++: this object can be treated as rvalue; its resources may be moved from.
- Interview point: std::move() does not itself move anything. It is essentially a cast to an rvalue reference. Actual move happens when appropriate move constructor or move assignment operator is selected.

25. RAW POINTERS AND OWNERSHIP

- Raw pointer can point to dynamically allocated memory, and multiple raw pointers can point to same memory.
- Example:
    int* arr = new int[100];
    int* ptr = arr;
    int* ptr2 = ptr;
- All three pointers refer to same dynamically allocated array.
- Raw pointers do not automatically manage memory.
- Programmer is responsible for releasing memory:
    delete[] arr;
- If delete[] forgotten, memory leak can occur.
- new[] must match delete[]:
    int* arr = new int[100];
    delete[] arr; // correct
    delete arr; // wrong
- Compiler warning: 'delete' applied to a pointer that was allocated with 'new[]'
- Valgrind also detected: Mismatched free() / delete / delete []
- Correct pairing:
    new T -> delete
    new T[n] -> delete[]
- Dangling pointer: after delete[] arr; ptr still contains old address, but memory no longer owned/valid.
- Dereferencing ptr: std::cout << *ptr; is undefined behavior.
- Dangerous: delete[] arr; int* ptr2 = ptr; std::cout << *ptr2; Both point to memory already released.
- Creating another pointer does not create another copy of allocated memory.
- Valgrind experiment: With delete arr; after new int[100]; Valgrind reported ERROR SUMMARY: 1 errors. After changing to delete[] arr; ERROR SUMMARY: 0 errors.
- Ownership: Raw pointers can share address, but that does not tell C++ who owns memory, who should delete it, when it should be deleted.
- This makes manual memory management error-prone.
- Smart pointers: std::unique_ptr, std::shared_ptr, std::weak_ptr help express and manage ownership automatically.
- std::unique_ptr important when there should be one clear owner of dynamically allocated memory.
- Interview points:
    Raw pointers do not automatically manage dynamically allocated memory.
    Multiple raw pointers can point to same memory.
    new pairs with delete.
    new[] pairs with delete[].
    Accessing memory after delete/delete[] creates dangling-pointer problem and can cause undefined behavior.
    std::unique_ptr provides RAII-based ownership and automatically releases resource when it goes out of scope.

26. SMART POINTERS, RAII, std::unique_ptr

RAII:
- Resource lifetime automatically managed with object lifetime.
- Example:
    class UDT {
    public:
        UDT() { std::cout << "UDT Created" << std::endl; }
        ~UDT() { std::cout << "UDT Destructed" << std::endl; }
    };
    int main() {
        UDT mike;
        return 0;
    }
- Output: UDT Created, then UDT Destructed.
- Object scope ends, destructor automatically called.

Smart pointer:
- Class/object that automatically manages ownership and lifetime of dynamically allocated memory.
- Main smart pointers: std::unique_ptr, std::shared_ptr, std::weak_ptr.

Empty unique_ptr:
- std::unique_ptr<UDT> mike; initially mike is nullptr. No UDT object created.

unique_ptr with dynamically allocated object:
    std::unique_ptr<UDT> mike = std::unique_ptr<UDT>(new UDT);
- Flow: new UDT -> heap object -> unique_ptr owns object -> scope ends -> unique_ptr destructor -> UDT destructor -> memory released automatically.
- Manual delete mike; is wrong. delete is for raw pointers, not unique_ptr object.

unique_ptr automatically deletes:
- At end of main scope, unique_ptr destructor -> UDT destructor -> memory released.
- Therefore manually delete not needed.

unique_ptr for arrays:
    std::unique_ptr<UDT[]> arr = std::unique_ptr<UDT[]>(new UDT[10]);
- Better modern C++:
    std::unique_ptr<UDT[]> arr = std::make_unique<UDT[]>(10);
- make_unique preferred. 10 objects created and automatically destroyed at scope end.
- No manual delete[] required.

unique_ptr = unique ownership:
- Main rule: one resource has one owner.
- Copy not allowed:
    std::unique_ptr<UDT> mike = std::make_unique<UDT>();
    std::unique_ptr<UDT> joe = mike; // compilation error
- Reason: if copy allowed, same resource could have multiple owners, against unique_ptr ownership model.

Ownership transfer using std::move:
- Copy not allowed: std::unique_ptr<UDT> joe = mike; // error
- But ownership transfer allowed:
    std::unique_ptr<UDT> joe = std::move(mike);
- Before: mike owns UDT, joe is nullptr.
- After: mike is nullptr, joe owns UDT.
- Important: std::move() does not itself move object; it makes expression rvalue to enable move operation/constructor.

Raw pointer vs unique_ptr:
- Raw pointer: ownership unclear; manual delete required for owned heap memory; memory leak risk; dangling pointer risk; copy allowed; multiple pointers possible; manual memory management.
- unique_ptr: ownership clearly represented; automatic cleanup; RAII prevents normal ownership leaks; safer lifetime management; copy prohibited; one unique owner; scope-based management.
- Important: Multiple raw pointers can point to same object, but that does not mean all are owners.

unique_ptr and RAII:
- Scope ends -> mike destroyed -> owned UDT destroyed -> memory released.
- This is RAII-based automatic resource management.

Practical use-cases:
- Dynamically allocated object has single owner.
- Ownership clearly represented.
- Avoid manual delete.
- Exception-safe memory management.
- Manage dynamically allocated arrays.
- Modern C++ generally prefer std::unique_ptr<T> instead of manual T* ptr = new T; delete ptr;

Interview traps:
1. unique_ptr copy not allowed:
    auto p2 = p1; // error
    auto p2 = std::move(p1); // OK
2. unique_ptr not for shared ownership. Use std::shared_ptr<T>.
3. std::move() does not itself move memory. It enables move semantics/ownership transfer.
4. Do not manually delete smart pointer:
    delete ptr; // wrong if ptr is unique_ptr
    delete[] ptr; // wrong if ptr is unique_ptr
5. Array use:
    std::unique_ptr<T[]> arr = std::make_unique<T[]>(10);

Core rule:
RAII -> object lifetime controls resource lifetime -> unique_ptr -> single ownership -> scope ends -> automatic destruction.

27. SHARED POINTERS AND std::shared_ptr

- shared_ptr allows multiple pointers to share ownership of same dynamically allocated object.
- Example:
    class UDT { ... };
    std::shared_ptr<UDT> ptr1 = std::make_shared<UDT>();

Shared ownership:
    std::shared_ptr<UDT> ptr1 = std::make_shared<UDT>();
    {
        std::shared_ptr<UDT> ptr2 = ptr1;
        std::cout << "Use count: " << ptr2.use_count() << std::endl;
    }
- Initially ptr1 owns UDT, use_count = 1.
- After copying, ptr1 and ptr2 share UDT, use_count = 2.
- When ptr2 scope ends, use_count = 1.
- When ptr1 also destroyed, use_count = 0, UDT destructor called.

use_count():
- Tells how many shared_ptr objects currently own same resource.
- ptr1 only -> 1.
- ptr1 + ptr2 -> 2.
- ptr1 + ptr2 + ptr3 -> 3.

Scope and shared_ptr:
- Outer scope ptr1 created: use_count = 1.
- Inner scope ptr2 created: use_count = 2.
- Inner scope ends ptr2 destroyed: use_count = 1.
- Outer scope ends ptr1 destroyed: use_count = 0 -> UDT destroyed.

shared_ptr vs unique_ptr:
- unique_ptr: unique ownership; copy not allowed; multiple owners no; reference count no; automatic cleanup yes; main purpose single ownership.
- shared_ptr: shared ownership; copy allowed; multiple owners yes; reference count yes; automatic cleanup yes; main purpose shared ownership.
- Example:
    std::unique_ptr<UDT> p1 = std::make_unique<UDT>();
    // std::unique_ptr<UDT> p2 = p1; // error
    std::shared_ptr<UDT> p1 = std::make_shared<UDT>();
    std::shared_ptr<UDT> p2 = p1; // OK

make_shared:
- Preferred: std::shared_ptr<UDT> ptr = std::make_shared<UDT>();
- Instead of: std::shared_ptr<UDT> ptr = std::shared_ptr<UDT>(new UDT);
- Modern C++ generally prefers std::make_shared.

Reference count and thread safety:
- shared_ptr reference-count/control-block management is thread-safe.
- Multiple threads can safely manage their shared_ptr copies.
- Important: shared_ptr does not automatically make pointed-to object thread-safe.
- If two threads modify same object's data, synchronization may be needed.

Ownership vs pointer:
- Raw pointer: UDT* p1 = ...; UDT* p2 = p1; Multiple pointers same object, but ownership not clearly defined.
- shared_ptr: auto p1 = std::make_shared<UDT>(); auto p2 = p1; Explicitly shared ownership.

Practical use-cases:
- Multiple parts of program need to own same object.
- Object lifetime depends on multiple owners.
- Share ownership instead of transfer.
- Resource destroyed when last owner disappears.

Interview traps:
1. shared_ptr copyable: auto p2 = p1; // OK. Increases reference count.
2. Last owner destroyed, object destroyed: use_count 2 -> 1 -> 0 -> object destroyed.
3. shared_ptr thread-safe is not equal to object thread-safe.
4. shared_ptr overhead greater than unique_ptr because it maintains shared ownership/control-block/reference counting.

Core rule:
unique_ptr -> single ownership -> no copying -> std::move for ownership transfer.
shared_ptr -> shared ownership -> copy allowed -> reference count -> last owner destroyed -> resource destroyed.

28. WEAK POINTERS AND std::weak_ptr

- weak_ptr is non-owning smart pointer that observes object but does not take ownership.
- Example:
    std::shared_ptr<UDT> ptr1 = std::make_shared<UDT>();
    std::weak_ptr<UDT> ptr2 = ptr1;
- ptr2 observes UDT but does not own it.

weak_ptr does not increase reference count:
    std::shared_ptr<UDT> ptr1 = std::make_shared<UDT>();
    std::weak_ptr<UDT> ptr2 = ptr1;
    std::cout << ptr2.use_count() << std::endl;
- Output: Use count: 1.
- If ptr2 were shared_ptr, output would be 2.
- Core difference:
    shared_ptr shares ownership and increases count.
    weak_ptr does not share ownership and count unchanged.

Scope behaviour:
    {
        std::shared_ptr<UDT> ptr1 = std::make_shared<UDT>();
        {
            std::weak_ptr<UDT> ptr2 = ptr1;
            std::cout << ptr2.use_count() << std::endl;
        }
        std::cout << ptr1.use_count() << std::endl;
    }
- Output:
    UDT Created
    Use count: 1
    use count: 1
    UDT Destructed
- ptr2 destroy does not destroy object because ptr2 is not owner.
- Actual owner is ptr1. After ptr1 destroyed, use_count = 0, UDT destructed.

Why weak_ptr:
- Non-owning reference.
- Safer handling of dangling pointer situations.
- Break shared_ptr reference cycles.
- Check whether object is alive without ownership.

weak_ptr cannot be directly dereferenced:
    std::weak_ptr<UDT> ptr2 = ptr1;
    // *ptr2; // error
- Must use lock():
    std::shared_ptr<UDT> temp = ptr2.lock();
- If object alive: weak_ptr -> lock() -> shared_ptr -> UDT.
- If object destroyed:
    if (auto temp = ptr2.lock()) {
        // Object alive
    } else {
        // Object already destroyed
    }

expired():
- weak_ptr::expired() checks whether managed object has been destroyed.
    if (ptr2.expired()) {
        std::cout << "Object destroyed";
    }
- Normally lock() preferred when object also needs safe access.

shared_ptr cyclic dependency problem:
- With shared_ptr, cycle can be created:
    Object A has shared_ptr to Object B.
    Object B has shared_ptr to Object A.
- Reference counts never reach 0.
- Result: memory leak.

weak_ptr breaks cycle:
- Object A has shared_ptr to Object B.
- Object B has weak_ptr to Object A.
- weak_ptr does not increase reference count.
- Cycle does not create ownership.

shared_ptr vs weak_ptr:
- shared_ptr: shared ownership; copy allowed; reference count increases; keeps object alive; direct dereference yes; lock no; break cycles no; main purpose shared ownership.
- weak_ptr: non-owning; copy allowed; reference count increases no; keeps object alive no; direct dereference no; lock yes; break cycles yes; main purpose safe observation.

Raw pointer vs weak_ptr:
- Raw pointer: object destroyed -> ptr still contains old address -> dangling pointer.
- weak_ptr: shared_ptr owns UDT; weak_ptr observes UDT. UDT destroyed -> weak_ptr becomes expired -> lock() returns empty shared_ptr.
- Therefore weak_ptr is safer abstraction for non-owning observation.

Practical use-cases:
- Parent-child relationships.
- Graph/data structures.
- Observer pattern.
- Caches.
- Back references.
- Break shared_ptr cyclic dependencies.
- Observe object without extending its lifetime.

Interview traps:
1. weak_ptr does not own. Reference count not increased.
2. weak_ptr does not keep object alive. Last shared_ptr destroyed, object may be destroyed.
3. weak_ptr cannot be directly dereferenced. Use lock().
4. weak_ptr primary purpose is non-owning observation, especially breaking ownership cycles.

Core rule:
unique_ptr -> single ownership.
shared_ptr -> shared ownership -> reference count increases.
weak_ptr -> non-owning observation -> reference count unchanged -> lock() gives shared_ptr -> can break shared_ptr cycles.

29. HEADER FILES, DECLARATIONS, DEFINITIONS

- Header file usually .hpp or .h.
- Example interface.hpp:
    #ifndef INTERFACE_HPP
    #define INTERFACE_HPP
    int sum(int a, int b);
    #endif
- Header commonly contains declarations.
- Source file interface.cpp:
    #include "interface.hpp"
    int sum(int a, int b) {
        return a + b;
    }
- This contains definition/implementation.

Declaration vs definition:
- Declaration: int sum(int a, int b); tells compiler function named sum exists, takes two int, returns int.
- Definition: int sum(int a, int b) { return a + b; } provides actual implementation.
- Core difference: declaration says what exists; definition says how it works.

main.cpp:
    #include <iostream>
    #include "interface.hpp"
    int main() {
        std::cout << sum(3, 7) << std::endl;
        return 0;
    }
- main.cpp does not directly know implementation of sum. It only gets declaration through header.

Compilation and linking:
    clang++ -g -std=c++17 main.cpp interface.cpp -o main
- Flow:
    main.cpp -> preprocessor -> compiler -> main.o
    interface.cpp -> preprocessor -> compiler -> interface.o
    main.o + interface.o -> linker -> executable
- Compiler converts individual source files to object code.
- Linker connects definitions from different object files.
- If main.cpp calls sum(3, 7) and interface.cpp has definition, linker connects them.

Header guards:
    #ifndef INTERFACE_HPP
    #define INTERFACE_HPP
    int sum(int a, int b);
    #endif
- Ensure same header content included multiple times in one translation unit does not cause duplicate declaration/definition problems.
- Pattern: #ifndef, #define, header content, #endif.
- Modern alternative: #pragma once.

What #include does:
- #include "interface.hpp" instructs preprocessor to include header content in source file.
- After preprocessing, declaration is conceptually available.
- #include itself is not linker.

Standard library iostream:
- Inspected system iostream header contained declarations like:
    extern ostream cout;
    extern ostream cerr;
    extern ostream clog;
- iostream internally includes other standard headers like ios, istream, ostream, streambuf.

System headers vs user headers:
    #include <iostream> usually for standard/system headers.
    #include "interface.hpp" usually for project/user headers.
- Basic convention: angle brackets for standard/system headers; quotes for project/user headers.

Practical project structure:
    project/
        main.cpp
        interface.cpp
        interface.hpp
- interface.hpp:
    #ifndef INTERFACE_HPP
    #define INTERFACE_HPP
    int sum(int a, int b);
    #endif
- interface.cpp:
    #include "interface.hpp"
    int sum(int a, int b) {
        return a + b;
    }
- main.cpp:
    #include <iostream>
    #include "interface.hpp"
    int main() {
        std::cout << sum(3, 7) << std::endl;
    }
- Compile: clang++ -g -std=c++17 main.cpp interface.cpp -o main
- Run: ./main
- Output: 10

Interview traps:
1. #include does not compile/link. It is preprocessor directive.
2. Declaration is not definition.
3. Header file commonly contains declarations. Implementation in .cpp enables code organization and separate compilation.
4. .cpp files can be compiled separately to .o files, then linker creates executable.
5. Header guard purpose: prevent problems from multiple inclusion.

Core rule:
.hpp/.h -> declarations/interfaces.
.cpp -> definitions/implementation.
#include -> preprocessor.
Compiler -> .cpp to object file.
Linker -> object files to executable.

30. CONSTRUCTORS AND DESTRUCTORS

Constructor:
- Special member function automatically called when object is created.
- Example:
    class Student {
    public:
        Student() {
            std::cout << "Constructor" << std::endl;
        }
    };
- Properties: name same as class; no return type; automatically called at object creation; used to initialize/setup object.
- Student mike; output: Constructor.

Destructor:
- Special member function automatically called when object destroyed.
- Example:
    class Student {
    public:
        ~Student() {
            std::cout << "Destructor" << std::endl;
        }
    };
- Properties: name is ~ClassName; no return type; automatically called at object destruction; used for resource cleanup.

Stack object:
    int main() {
        Student mike;
    }
- Object created inside scope. Scope ends, destructor automatically called.
- Output: Constructor, Destructor.

Heap object:
    Student* mike = new Student;
- new creates object on heap. Constructor automatically called.
- Destructor does not automatically happen immediately after new.
    Student* mike = new Student;
    delete mike;
- delete calls destructor and releases allocated memory.
- Output: Constructor, Destructor.
- Important: new -> constructor. delete -> destructor plus memory release.

new without delete:
    Student* mike = new Student;
- If delete mike; not done, heap memory not properly released. Can cause memory leak.

Constructor and destructor outside class:
- Can define inside class:
    class Student {
    public:
        Student() { std::cout << "Constructor"; }
        ~Student() { std::cout << "Destructor"; }
    };
- Or declaration in class and definition in .cpp:
    student.hpp:
        class Student {
        public:
            Student();
            ~Student();
            std::string m_name;
        };
    student.cpp:
        #include "student.hpp"
        #include <iostream>
        Student::Student() {
            std::cout << "Constructor" << std::endl;
        }
        Student::~Student() {
            std::cout << "Destructor: " << m_name << std::endl;
        }
- Student::Student() means Student class constructor.
- Student::~Student() means Student class destructor.
- :: is scope resolution operator.

Why Student() outside class is wrong:
- Student() { } outside class does not define constructor for Student class.
- Compiler does not know it is Student class constructor.
- Correct: Student::Student() { }
- Destructor: Student::~Student() { }

Multiple objects and destruction order:
    int main() {
        Student mike;
        Student sue;
        return 0;
    }
- Output:
    Constructor
    Constructor
    Destructor: sue
    Destructor: mike
- Rule: Construction forward order. Destruction reverse order.
- If mike created then sue created, destruction: sue destroyed then mike destroyed.

Object lifetime:
- Object creation -> Constructor -> Object exists -> Scope ends/delete -> Destructor -> Object destroyed.
- Stack object: Constructor -> scope -> Destructor.
- Heap object: Constructor -> object exists -> delete mike -> Destructor.

Constructor/destructor and resource management:
- Destructor major practical use is cleanup.
    class Database {
    public:
        Database() { // resource acquire
        }
        ~Database() { // resource release
        }
    };
- Resources: dynamic memory, file handles, database connections, locks, network resources, OS resources.
- Related to RAII.

Compilation and linking:
- Project: student.hpp, student.cpp, studentDemo.cpp.
- Compile: clang++ -std=c++17 studentDemo.cpp student.cpp -o studentDemo.
- Header has declarations: Student(); ~Student();
- .cpp has definitions: Student::Student() { } and Student::~Student() { }

Constructor vs destructor:
- Constructor: syntax Student(); called when object created; no return type; can have parameters; purpose initialization; automatically called; can be overloaded.
- Destructor: syntax ~Student(); called when object destroyed; no return type; cannot have parameters; purpose cleanup; automatically called; cannot be overloaded.

Compiler experiment:
- Heap object: Student* mike = new Student; output Constructor. No destructor until delete mike;
- Stack object: Student mike; scope end: Constructor, Destructor.
- Multiple stack objects: Student mike; Student sue; output Constructor, Constructor, Destructor: sue, Destructor: mike. Proves reverse destruction order.

Interview traps:
1. new creates object and calls constructor.
2. delete calls destructor and releases heap memory.
3. Forgetting delete for heap object -> memory leak.
4. Defining constructor outside class requires Student::Student().
5. Destructor outside class -> Student::~Student().
6. :: is scope resolution operator.
7. Stack objects automatically destroyed at scope end.
8. Object destruction is reverse order of construction.
9. Constructor for initialization, destructor for cleanup.
10. Manually calling destructor is not replacement for normal object lifetime management.

Core rule:
Constructor -> object life starts.
Destructor -> object life ends.
new -> constructor. delete -> destructor plus memory release.
Stack object: scope enter -> constructor; scope exit -> destructor.

31. OBJECT CREATION MAPPING

- Object creation -> Default constructor.
- New object from object -> Copy constructor.
- Existing object = object -> Copy assignment.
- Object destruction -> Destructor.

32. RULE OF THREE

Definition:
- If a resource-managing class requires custom destructor, copy constructor, or copy assignment operator, it generally needs to explicitly define all three.
- The three:
    ~Student(); // Destructor
    Student(const Student& other); // Copy Constructor
    Student& operator=(const Student& other); // Copy Assignment
- Normal/custom constructor is NOT part of Rule of Three.

Why needed:
- Suppose class manually manages dynamic memory:
    class Student {
    public:
        int* marks;
        Student() { marks = new int(100); }
        ~Student() { delete marks; }
    };
- Student a; Student b = a;
- Compiler-generated copy constructor performs shallow copy:
    a.marks and b.marks point to same memory.
- When both destructors execute delete marks; same memory deleted twice -> double delete / undefined behavior.

Solution: deep copy.
- Copy constructor:
    Student(const Student& other) {
        marks = new int(*other.marks);
    }
- Now a.marks and b.marks own separate memory.
- Copy assignment operator:
    Student& operator=(const Student& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            delete marks;
            marks = new int(*other.marks);
        }
        return *this;
    }
- this != &other protects against self-assignment.
- Destructor:
    ~Student() {
        delete marks;
    }
- Releases dynamically allocated resource.

Complete example:
    class Student {
    public:
        int* marks;
        Student(int value) {
            marks = new int(value);
        }
        Student(const Student& other) {
            marks = new int(*other.marks);
        }
        Student& operator=(const Student& other) {
            if (this != &other) {
                delete marks;
                marks = new int(*other.marks);
            }
            return *this;
        }
        ~Student() {
            delete marks;
        }
    };

Copy constructor vs copy assignment:
- Student b = a; -> Copy Constructor.
- Student b(a); -> Copy Constructor.
- b = a; -> Copy Assignment Operator.
- Easy trick: new object being created -> Copy Constructor. Existing object receiving data -> Copy Assignment.

Interview trap:
- Wrong: If I write any custom constructor, Rule of Three applies.
- Correct: Rule of Three concerns destructor, copy constructor, copy assignment operator. It does not include normal/custom constructor.

Relationship with Rule of Five:
- Rule of Three plus Move Constructor and Move Assignment Operator gives Rule of Five.

One-line interview answer:
- Rule of Three says that if a resource-managing class needs a user-defined destructor, copy constructor, or copy assignment operator, it generally needs all three.

33. RULE OF TWO FRAGMENT

- Your pasted note has incomplete text:
    Rule of two states that if we are changing copy constructor Rule of two states that if we are changing copy constructori
- This fragment is incomplete.
- Standard C++ rules covered are Rule of Zero, Rule of Three, and Rule of Five.

34. PASSING OBJECTS BY VALUE VS REFERENCE

Pass by value:
    void processByValue(Array tempArr) {
        tempArr.setData(0, 99999);
    }
    processByValue(myArr);
- myArr copy is made.
- Flow: myArr -> Copy Constructor -> tempArr.
- tempArr is completely separate object.
- tempArr.setData modifies only copy.
- Original myArr remains 100 200 2 ...
- Copy tempArr becomes 99999 200 2 ...

Pass by reference:
    void processByReference(const Array& sharedArr) {
        sharedArr.printData();
    }
    processByReference(myArr);
- No copy.
- myArr and sharedArr refer to same object.
- sharedArr is reference/alias to myArr.
- Therefore: copy constructor not called; no extra Array object; large data not copied; efficient.

Why const Array&:
    void processByReference(const Array& sharedArr)
- const means function cannot modify object.
- sharedArr.printData(); allowed.
- sharedArr.setData(0, 99999); not allowed.
- If function needs modify:
    void process(Array& arr) {
        arr.setData(0, 99999);
    }
- If only read:
    void process(const Array& arr) {
        arr.printData();
    }
- Best practice: large object read-only pass -> const T&.

100,000 element example:
- Array constructor fills 100,000 integers.
- Pass by value: copy constructor copies 100,000 elements.
- Function end: tempArr destructor destroys copy. Original myArr unaffected.
- Final output: Original myArr status: 100 200 2 ...

Pass by value vs pass by reference:
- Pass by value: syntax Array arr; copy yes; copy constructor called; extra object yes; large object expensive; original modify no; read-only possible but still copy; memory overhead higher.
- Pass by reference: syntax Array& arr; copy no; copy constructor not called; extra object no; large object efficient; original modify yes if non-const; read-only const Array&; memory overhead very low.

Compiler/runtime observation:
- Demo 1 output: Passing Array By Value, Copy Constructor Called. Proves copy constructor invoked.
- Demo 2 output: Passing Array By Reference, no copy constructor log. Proves no copy.

const_cast not needed:
- Original code had const_cast<Array&>(sharedArr).printData(); This is unnecessary.
- Better: make printData() const:
    void printData() const;
- Implementation:
    void Array::printData() const {
        for (size_t i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
            std::cout << data[i] << " ";
        }
        std::cout << "... [Total size: " << data.size() << "]" << std::endl;
    }
- Then processByReference(const Array& sharedArr) can call sharedArr.printData(); directly.
- Why: printData() does not modify object, so making it const member function is logically correct.

Practical use cases:
- Small primitive types: void print(int x); value pass fine.
- Large objects: void process(const std::vector<int>& data); reference efficient.
- Object modify: void update(Array& arr);
- Object intentionally copy: void process(Array arr); useful when function needs independent copy.

Interview trap:
- Wrong: Reference pass copies object.
- Correct: Passing by reference does not create a copy of referenced object.

Another trap:
- const Array& means no copy plus read-only access.
- Common and efficient pattern for large objects.

One-line revision:
- Pass by value -> Copy Constructor -> New Object -> Expensive for large objects.
- Pass by reference -> No Copy -> Same Object -> Efficient.
- const T& -> No Copy plus Read Only.

Core concept:
- Array tempArr means give me a new Array object.
- const Array& arr means give me reference to existing Array; I will not modify it.

35. MEMBER INITIALIZER LIST AND INITIALIZATION ORDER

Member initializer list:
- In constructor, initializing members after colon is member initializer list.
- Example:
    class Vector {
        int x;
        int y;
        int z;
    public:
        Vector() : x(3), y(2), z(1) {}
    };
- Initialization: x = 3, y = 2, z = 1.

Initializer list vs constructor body:
- Constructor body:
    Vector() {
        x = 3;
        y = 2;
        z = 1;
    }
- Members first initialize, then body assignment happens.
- Member initializer list:
    Vector() : x(3), y(2), z(1) {}
- Members directly initialize.
- Golden difference: initializer list is initialization; constructor body is assignment.

Initialization order:
- C++ does not initialize members in the order written in initializer list.
- Initialization always happens in declaration order in class.
- Example:
    class Vector {
        int x;
        int y;
        int z;
    public:
        Vector() : z(1), y(2), x(3) {}
    };
- Initializer list order: z, y, x.
- Actual initialization: x = 3, y = 2, z = 1.
- Because declaration order is x, y, z.
- Golden rule: declaration order equals initialization order. Initializer-list order does NOT determine initialization order.

Dangerous example:
    class Vector {
        int x;
        int y;
        int z;
    public:
        Vector() : x(z), y(z), z(3) {}
    };
- Actual execution:
    1. x = z; z not initialized yet.
    2. y = z; z not initialized yet.
    3. z = 3; OK.
- Using z before initialization for int can be undefined behavior.

Correct dependency order:
- If want z = 3, x = z, y = z, put declaration order according to dependency:
    class Vector {
        int z;
        int x;
        int y;
    public:
        Vector() : z(3), x(z), y(z) {}
    };
- Actual order: z = 3, x = z = 3, y = z = 3.
- So z = 3, x = 3, y = 3.

Why initializer list important:
- const member:
    class Student {
        const int id;
    public:
        Student(int x) : id(x) {}
    };
- const member cannot be assigned in constructor body:
    Student(int x) {
        id = x; // error
    }
- Reference member:
    class Student {
        int& ref;
    public:
        Student(int& x) : ref(x) {}
    };
- Reference must be initialized in initializer list.
- Member with no default constructor:
    class Engine {
    public:
        Engine(int power) {}
    };
    class Car {
        Engine engine;
    public:
        Car() : engine(500) {}
    };
- Engine has no default constructor, so engine must be initialized in initializer list.

Practical compiler trap:
    class Vector {
        int x;
        int y;
    public:
        Vector() : y(20), x(10) {}
    };
- Compiler actually initializes x = 10, y = 20.
- Compiler warning may appear: -Wreorder-ctor.
- Best practice: write initializer list in same order as declaration:
    Vector() : x(10), y(20) {}

Important concepts:
- Class declaration order: int x; int y; int z;
- Initialization order: x, y, z.
- Initializer list order: Vector() : z(1), x(3), y(2) {} does not change initialization order.

Interview traps:
1. Question: In initializer list, does member written first initialize first?
   Answer: No.
   Correct: Class declaration order determines initialization order.
2. Vector() : y(x), x(10) {} with declaration int x; int y;
   Actual: x = 10, y = x = 10. Valid because x initialized first in declaration order.
3. Vector() : x(y), y(10) {}
   Actual: x = y before y initialized; y = 10. Dangerous/undefined behavior for int.

One-line interview answer:
- C++ initializes data members in the order of their declaration in the class, regardless of the order in the member initializer list.

36. RULE OF FIVE

Definition:
- Rule of Five states that when a class manages a resource/ownership manually, such as dynamic memory using new/delete, we may need to define five special member functions:
    1. Destructor
    2. Copy Constructor
    3. Copy Assignment Operator
    4. Move Constructor
    5. Move Assignment Operator
- Normal constructor is not part of Rule of Five.

The five functions:
    class Student {
        int* marks;
    public:
        Student(); // Normal Constructor
        ~Student(); // 1. Destructor
        Student(const Student& other); // 2. Copy Constructor
        Student& operator=(const Student& other); // 3. Copy Assignment
        Student(Student&& other); // 4. Move Constructor
        Student& operator=(Student&& other); // 5. Move Assignment
    };

Rule of Three plus move operations:
- Rule of Three: destructor, copy constructor, copy assignment.
- Move operations: move constructor, move assignment.
- Rule of Three plus move operations equals Rule of Five.

Copy constructor vs move constructor:
- Copy: Student a; Student b = a; new object b created from a. Proper deep copy creates separate resource.
- Move: Student a; Student b = std::move(a); resource ownership can be transferred. Move generally avoids expensive deep copy.
- Before move: a owns Resource A.
- After move: a is nullptr, b owns Resource A.

Copy assignment vs move assignment:
- Copy assignment: both objects already exist: Student a; Student b; b = a; calls Student& operator=(const Student& other);
- Move assignment: both objects already exist: Student a; Student b; b = std::move(a); calls Student& operator=(Student&& other);

Important difference:
- New object is created: Copy -> Copy Constructor; Move -> Move Constructor.
- Object already exists: Copy -> Copy Assignment; Move -> Move Assignment.
- Examples:
    Student b = a; // Copy Constructor
    Student b = std::move(a); // Move Constructor
    b = a; // Copy Assignment
    b = std::move(a); // Move Assignment

Why move operations matter:
- If object owns huge resource like 10 MB data:
    Copy: 10 MB duplicated.
    Move: ownership transferred.
- Move avoids expensive resource duplication.
- Typical resources: dynamic memory, file handles, sockets, database connections, large buffers, OS resources.

Move constructor example:
    class Student {
        int* marks;
    public:
        Student(int value) { marks = new int(value); }
        ~Student() { delete marks; }
        Student(Student&& other) {
            marks = other.marks;
            other.marks = nullptr;
        }
    };
- Move: Student a(100); Student b(std::move(a));
- Before: a owns [100], b empty.
- After: a is nullptr, b owns [100].

Move assignment example:
    Student& operator=(Student&& other) {
        if (this != &other) {
            delete marks;
            marks = other.marks;
            other.marks = nullptr;
        }
        return *this;
    }
- Important steps:
    1. Check self-assignment.
    2. Release current resource.
    3. Take other's resource.
    4. Set other's pointer to nullptr.
    5. Return *this.

Moved-from object:
- After Student b = std::move(a); a is still valid object, but state is generally unspecified.
- Do not assume a is always empty.
- For your own move constructor, leave moved-from object in safe/destructible state, often other.marks = nullptr.

Rule of Five and std::move:
- std::move(a); does not itself move resource.
- It converts/casts expression so move operations can be selected.
- std::move(a) -> rvalue/xvalue expression -> Move Constructor or Move Assignment -> resource transfer.

Rule of Five vs Rule of Three:
- Rule of Three: destructor, copy constructor, copy assignment.
- Rule of Five adds move constructor, move assignment.
- If class manually manages resource, Rule of Five is more complete when move semantics relevant.

Modern C++ Rule of Zero:
- Modern C++ often tries to avoid manually implementing all five.
- Use RAII classes such as std::vector, std::string, std::unique_ptr, std::shared_ptr.
- Example:
    class Student {
        std::vector<int> marks;
    };
- You do not manually manage vector's memory.
- Standard library types handle construction, destruction, copying, moving, resource management.
- Rule of Zero: Prefer types that manage resources themselves, so your class doesn't need custom special member functions.
- Progression: Rule of Zero -> Rule of Three -> Rule of Five.

Interview traps:
1. Rule of Five does not include normal constructor. It includes destructor, copy constructor, copy assignment, move constructor, move assignment.
2. Student b = std::move(a); is not Move Assignment. It is Move Constructor because b is a new object.
3. b = std::move(a); is Move Assignment because b already exists.
4. Wrong: std::move() moves the object.
   More accurate: std::move() makes an expression eligible for move semantics; actual transfer is performed by selected move constructor or move assignment operator.

One-line interview answer:
- Rule of Five says that a resource-managing C++ class may need a destructor, copy constructor, copy assignment operator, move constructor, and move assignment operator to correctly manage ownership during destruction, copying, and moving.

37. RULE OF FIVE WITH VECTOR, COPY, MOVE, noexcept

Experiment setup:
- Class IntArray manually manages dynamic memory:
    class IntArray {
    public:
        IntArray(std::string name);
        ~IntArray();
        IntArray(const IntArray& rhs);
        IntArray& operator=(const IntArray& rhs);
        IntArray(IntArray&& source);
        IntArray& operator=(IntArray&& source);
    private:
        std::string m_name;
        int* m_data;
    };
- It follows Rule of Five.

vector.push_back(temp) -> Copy:
    std::vector<IntArray> arr1;
    for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        IntArray temp(std::to_string(i));
        arr1.push_back(temp);
    }
- temp is lvalue.
- temp -> lvalue -> push_back(temp) -> Copy Constructor.
- Output:
    0 was constructed
    was copy constructed from 0
    0 was destructed
- The temporary/local temp and object inside vector are two different objects.

std::move(temp) -> Move:
    arr1.push_back(std::move(temp));
- temp -> std::move(temp) -> xvalue -> Move Constructor.
- Resource can be transferred instead of deep-copied.
- Before: temp owns dynamic array.
- After move: temp is nullptr, vector owns dynamic array.
- Important: std::move() itself does not move anything. It allows move constructor/assignment to be selected.

Why first experiment had many copies:
- Without arr1.reserve(10), vector has limited capacity.
- When capacity full, vector must allocate larger memory block.
- Typical growth: 1 -> 2 -> 4 -> 8 -> 16.
- During reallocation: old vector -> allocate larger storage -> transfer existing objects -> destroy old objects.
- Because IntArray move constructor is not noexcept, std::vector may prefer copy constructor during reallocation to preserve exception-safety.
- Output may show:
    was copy constructed from 0(copy)
    was copy constructed from 1(copy)
    was copy constructed from 2(copy)

reserve(10) changes experiment:
    std::vector<IntArray> arr1;
    arr1.reserve(10);
- Vector reserves enough capacity for 10 elements before insertion.
- No vector reallocation required.
- Existing vector elements do not need copy/move because of vector growth.
- Output becomes:
    0 constructed
    0 copied
    0 destructed
    1 constructed
    1 copied
    1 destructed
    ...
- Copy here comes from arr1.push_back(temp); not from vector reallocation.

reserve() vs std::move():
- reserve() prevents unnecessary vector reallocations: no capacity growth, no relocation of existing elements.
- std::move() allows object to be moved instead of copied: lvalue -> rvalue/xvalue -> Move Constructor.
- They solve different problems.

noexcept and std::vector:
- Make move constructor:
    IntArray::IntArray(IntArray&& source) noexcept {
        m_name = source.m_name;
        source.m_name = "";
        m_data = source.m_data;
        source.m_data = nullptr;
    }
- Declaration:
    IntArray(IntArray&& source) noexcept;
- Move constructor promises: this move operation will not throw an exception.
- During vector reallocation, this makes moving existing elements preferable to copying.
- Without noexcept: vector reallocation -> move may throw -> copy may be preferred.
- With noexcept: vector reallocation -> safe move -> move existing objects.

Copy assignment bug in experiment:
    IntArray& IntArray::operator=(const IntArray& rhs) {
        if (this != &rhs) {
            delete[] m_data;
            m_name = rhs.m_name + "(copy)";
            for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
                m_data[i] = rhs.m_data[i];
            }
        }
        return *this;
    }
- Problem: delete[] m_data; after deletion, m_data is dangling. Then m_data[i] = rhs.m_data[i]; uses dangling pointer.
- Correct:
    IntArray& IntArray::operator=(const IntArray& rhs) {
        if (this != &rhs) {
            delete[] m_data;
            m_data = new int[10];
            m_name = rhs.m_name + "(copy)";
            for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
                m_data[i] = rhs.m_data[i];
            }
        }
        return *this;
    }

Move assignment bug:
    IntArray& IntArray::operator=(IntArray&& source) {
        if (this != &source) {
            m_name = source.m_name;
            source.m_name = "";
            m_data = source.m_data;
            source.m_data = nullptr;
        }
        return *this;
    }
- Potential problem: this->m_data old resource is overwritten by m_data = source.m_data; without first releasing old resource. Causes memory leak.
- Better:
    IntArray& IntArray::operator=(IntArray&& source) noexcept {
        if (this != &source) {
            delete[] m_data;
            m_name = std::move(source.m_name);
            m_data = source.m_data;
            source.m_data = nullptr;
        }
        return *this;
    }

Copy vs move:
- Copy: source owns Resource A; destination owns Resource B. Resource duplicated.
- Move: source is nullptr; destination owns Resource A. Ownership/resource transferred.

push_back decision:
- arr1.push_back(temp); temp is lvalue -> Copy Constructor.
- arr1.push_back(std::move(temp)); std::move(temp) is xvalue -> Move Constructor.
- For rvalue temporary: arr1.push_back(IntArray("hello")); move constructor may also be selected when applicable.

Vector reallocation decision:
- When std::vector needs to grow:
    Does element have suitable noexcept move?
    Yes -> Move.
    No -> Copy may be preferred.
- Therefore IntArray(IntArray&&) noexcept; is important for movable resource-owning types stored in containers.

Complete mental model:
- std::vector<IntArray> push_back(temp): temp is lvalue -> Copy Constructor -> Vector object.
- push_back(std::move(temp)): temp becomes xvalue -> Move Constructor -> Vector object.
- When vector grows: capacity full -> reallocation -> existing elements relocated -> if noexcept move available, move; otherwise copy may occur.

Practical takeaways:
- push_back(temp): copy lvalue.
- push_back(std::move(temp)): move from object.
- reserve(n): prevent reallocations up to capacity.
- Move constructor: transfer resource during construction.
- Move assignment: transfer resource to existing object.
- noexcept move: makes move preferable/safe during vector reallocation.
- Copy constructor: creates independent copy.
- Copy assignment: replaces resource of existing object.
- Destructor: releases owned resource.

Interview traps:
1. reserve() does not create elements. arr1.reserve(10) means capacity >= 10, size = 0. It does not mean size = 10.
2. reserve() does not make push_back(temp) a move. arr1.reserve(10); arr1.push_back(temp); still copies because temp is lvalue.
3. std::move() does not physically move memory. It enables move semantics. Move constructor/assignment performs actual transfer.
4. noexcept is important for containers. Throwing move constructor can cause std::vector to prefer copying during reallocation when copying is available.
5. Move assignment must release old resource before m_data = source.m_data; generally needs to release destination's old resource.

One-line interview answer:
- reserve() avoids vector reallocations, std::move() enables moving instead of copying, and a noexcept move constructor allows std::vector to efficiently move existing elements during reallocation.

38. FINAL OBSERVED ./rule52 OUTPUT MEANING

Output:
0 was constructed
0 was moved constructor
 was destructed
1 was constructed
1 was moved constructor
 was destructed
...
9 was constructed
9 was moved constructor
 was destructed
9 was destructed
8 was destructed
7 was destructed
6 was destructed
5 was destructed
4 was destructed
3 was destructed
2 was destructed
1 was destructed
0 was destructed

Meaning:
- Each temporary 0 through 9 was constructed.
- Each temporary was moved into the vector.
- Each temporary was destructed after move.
- At the end, vector elements were destructed in reverse order: 9 down to 0.

39. CORE ONE-LINERS

- 321.5 is double. 321.5f is float.
- 'abc' is multi-character constant, implementation-defined.
- sizeof(ptr) gives pointer size. sizeof("abcd") gives 5.
- const means no modification after initialization.
- Scope means where accessible. Lifetime means how long exists.
- Array parameter decays to pointer.
- T& binds lvalue only. const T& binds lvalue and rvalue. T&& binds rvalue only.
- std::move() is cast to rvalue; it does not itself move.
- new pairs with delete. new[] pairs with delete[].
- unique_ptr is single ownership, non-copyable.
- shared_ptr is shared ownership, reference count.
- weak_ptr is non-owning observation, breaks cycles.
- Declaration says what exists. Definition says how it works.
- Constructor starts object life. Destructor ends object life.
- Construction is forward order. Destruction is reverse order.
- Rule of Three is destructor plus copy constructor plus copy assignment.
- Rule of Five is Rule of Three plus move constructor plus move assignment.
- Rule of Zero is use RAII types, write none.
- Member initialization order is declaration order, not initializer-list order.
- Pass by value copies. Pass by reference does not copy. const T& is no copy plus read-only.
- reserve() avoids vector reallocation. noexcept move makes vector prefer move during reallocation.

40. INHERITANCE AND VIRTUAL FUNCTIONS

Inheritance basics:
- Base class (parent), derived class (child).
- public inheritance means is-a relationship.
- Access: public everywhere, protected in derived, private not directly accessible.

Virtual functions:
- Declared with virtual in base class.
- Enables runtime polymorphism: correct derived function called through base pointer or reference.

Without virtual:
- Function call resolved at compile time based on static type.
- Base version is called.

With virtual:
- Function call resolved at runtime based on dynamic type.
- Derived version is called.

override keyword:
- C++11, placed after parameter list.
- Ensures derived function actually overrides a base virtual function.
- Catches mistakes like wrong parameters, const, or return type.
- Optional but recommended.

virtual keyword:
- Written in base class.
- Stays virtual in all derived classes even if not repeated.
- In derived, can write virtual or override or both.

Pure virtual function:
- Declared with = 0.
- Class with at least one pure virtual is abstract.
- Cannot create objects of abstract class.
- Must be overridden in derived class.
- If not overridden, derived class remains abstract and cannot be instantiated.

vtable and vptr:
- vtable means virtual table, implementation mechanism for dynamic dispatch.
- Class with virtual functions gets a vtable with pointers to its virtual functions.
- Each object has hidden vptr pointing to its class vtable.
- Call through base pointer uses vptr to find correct function.
- vtable and vptr are implementation details, not in C++ standard.

Virtual destructor:
- If class has virtual functions, it should have a virtual destructor.
- Needed when deleting derived object through base pointer.
- Without virtual destructor, only base destructor may be called, causing undefined behavior and resource leaks.
- Example: class Base { public: virtual ~Base() {} };

Object slicing:
- Happens when derived object assigned or passed by value to base object.
- Derived part is sliced off.
- Avoid by using pointers or references.
- Passing by value disables polymorphism.

Interview traps:
- Without virtual, base version called. With virtual, derived version called.
- override optional but recommended.
- virtual in base, implied in derived.
- Pure virtual must be overridden for concrete class.
- vtable and vptr are implementation details.
- Virtual destructor needed if virtual functions present.
- Object slicing occurs with pass-by-value to base. Use reference or pointer.
- Virtual calls in constructor or destructor do not behave polymorphically. Base version called.
- Default arguments resolved at compile time based on static type.
- Access specifiers do not affect virtual dispatch. Private virtual can be overridden.

Core rule:
- Inheritance: derived is-a base.
- virtual: runtime polymorphism.
- override: ensures correct overriding.
- vtable: compiler mechanism for virtual dispatch.
- vptr: hidden pointer to vtable.
- pure virtual: abstract class, must be overridden.
- virtual destructor: safe deletion through base pointer.
- object slicing: avoid with references or pointers.

41. TEMPLATES

What is a template:
- A template is a blueprint for generating code.
- It allows writing code once and using it with different types.
- Compiler generates specific code for each type used.
- This is called generic programming.

Two main kinds:
- Function templates.
- Class templates.

41. MULTIPLE INHERITANCE, DIAMOND PROBLEM, NONCOPYABLE

Multiple inheritance basics:
- A class can inherit from more than one base class.
- Syntax: struct Coltriever : public Golden, public BorderCollie.
- Coltriever gets members from both Golden and BorderCollie.
- If both share a common base Dog, Coltriever gets two Dog subobjects.

Diamond problem:
- Dog is base.
- Golden inherits Dog.
- BorderCollie inherits Dog.
- Coltriever inherits Golden and BorderCollie.
- Coltriever contains two Dog subobjects: one through Golden, one through BorderCollie.
- Converting Coltriever pointer to Dog pointer is ambiguous.
- Error: ambiguous conversion from derived class Coltriever to base class Dog.
- Compiler shows both paths: Coltriever -> Golden -> Dog and Coltriever -> BorderCollie -> Dog.

Fix for diamond problem:
- Use virtual inheritance.
- Change: struct Golden : virtual public Dog.
- Change: struct BorderCollie : virtual public Dog.
- Now Coltriever has only one shared Dog subobject.
- Conversion to Dog pointer becomes unambiguous.

Virtual functions and multiple inheritance:
- Without virtual: call resolved at compile time based on object type.
- With virtual: call resolved at runtime based on dynamic type.
- Output is same when calling through concrete objects.
- Difference appears when calling through base pointer or reference.

NonCopyable idiom:
- NonCopyable deletes copy constructor and copy assignment.
- Any class inheriting NonCopyable becomes non-copyable.
- This is a common C++ idiom to disable copying.

NonCopyable default constructor:
- If you declare any constructor, the implicit default constructor is removed.
- Deleted copy constructor is still a user-declared constructor.
- So NonCopyable has no default constructor.
- Dog inherits NonCopyable, so Dog cannot be default-constructed.
- All derived classes also cannot be default-constructed.
- Error: call to implicitly-deleted default constructor.
- Fix: add NonCopyable() = default;

Deleted copy constructor propagates:
- NonCopyable deletes copy constructor.
- Dog inherits NonCopyable, so Dog copy constructor is implicitly deleted.
- Error: call to implicitly-deleted copy constructor of Dog.
- Same for Golden, BorderCollie, Coltriever.
- You cannot copy Dog objects.

Deleted copy assignment propagates:
- NonCopyable deletes copy assignment.
- Dog copy assignment is implicitly deleted.
- Error: object of type Dog cannot be assigned because its copy assignment operator is implicitly deleted.
- You cannot assign Dog objects.

What you can do instead:
- Use pointers: Dog* dog3 = dog2;
- Use references: Dog& dog3 = dog2;
- Do not copy or assign Dog objects if NonCopyable is in the hierarchy.

Direct derived pointer works:
- Coltriever* coltriever = new Coltriever; compiles fine.
- Diamond ambiguity only appears when converting to common base Dog*.
- If you stay with Coltriever*, no ambiguity.

Interview traps:
- Multiple inheritance can create multiple base subobjects.
- Diamond problem causes ambiguous conversion to common base.
- Virtual inheritance fixes diamond problem.
- Declaring any constructor removes implicit default constructor.
- Deleted copy constructor in base makes derived non-copy-constructible.
- Deleted copy assignment in base makes derived non-copy-assignable.
- NonCopyable makes all derived classes non-copyable.
- Virtual and override do not change output when calling through concrete objects.
- To see polymorphism, use base pointer or reference.

Core rule:
- Multiple inheritance: derived gets members from all bases.
- Diamond problem: two base subobjects, ambiguous conversion.
- Virtual inheritance: one shared base subobject.
- NonCopyable: deletes copy constructor and copy assignment.
- Declaring any constructor removes implicit default constructor.
- Deleted copy operations propagate to all derived classes.
- Use pointers or references instead of copying when NonCopyable is in hierarchy.

One-line summary:
Multiple inheritance can cause the diamond problem when two base classes share a common base. Virtual inheritance fixes it. NonCopyable deletes copy constructor and copy assignment, making all derived classes non-copyable. Declaring any constructor removes the implicit default constructor, so add = default if needed.

42. CPPMAN INSTALLATION AND USAGE

What is cppman:
- cppman is a command-line tool to view C++ man pages.
- It fetches documentation from cppreference.com or cplusplus.com.
- Works like Unix man command but for C++ standard library.
- Supports offline caching.

Installation:
- Command: brew install cppman
- Homebrew warned about untrusted tap: mongodb/brew
- This warning is unrelated to cppman.
- cppman version installed: 0.6.3
- 18 dependencies installed, including python@3.14, ghostscript, tesseract, pango.

Homebrew trust warning:
- Homebrew is ignoring formulae from untrusted taps.
- To trust a specific formula: brew trust --formula mongodb/brew/mongodb-community@7.0
- To trust a whole tap: brew trust mongodb/brew
- To disable trust checks: export HOMEBREW_NO_REQUIRE_TAP_TRUST=1 (not recommended)

Python shadowing warning:
- python@3.14 installed by Homebrew.
- Several executables shadowed by Anaconda: idle3, pip3, pydoc3, python3, python3-config.
- Running these names uses Anaconda version, not Homebrew version.
- This is only a warning, not an error.

Basic usage:
- cppman std::vector opens the man page for std::vector.
- cppman automatically tries std:: prefix first.

Help:
- cppman -h or cppman --help shows all options.

Important options:
- -s SOURCE: select cppreference.com or cplusplus.com. Default is cppreference.com.
- -c: cache all pages for offline browsing.
- -C: clear all cached files.
- -f KEYWORD: find page and show selection menu if multiple matches.
- -o: force update existing cache.
- -m MANDB: true or false. If true, adds to mandb for man command.
- -p PAGER: vim, nvim, less, system, mansystem. Default is vim.
- -r: rebuild index database.
- -v: show version.
- -n MAX_RESULTS: maximum search results.
- -h: help.

Observations:
- cppman -s cppreference.com: Source set to cppreference.com.
- cppman vector: opens std::vector man page.
- cppman -k vector: error: no such option: -k. cppman does not support -k like man.
- cppman -f vector: shows selection menu. Page 1/3 lists 20 results. Page 2/3 lists 21 to 40. Page 3/3 lists 41 to 51. Navigation: number to select, n for next, p for previous, Enter to cancel.
- Pressing q: Please enter a valid number or navigation command.
- Pressing Ctrl+C: Operation cancelled.
- cppman std::vector::at: caused zsh error: no such file or directory: T,Allocator. Shell interpreted angle brackets and comma as redirection.
- Correct usage: quote the page name: cppman "std::vector::at" or use cppman -f std::vector::at.
- cppman array: showed escape characters ^[OB%. May be terminal rendering issue.

Key observations:
- cppman is useful for quick C++ documentation lookup.
- Supports offline caching.
- Has selection menu for ambiguous names.
- Does not support -k option.
- Shell quoting needed for names with :: or <>.

Interview traps:
- cppman is not part of standard C++ toolchain. It is third-party.
- Homebrew may warn about untrusted taps unrelated to cppman.
- Python shadowing by Anaconda can cause confusion.
- Shell special characters in page names need quoting.

Core rule:
- cppman std::vector: opens std::vector man page.
- cppman -f keyword: shows selection menu.
- cppman -s source: selects documentation source.
- cppman -c: caches all pages for offline use.
- Quote page names containing ::, <, >, or , to avoid shell errors.

One-line summary:
cppman is a command-line C++ man page viewer. Install with brew install cppman. Use cppman std::vector to view documentation. Use -f for selection menu. Quote names with special characters. It caches pages for offline use and supports cppreference.com or cplusplus.com as sources.

43. CONST VARIABLES, CONST PARAMETERS, AND SIMILARITY TO PRIVATE

Const variable assignment error:
- Code:
    int main() {
        const float num = 2.3f;
        num = 7.8f;
        return 0;
    }
- Error:
    cannot assign to variable 'num' with const-qualified type 'const float'
    note: variable 'num' declared const here

Why:
- const variable cannot be modified after initialization.
- Assignment to a const variable is a compile-time error.

Const parameter assignment error:
- Code:
    void setValue(const float n) {
        n = 4.6f;
    }
- Error:
    cannot assign to variable 'n' with const-qualified type 'const float'
    note: variable 'n' declared const here

Why:
- Function parameter n is const.
- Inside the function, n cannot be modified.
- The const applies to the parameter as seen inside the function.

Similarity to private in a class:
- private members cannot be accessed from outside the class.
- const variables and const parameters cannot be modified after initialization or inside a const context.
- Both restrict what you can do from a certain place.
- But they are not the same:
    private is about access control. You can still modify a private member inside class methods.
    const is about modification. You cannot modify a const object anywhere after initialization.
- So the similarity is only in the sense of restriction. The nature of restriction is different.

Important distinction:
- A const parameter passed by value is a copy. The const prevents modifying that copy.
- A const reference parameter prevents modifying the original object.
- A const member function prevents modifying the object's data members.

Interview points:
- const variable: cannot be assigned after initialization.
- const parameter: cannot be assigned inside the function.
- const member function: cannot modify non-mutable data members.
- private: access control, not modification control.
- Do not confuse access with modification.

Core rule:
- const means read-only after initialization.
