- Attempting to modify a const object gives a compile-time error.
- private means not accessible from outside the class, but still modifiable inside.
- Both restrict behavior, but for different reasons.

One-line summary:
const prevents modification after initialization. private prevents access from outside the class. They both restrict what you can do, but const is about modification and private is about access.

44. INITIALIZATION FORMS, NARROWING, INITIALIZER_LIST

Three initialization forms:
- Copy initialization: int x = 4;
- Direct initialization: int y(3);
- Brace initialization: int z{5};

Narrowing conversion behavior:
- int x = 4.6; gives warning only. Value changes from 4.6 to 4.
- int y(3.3); gives warning only. Value changes from 3.3 to 3.
- int z{5.2}; gives error: type double cannot be narrowed to int in initializer list.
- Brace initialization {} rejects narrowing conversions.
- Copy initialization = and direct initialization () only give warnings.

std::vector initialization:
- std::vector<int> v{1, 2, 3, 4}; works. Prints 1 2 3 4.
- std::vector<int> v(1, 2, 3, 4); fails. No matching constructor for 4 arguments.
- Braces {} match initializer_list constructor.
- Parentheses () look for a constructor with exactly matching number and types of arguments.
- Vector has no 4-argument constructor. So parentheses fail.

What is std::initializer_list:
- Header: #include <initializer_list>
- Simple class. Contains pointer to first element and size.
- Has begin(), end(), size() methods.
- Does not overload any operator.

How braces {} work:
- Braces are not an operator. They are special compiler syntax.
- Two mechanisms:
    Aggregate initialization for arrays and simple structs.
    initializer_list constructor for classes like std::vector.
- Compiler decides based on the type.

Aggregate initialization:
- For arrays: int arr[4] = {1, 2, 3, 4}; Direct element initialization. No function call.
- For simple structs: Point p{1, 2}; Direct member initialization. No constructor.
- No initializer_list involved.

Initializer_list constructor:
- For classes with a constructor taking std::initializer_list.
- Compiler creates an initializer_list from the braces.
- Then calls the matching constructor.
- Example: vector(std::initializer_list<T> init);
- This constructor is a normal member function with parentheses in its parameter list.
- The braces at the call site are special syntax, not operator overloading.

Is operator overloading done inside initializer_list:
- No. std::initializer_list does not overload any operator.
- The compiler has built-in support for braces.
- The compiler creates the initializer_list automatically.
- Then passes it to the constructor.
- No operator overloading involved.

UDT example with initializer_list constructor:
- struct UDT has two constructors:
    UDT(int a, int b, int c)
    UDT(std::initializer_list<int> data)
- UDT u{1, 2, 3}; calls initializer_list constructor. Output: initializer_list constructor.
- UDT u2(1.1, 2.2, 3.3); calls 3-parameter constructor. Output: Constructor with 3 pram. Gives warnings for double to int.
- UDT u2{1.1, 2.2, 3.3}; gives errors for narrowing. Braces reject narrowing.
- This confirms: braces prefer initializer_list constructor and reject narrowing.

Storing initializer_list as member:
- m_data is std::initializer_list<int> member.
- initializer_list does not own the data. It points to a temporary array.
- The temporary array may be destroyed after the constructor ends.
- Storing initializer_list as a member is dangerous. Can lead to dangling reference.
- Better to copy the values into std::vector or another owning container.

Warning observed:
- Line m_data; gives warning: expression result unused.
- m_data; just reads the member and does nothing. It is a no-op.

Summary of rules:
- Braces {} reject narrowing conversions. Parentheses () allow them with warnings.
- Braces {} prefer initializer_list constructor.
- Parentheses () use regular constructors.
- Braces are special compiler syntax, not operator overloading.
- std::initializer_list has no operator overloading.
- initializer_list does not own its data. Do not store it as a member.
- Arrays and simple structs use aggregate initialization with braces.
- Classes with initializer_list constructor use that constructor with braces.

Interview traps:
- Braces {} are not operator overloading.
- initializer_list does not own data. Storing it as a member is dangerous.
- Parentheses () do not use initializer_list constructor in the same way.
- Narrowing is an error with braces, warning with parentheses.
- Aggregate initialization and initializer_list constructor are two different mechanisms.

One-line summary:
Braces {} are special compiler syntax. They work in two ways: aggregate initialization for arrays and simple structs, and initializer_list constructor call for classes like std::vector. Braces reject narrowing conversions. Parentheses allow narrowing with warnings. std::initializer_list does not overload any operator and does not own its data. Do not store initializer_list as a member.

45. COMPOSITION VS AGGREGATION

Composition:
- Strong ownership.
- Part cannot exist without the whole.
- When whole is destroyed, part is also destroyed.
- Part is owned by the whole.
- Member type: value or unique_ptr.
- Lifetime of part tied to lifetime of whole.

Example:
    class Engine {
    public:
        Engine() { std::cout << "Engine created\n"; }
        ~Engine() { std::cout << "Engine destroyed\n"; }
    };

    class Car {
        Engine engine;   // value member: composition
    public:
        Car() { std::cout << "Car created\n"; }
        ~Car() { std::cout << "Car destroyed\n"; }
    };

Behavior:
- Creating Car creates Engine.
- Destroying Car destroys Engine.
- Engine cannot exist without Car.

Aggregation:
- Weak ownership.
- Part can exist independently of whole.
- When whole is destroyed, part may still exist.
- Part is not owned by whole.
- Member type: pointer or reference.
- Lifetime of part not tied to lifetime of whole.

Example:
    class Professor {
    public:
        Professor() { std::cout << "Professor created\n"; }
        ~Professor() { std::cout << "Professor destroyed\n"; }
    };

    class Department {
        Professor* professor;   // pointer member: aggregation
    public:
        Department(Professor* p) : professor(p) { }
        ~Department() { }       // does not delete professor
    };

Behavior:
- Professor created outside Department.
- Department holds pointer or reference to Professor.
- Destroying Department does not destroy Professor.
- Professor can outlive Department.

Key differences:
- Composition: strong ownership; part dies with whole; member is value or unique_ptr.
- Aggregation: weak ownership; part independent; member is pointer or reference.

Character example:
- Point2D m_position and m_direction are composition. Character owns them.
- Point2D& ref_home is aggregation. Character refers to external Point2D but does not own it.
- External Point2D p is created outside and can outlive Character.

Reference member initialization:
- Reference member must be initialized in member initializer list.
- Character(Point2D& ref) : ref_home(ref) { } is correct.
- Assignment in constructor body (ref_home = ref;) is wrong. References cannot be assigned after binding.
- Error: constructor must explicitly initialize reference member.
- The line ref_home = ref; inside body causes error: cannot assign to reference member.
- Remove that line. Initializer list already binds the reference.

Missing semicolon error:
- struct Point2D { int x, y; } must end with semicolon.
- Error: expected ';' after struct.
- Fix: add semicolon after closing brace.

Interview traps:
- Composition is not just has-a. It is owns-a with lifetime control.
- Aggregation is has-a without ownership.
- Value members are composition.
- Pointer and reference members are usually aggregation.
- unique_ptr member is composition (ownership).
- shared_ptr member can be composition or aggregation depending on design.
- Raw pointer member is usually aggregation, not composition.
- Reference member must be initialized in initializer list, not assigned in body.

Core rule:
- Composition: whole owns part; part dies with whole; use value or unique_ptr.
- Aggregation: whole refers to part; part can outlive whole; use pointer or reference.
- Reference member: initialize in initializer list; cannot assign in constructor body.

One-line summary:
Composition means the part is owned by the whole and dies with it. Aggregation means the whole refers to a part but does not own it, and the part can outlive the whole. Value members are composition. Pointer and reference members are usually aggregation. Reference members must be initialized in the member initializer list, not assigned in the constructor body.

47. VIRTUAL INHERITANCE AND THE DIAMOND PROBLEM

The diamond problem:
- A common base class is inherited by two or more derived classes.
- Those derived classes are then inherited by a final class.
- The final class contains multiple copies of the common base.
- Accessing a member from the common base becomes ambiguous.
- Example: Entity -> Warrior, Entity -> Goblin, Player -> Warrior + Goblin.
- Player has two Entity subobjects: one through Warrior, one through Goblin.
- mike.x is ambiguous because the compiler does not know which Entity you mean.

Non-virtual inheritance:
- Each path creates its own base subobject.
- Warrior has its own Entity.
- Goblin has its own Entity.
- Player has two Entity subobjects.
- Debugger showed:
    Player mike = {
      Warrior = { Entity = (x=4, y=5), power=10 }
      Goblin  = { Entity = (x=4, y=5), power=7 }
    }
- To access, you must use scope:
    mike.Warrior::x
    mike.Goblin::x
- Output: 4 5 4 5 7
- Both Entity subobjects have same values but are separate objects.
- Changing one does not affect the other.

Virtual inheritance fix:
- Use virtual public when inheriting the common base.
    struct Warrior : virtual public Entity
    struct Goblin : virtual public Entity
- Now Player has only one shared Entity subobject.
- The two paths merge into a single Entity.
- mike.Warrior::x and mike.Goblin::x refer to the same Entity.
- Plain mike.x becomes unambiguous.
- You can still use scope resolution, but it points to the same object.

Code:
    struct Entity {
        int x{4}, y{5};
    };

    struct Warrior : virtual public Entity {
        int power{10};
    };

    struct Goblin : virtual public Entity {
        int power{7};
    };

    struct Player : public Warrior, public Goblin {
    };

    int main() {
        Player mike;
        std::cout << mike.Warrior::x << std::endl;
        std::cout << mike.Warrior::y << std::endl;
        std::cout << mike.Goblin::x << std::endl;
        std::cout << mike.Goblin::y << std::endl;
        std::cout << mike.Goblin::power << std::endl;
        return 0;
    }

Expected output:
    4
    5
    4
    5
    7

But note:
- mike.Warrior::x and mike.Goblin::x now access the same Entity.
- Their addresses are the same.
- Modifying one affects the other.
- In the non-virtual case, they were separate objects.

Key points:
- Virtual inheritance solves the diamond problem by sharing the common base.
- The common base is constructed only once.
- The most derived class (Player) is responsible for constructing the virtual base.
- Order of construction: virtual base first, then other bases in declaration order, then derived.
- Virtual inheritance adds some overhead and complexity.
- Use it only when necessary (true diamond).

Interview traps:
- Diamond problem causes ambiguous member access.
- Virtual inheritance creates a single shared base subobject.
- Scope resolution can still be used but points to the same object.
- Without virtual, each path has its own base subobject.
- With virtual, there is only one base subobject.
- Virtual base is constructed by the most derived class.
- Virtual inheritance is not the same as virtual functions.
- Virtual inheritance affects object layout and size.

One-line summary:
Virtual inheritance solves the diamond problem by ensuring only one copy of the common base class exists in the most derived object. Without it, multiple copies exist and member access is ambiguous. Use virtual public when inheriting a base that appears more than once in the hierarchy.

49. ALIGNOF AND ALIGNMENT

Observation:
- Code:
    struct GameState {
        bool checkpoint;
        float score;
        short numberOfPlayers;
    };

    int main() {
        GameState gs;
        std::cout << alignof(gs) << std::endl;
        return 0;
    }

- Output: 4
- Warning: 'alignof' applied to an expression is a GNU extension [-Wgnu-alignof-expression]

What alignof does:
- alignof returns the alignment requirement of a type.
- Alignment is the memory address boundary that an object of that type must be placed on.
- For example, an alignment of 4 means the object's address must be a multiple of 4.
- alignof is a compile-time operator.

Why alignof(GameState) is 4:
- GameState contains:
    bool checkpoint (1 byte, alignment 1)
    float score (4 bytes, alignment 4)
    short numberOfPlayers (2 bytes, alignment 2)
- The alignment of a struct is the maximum alignment of its members.
- float has alignment 4, which is the largest among the members.
- Therefore, GameState has alignment 4.

Standard vs GNU extension:
- Standard C++: alignof takes a type, not an expression.
    Correct: alignof(GameState)
- GNU extension: alignof can take an expression.
    alignof(gs) works but gives warning.
- To avoid the warning, use alignof(GameState).

Memory layout and size:
- Members are laid out in order with padding to satisfy alignment.
- bool checkpoint at offset 0.
- 3 bytes padding to align float to offset 4.
- float score at offset 4.
- short numberOfPlayers at offset 8
- 2 bytes padding at the end to make total size a multiple of alignment (4).
- Total size: 12 bytes.
- Alignment: 4 bytes.

Connection to earlier leak observation:
- Earlier, 100 allocations of GameState showed 32 bytes leaked each.
- That 32 bytes was from the memory allocator's bucket size, not the actual object size.
- The actual object size is likely 12 bytes.
- malloc rounds up small allocations to a bucket size (e.g., 16 or 32 bytes) for efficiency.
- This is why leaks reported 32 bytes per allocation.

Key points:
- alignof gives the alignment requirement of a type.
- For a struct, alignment is the maximum alignment of its members.
- Use alignof(Type) in standard C++, not alignof(expression).
- Padding ensures each member is properly aligned.
- Total struct size is padded to a multiple of its alignment.
- Allocator may use larger bucket sizes, which is why leak reports may show more bytes than sizeof.

Interview traps:
- alignof is not the same as sizeof.
- alignof on an expression is a GNU extension; standard C++ requires a type.
- Struct alignment is determined by the largest member alignment.
- Padding can increase struct size.
- Memory allocator bucket sizes can differ from sizeof.

One-line summary:
alignof returns the alignment requirement of a type. For GameState, the largest member is float with alignment 4, so the struct alignment is 4. Use alignof(GameState) instead of alignof(gs) to avoid GNU extension warning. Padding ensures proper alignment and may increase the struct size beyond the sum of its members.

50. PIMPL IDIOM

Definition:
- Pimpl means Pointer to Implementation.
- It hides implementation details from the header file.
- The public class holds only a pointer to a forward-declared implementation class.
- The implementation class is defined in the .cpp file.

Why use Pimpl:
- Reduces compilation dependencies.
- Header does not need to include all private member headers.
- Changing implementation does not force recompilation of user code.
- Keeps ABI stable.

Basic structure with raw pointer:

person.hpp:
    #ifndef PERSON_HPP
    #define PERSON_HPP
    #include <string>
    class Person {
    public:
        Person(std::string s);
        ~Person();
        std::string getAttributes();
    private:
        struct pImplPerson;
        pImplPerson* m_impl;
    };
    #endif

person.cpp:
    #include "person.hpp"
    #include <iostream>

    struct Person::pImplPerson {
        std::string m_name;
        std::string m_strength;
        std::string m_speed;
    };

    Person::Person(std::string s) {
        m_impl = new pImplPerson;
        m_impl->m_name = s;
        m_impl->m_strength = "n/a";
        m_impl->m_speed = "n/a";
    }

    Person::~Person() {
        delete m_impl;
    }

    std::string Person::getAttributes() {
        return m_impl->m_name + "," + m_impl->m_strength + "," + m_impl->m_speed;
    }

main.cpp:
    #include <iostream>
    #include "person.hpp"
    int main() {
        Person mike("mike");
        std::cout << mike.getAttributes() << std::endl;
        return 0;
    }

Output:
    mike,n/a,n/a

Common mistake:
- If m_impl is not allocated in constructor, it is uninitialized.
- Dereferencing m_impl->m_name gives undefined behavior.
- On macOS ARM64, this can cause a bus error.
- Fix: allocate m_impl with new pImplPerson in constructor.
- Also delete m_impl in destructor.

Using std::unique_ptr:

person.hpp:
    #include <memory>
    #include <string>
    class Person {
    public:
        Person(std::string s);
        ~Person();
        std::string getAttributes();
    private:
        struct pImplPerson;
        std::unique_ptr<pImplPerson> m_impl;
    };

person.cpp:
    Person::Person(std::string s) {
        m_impl = std::make_unique<pImplPerson>();
        m_impl->m_name = s;
        m_impl->m_strength = "n/a";
        m_impl->m_speed = "n/a";
    }

    Person::~Person() {
    }

    std::string Person::getAttributes() {
        return m_impl->m_name + "," + m_impl->m_strength + "," + m_impl->m_speed;
    }

Key point:
- unique_ptr automatically deletes m_impl.
- Destructor can be empty.
- But destructor must still be defined in the .cpp file.
- Why: unique_ptr needs complete type of pImplPerson at destruction.
- At header, pImplPerson is incomplete. So default destructor in header would fail.
- Defining destructor in .cpp where pImplPerson is complete solves the problem.

Interview traps:
- Pimpl hides implementation and reduces compile-time dependencies.
- Raw pointer version requires manual new and delete.
- unique_ptr version is safer but still requires destructor in .cpp.
- Uninitialized pointer in Pimpl causes bus error or segfault.
- Forward declaration of nested struct is enough in header.
- Full definition of pImplPerson goes in .cpp.

One-line summary:
Pimpl idiom uses a pointer to a forward-declared implementation class to hide details from the header. The implementation is defined in the .cpp file. Use new/delete or unique_ptr to manage the pointer. If using unique_ptr, define the destructor in the .cpp file because the implementation type is incomplete in the header.

51. NAME SHADOWING IN CONSTRUCTOR AND MEMBER INITIALIZER LIST FIX

Problem case:
- Code:
    class Person {
    public:
        Person(int age) {
            age = age;
        }
        int getAge() const { return age; }
    private:
        int age;
    };

    int main() {
        Person p1(400);
        std::cout << p1.getAge() << std::endl;
    }

- Output: -291929896 (garbage value) 

Why garbage output:
- Constructor has parameter named age and member named age.
- Inside constructor body, age = age; assigns parameter to itself.
- Member variable age is not touched.
- Member age remains uninitialized.
- getAge() returns uninitialized member age.
- This is undefined behavior / garbage value.

Name shadowing:
- Function parameter shadows the class data member.
- Inside the constructor body, the name age refers to the parameter, not the member.
- So member is never initialized.

Fixes:

Fix 1: Use this pointer
- Person(int age) {
      this->age = age;
  }
- this->age refers to member, age on right refers to parameter.

Fix 2: Use member initializer list
- Person(int age) : age(age) {
  }
- Here age(age) means: initialize member age with parameter age.
- This is the preferred way.

Fix 3: Rename parameter
- Person(int a) : age(a) {
  }
- Or Person(int a) { age = a; }

Output after fix:
- Person p1(400);
- p1.getAge() returns 400.
- Correct output.

Key points:
- Member initializer list syntax distinguishes member from parameter.
- age(age) means member age initialized with parameter age.
- Constructor body assignment can be shadowed by parameter name.
- Prefer member initializer list for clarity and correctness.

Interview traps:
- age = age; in constructor body does not initialize the member. It assigns parameter to itself.
- Use this->age = age; or member initializer list : age(age).
- Name shadowing can silently leave members uninitialized.
- Uninitialized member read is undefined behavior.

One-line summary:
When a constructor parameter has the same name as a data member, assignment in the body (age = age) only touches the parameter, leaving the member uninitialized. Use this->age = age or member initializer list : age(age) to correctly initialize the member.

52. FUNCTION TEMPLATES

What is a function template:
- A blueprint for generating functions.
- One function definition works with many types.
- Compiler generates a separate function for each type used.
- This is compile-time polymorphism.

Basic syntax:
    template <typename T>
    T square(T input) {
        return input * input;
    }

- template keyword starts template declaration.
- typename T declares a type parameter.
- class can be used instead of typename. Both mean the same here.
- T is a placeholder for the actual type.

Implicit template argument deduction:
- Code:
    std::cout << square(5) << std::endl;
    std::cout << square(5.5) << std::endl;
    std::cout << square(5.6f) << std::endl;

- Output:
    25
    30.25
    31.36

- square(5): T is deduced as int. Result 25.
- square(5.5): T is deduced as double. Result 30.25.
- square(5.6f): T is deduced as float. Result 31.36.
- Compiler looks at argument type and sets T accordingly.

Explicit template arguments:
- Code:
    std::cout << square<int>(5) << std::endl;
    std::cout << square<double>(5.5) << std::endl;
    std::cout << square<float>(5.6f) << std::endl;

- Output:
    25
    30.25
    31.36

- Here T is explicitly specified inside angle brackets.
- square<int>(5): T is int.
- square<double>(5.5): T is double.
- square<float>(5.6f): T is float.
- No deduction needed. You tell the compiler directly.

When to use explicit template arguments:
- When deduction is not possible.
- When you want to force a specific type.
- When there is ambiguity.
- When converting the argument to a different type is desired.
- Example: square<double>(5) would use T as double even though 5 is int.

Implicit vs explicit comparison:
- Implicit: square(5) -> compiler deduces T from argument.
- Explicit: square<int>(5) -> programmer specifies T directly.
- Both produce the same result when types match.

How compiler handles templates:
- When you call square(5), compiler generates a function square<int>.
- When you call square(5.5), compiler generates square<double>.
- When you call square(5.6f), compiler generates square<float>.
- These are separate functions in the compiled code.
- This process is called template instantiation.

Key points:
- Function templates enable generic programming.
- T is deduced automatically in most cases.
- Explicit template arguments override deduction.
- Each type used creates a separate instantiation.
- Templates are resolved at compile time, not runtime.

Interview traps:
- typename and class are interchangeable as template parameters.
- Template code is not compiled until instantiated.
- Explicit template arguments are written with angle brackets after function name.
- Implicit deduction happens from argument types.
- Templates are compile-time polymorphism, unlike virtual functions which are runtime polymorphism.
- If T is explicitly specified, the argument may be converted to that type.

One-line summary:
Function templates let you write one function that works with many types. The compiler deduces T from the argument (implicit) or you specify T directly (explicit). Each type used generates a separate function at compile time. This is compile-time polymorphism.

54. C++ INSIGHTS AND NON-TYPE TEMPLATE PARAMETERS

Code:

    #include <iostream>
    #include <typeinfo>

    template<typename T1, size_t N>
    void foo(T1 input1) {
        std::cout << typeid(input1).name() << std::endl;
        std::cout << typeid(N).name() << std::endl;
    }

    int main() {
        foo<int, 5>(5);
        return 0;
    }

C++ Insights transformed output:

    template<>
    void foo<int, 5>(int input1) {
        std::operator<<(std::cout, typeid(input1).name()).operator<<(std::endl);
        std::operator<<(std::cout, typeid(5UL).name()).operator<<(std::endl);
    }

Key observations from C++ Insights:

- T1 is deduced as int.
- N is explicitly provided as 5.
- The compiler generates foo<int, 5>.
- Non-type template parameter N is replaced by the literal 5UL.
- N is of type size_t. On this platform, size_t is unsigned long.
- The compiler represents the value 5 as 5UL (5 unsigned long).
- typeid(N).name() becomes typeid(5UL).name().

Why this matters:

- Non-type template parameters are compile-time constants.
- They are not function arguments. They do not exist at runtime.
- The value is baked directly into the generated function.
- There is no memory storage for N at runtime.
- This is why non-type template parameters must be compile-time constants.

Output:

- typeid(input1).name() gives "i" for int.
- typeid(5UL).name() gives "m" for unsigned long.
- This matches the previous observation.

Operator translation:

- std::cout << typeid(input1).name() << std::endl;
- becomes:
    std::operator<<(std::cout, typeid(input1).name()).operator<<(std::endl);
- This shows that << is a function call, not special syntax.

Key points:

- size_t is typically unsigned long on 64-bit systems.
- C++ Insights reveals how the compiler handles non-type template parameters.
- The value is substituted as a literal (5UL) in the generated code.
- typeid(...).name() is implementation/ABI dependent.

Interview traps:

- Non-type template parameters are compile-time constants, not runtime values.
- The compiler substitutes the literal value directly into the instantiated code.
- size_t is not a distinct type. It is an alias for an unsigned integer type, usually unsigned long.
- Do not confuse non-type template parameters with regular function parameters.

One-line summary:
C++ Insights shows that non-type template parameters are substituted with their literal values (like 5UL) during template instantiation. They do not exist at runtime and are not passed as function arguments. size_t maps to unsigned long on this platform, so typeid(N).name() becomes typeid(5UL).name() giving "m".

53. VARIADIC TEMPLATES

What is a variadic template:
- A template that accepts any number of template arguments.
- Uses a parameter pack: typename... Args.
- Enables writing functions with variable number of arguments with type safety.

Basic syntax:
- Parameter pack in template: typename... Args
- Function parameter pack: Args... args
- Expansion: args...

Example:
    template<typename T>
    T sum(T args) {
        return args;
    }

    template<typename T, typename... Args>
    T sum(T start, Args... args) {
        return start + sum(args...);
    }

Two overloads:
- Base case: single argument, returns it.
- Recursive case: takes first argument plus pack, returns start + sum(rest).

Call:
    sum<int>(1, 2.2f, 3.4f, 4, 5)

Expansion:
    1 + sum(2.2f, 3.4f, 4, 5)
    1 + 2.2f + sum(3.4f, 4, 5)
    1 + 2.2f + 3.4f + sum(4, 5)
    1 + 2.2f + 3.4f + 4 + sum(5)
    1 + 2.2f + 3.4f + 4 + 5

Result mathematically: 15.6

Return type effect:
- If T is int (sum<int>), final result 15.6 is converted to int, giving 15.
- If T is double (sum<double, double>), final result is 15.6.
- Output: 15 vs 15.6.

C++ Insights observations for sum<int>(1, 2.2f, 3.4f, 4, 5):
- Base case instantiated: sum<int>(int args)
- Recursive case instantiated: sum<int, float, float, int, int>(int start, float, float, int, int)
- Return expression: static_cast<int>(static_cast<float>(start) + sum(...))
- Each recursion step shown as separate instantiation:
    sum<float, float, int, int>
    sum<float, int, int>
    sum<int, int>
    sum<int>
- Float literals shown as 2.20000005F, 3.4000001F.

For sum<double, double>(1, 2.2f, 3.4f, 4, 5):
- T is explicitly double.
- Args starts with double and rest deduced.
- Return type is double.
- Additions happen in floating-point.
- Result is 15.6, no truncation.

Key points:
- Variadic templates accept any number of arguments.
- Parameter pack: typename... Args, Args... args, args...
- Recursive expansion peels off one argument at a time.
- Base case must exist to stop recursion.
- Explicit template arguments control T.
- Return type T determines whether truncation occurs.
- Compiler generates separate instantiations for each recursion step.
- C++ Insights shows all generated instantiations.

Use cases:
- Type-safe printf-like functions.
- Perfect forwarding wrappers.
- Tuple, variant implementations.
- Emplace functions like vector::emplace_back.
- std::make_unique, std::make_shared.

Interview traps:
- Variadic templates are compile-time, not runtime like C varargs.
- Base case is required to stop recursion.
- Parameter pack expansion uses the ... syntax.
- Return type T affects final numeric result.
- Explicit template arguments can change deduced types.
- C++ Insights reveals the recursive instantiation chain.

One-line summary:
Variadic templates accept any number of arguments using a parameter pack. Recursion peels off one argument at a time until the base case is reached. Explicit template arguments like <int> or <double> control the return type and therefore whether truncation happens. sum<int>(1, 2.2f, 3.4f, 4, 5) gives 15 due to truncation. sum<double, double>(1, 2.2f, 3.4f, 4, 5) gives 15.6 because the return type is double.

54. TRAILING RETURN TYPE WITH DECLTYPE IN TEMPLATES

Code:

    #include <iostream>

    template<typename T1, typename T2>
    auto multiply(const T1& a, const T2& b) -> decltype(a * b) {
        return a * b;
    }

    int main() {
        std::cout << multiply<float, int>(7.22f, 5) << std::endl;
        return 0;
    }

Output:

    36.1

Explanation:

- Trailing return type syntax: auto function(...) -> return_type
- The return type is decltype(a * b).
- decltype(a * b) is the type of the expression a * b.
- Here a is float, b is int. float * int gives float.
- So the return type is float.
- 7.22f * 5 = 36.1, printed as float.
- Parameters use const T1& and const T2& to avoid copies.
- Explicit template arguments <float, int> set T1 = float, T2 = int.

Why use decltype in trailing return type:

- When the return type depends on the template parameters.
- When the return type is not simply T1 or T2.
- Common in generic code where the result type is the type of an expression.
- Before C++14, this was the only way to write such templates.
- In C++14 and later, you can just write auto without trailing return type, and the compiler deduces it.

Key points:

- Trailing return type: auto f(...) -> decltype(expr)
- decltype(expr) gives the exact type of the expression.
- Useful when return type depends on template parameters.
- const T1& and const T2& avoid copying large objects.
- Explicit template arguments can force specific types.

Interview traps:

- decltype(a * b) uses the declared types of a and b, not their values.
- Without trailing return type, pre-C++14 could not express such generic return types easily.
- C++14 relaxed this: auto alone works.
- The trailing return type is still useful in some cases, e.g., when the return type is not deducible from the body alone or when using SFINAE.

One-line summary:
Trailing return type with decltype lets a function template express a return type that depends on the types of its parameters. In multiply<float, int>, float * int yields float, so the return type is float. The result 36.1 is printed as a float.

55. CLASS TEMPLATES AND NON-TYPE TEMPLATE PARAMETERS

Code:

    #include <iostream>

    template<typename T, int size>
    class Container {
    public:
        inline Container() {
            this->m_data = new T[static_cast<unsigned long>(size)];
        }

        inline ~Container() {
            delete[] this->m_data;
        }

        // inline Container(const Container<T, size> &) = delete;
        // inline Container<T, size> & operator=(const Container<T, size> &) = delete;

    private:
        T * m_data;
    };

    int main() {
        Container<int, 10> c1 = Container<int, 10>();
        Container<double, 10> c2 = Container<double, 10>();
        Container<float, 10> c3 = Container<float, 10>();
        return 0;
    }

C++ Insights transformed output:

- It generates explicit specializations for each instantiation:
    template<> class Container<int, 10>
    template<> class Container<double, 10>
    template<> class Container<float, 10>

- Inside each specialization:
    - The constructor replaces size with the literal 10:
        this->m_data = new int[static_cast<unsigned long>(10)];
    - The destructor is marked noexcept:
        inline ~Container() noexcept { delete[] this->m_data; }
    - The commented out copy operations are not generated, so the compiler provides implicit copy constructor and copy assignment.

Observations:

- Class templates allow generic classes.
- Non-type template parameter int size dictates compile-time array size.
- size is not a runtime variable; it is replaced by the literal 10 in the generated code.
- The constructor uses static_cast<unsigned long>(size) internally for array allocation.
- The copy constructor and copy assignment are commented out. This means the compiler will generate them implicitly.
- Implicit copy will be shallow: it copies the pointer m_data, not the array.
- Two objects sharing the same m_data will cause double delete when both are destroyed.
- This is a critical bug. The comments should be removed to actually delete the copy operations.

Key Points:

- Class template syntax: template<typename T, int size> class Name { ... };
- Instantiation: ClassName<Type, value> object;
- Non-type template parameters are compile-time constants.
- size is baked into the class at compile time.
- Compiler generates separate class definitions for each unique combination of template arguments.
- C++ Insights shows the exact generated code for each instantiation.
- Destructor is noexcept by default for standard types in some contexts.

Why the copy operations matter:

- If copy constructor and copy assignment are commented out, they are implicitly generated.
- Implicit copy will copy the pointer m_data, causing two objects to share the same dynamic array.
- When both go out of scope, delete[] m_data will be called twice, causing a double-free error.
- To prevent this, uncomment the delete lines. This makes the class non-copyable.

Interview traps:

- Non-type template parameters are compile-time constants, not runtime values.
- size is replaced by its literal value in the generated code.
- Commenting out = delete does not delete the function. It generates it implicitly.
- Dynamic memory management in templates requires care with copying.
- Use = delete to prevent copying when the class owns a resource.
- C++ Insights reveals the generated code and helps spot implicit operations.

One-line summary:
Class templates with non-type parameters like int size allow compile-time array sizing. The compiler generates a separate class for each combination of template arguments. The size is replaced by its literal value. If copy constructor and copy assignment are commented out, the compiler generates them implicitly, leading to shallow copies and potential double delete. Use = delete to prevent copying when the class owns a resource.

56. CLASS TEMPLATE ARGUMENT DEDUCTION (CTAD)

What is CTAD:
- CTAD means Class Template Argument Deduction.
- Introduced in C++17.
- Allows writing ClassName obj(args); without specifying template arguments.
- The compiler deduces template parameters from constructor arguments.

Basic example:

    template<typename first, typename second>
    struct Entry {
        Entry(first key, second val) {
            m_key = key;
            m_val = val;
        }
        first m_key;
        second m_val;
    };

    int main() {
        Entry entry(5, 100.1f);
        std::cout << entry.m_key << std::endl;
        std::cout << entry.m_val << std::endl;
    }

Output:
    5
    100.1

Observations:
- first is deduced as int from argument 5.
- second is deduced as float from argument 100.1f.
- No explicit template arguments needed.

Before C++17:
- You had to write: Entry<int, float> entry(5, 100.1f);

How CTAD works:
- The compiler generates an implicit deduction guide.
- The deduction guide is a function-like declaration:
    template<typename first, typename second>
    Entry(first key, second val) -> Entry<first, second>;
- The compiler uses this guide to match constructor arguments to template parameters.

C++ Insights output for Entry entry(5, 100.1f):
- Shows: Entry<int, float> entry = Entry<int, float>(5, 100.099998F);
- Shows the deduction guide at the bottom:
    template<typename first, typename second>
    Entry(first key, second val) -> Entry<first, second>;
- Shows the explicit instantiation for <int, float>.

CTAD failure case with std::vector:

Code:
    std::vector v1{1, 2.0f, 3, 4, 5};

Error:
    no viable constructor or deduction guide for deduction of template arguments of 'std::vector'

Why:
- The initializer list has mixed types: int and float.
- CTAD cannot deduce a single _Tp from mixed types.
- The deduction guide for initializer_list requires all elements to have the same type.

CTAD success case with std::vector:

Code:
    std::vector v1{1.0f, 2.0f, 3.0f, 4.0f, 5.0f};

Result:
- All elements are float.
- CTAD deduces _Tp = float.
- Works correctly.

C++ Insights output:
    std::vector<float, std::allocator<float>> v1 =
        std::vector<float, std::allocator<float>>{
            std::initializer_list<float>{1.0F, 2.0F, 3.0F, 4.0F, 5.0F},
            std::allocator<float>()
        };
- Shows explicit type float and the initializer_list construction.

Fixes for CTAD failure:
- Specify the type explicitly: std::vector<float> v1{1, 2.0f, 3, 4, 5};
- Or make all elements the same type: std::vector v1{1.0f, 2.0f, 3.0f, 4.0f, 5.0f};
- Or use explicit template arguments: std::vector<float> v1{1, 2.0f, 3, 4, 5};

Key points:
- CTAD was introduced in C++17.
- Works when the compiler can deduce a consistent type from constructor arguments.
- Mixed types in initializer_list break CTAD for std::vector.
- Implicit deduction guides are generated by the compiler.
- C++ Insights reveals the deduction guide explicitly.
- Float literals are shown with full precision in Insights output (100.099998F).
- The initializer_list deduction guide requires all elements to have the same type.

Interview traps:
- CTAD is not the same as template argument deduction for functions. It applies to class templates.
- Mixed types in an initializer_list break CTAD.
- Implicit deduction guides are generated automatically but can also be user-defined.
- Before C++17, all template arguments had to be specified explicitly.
- C++ Insights is a useful tool to see the generated deduction guides.

One-line summary:
CTAD (Class Template Argument Deduction) allows writing ClassName obj(args) without explicit template arguments. The compiler deduces template parameters from constructor arguments using implicit deduction guides. It works when all initializer_list elements have the same type. Mixed types like {1, 2.0f, 3} break CTAD for std::vector. Use explicit template arguments or consistent types to fix the issue.

57. MEMORY CHECKING TOOLS: VALGRIND VS LEAKS ON MACOS

Context:
- unique_pointer_delete program uses unique_ptr with a custom deleter IntDeleter.
- Wanted to check for memory leaks.

Tool 1: Valgrind

Command:
    valgrind ./unique_pointer_delete

Output:
    Assertion failed: (p), function atexit_register, file atexit.c, line 115.
    zsh: abort valgrind ./unique_pointer_delete

Observation:
- Valgrind fails with an assertion error on macOS ARM64.
- Valgrind has limited support on Apple Silicon.
- It is not reliable on this platform in this setup.

Tool 2: leaks

Command:
    leaks --atExit -- ./unique_pointer_delete

Output (important parts):
    IntDeleter Called
    Process 38686 is not debuggable. Due to security restrictions, leaks can only show or save contents of readonly memory of restricted processes.
    Physical footprint: 2288K
    Physical footprint (peak): 2288K
    Process 38686: 188 nodes malloced for 15 KB
    Process 38686: 0 leaks for 0 total leaked bytes.

Observations:
- The program ran and printed IntDeleter Called.
- leaks reported 0 leaks for 0 total leaked bytes.
- This confirms unique_ptr with custom deleter properly released memory.
- The custom deleter was invoked, so cleanup happened correctly.

About the warning:
- "Process is not debuggable due to security restrictions" is a macOS warning.
- Despite the warning, leaks still analyzed the process and reported 0 leaks.

About physical footprint:
- Physical footprint: 2288K.
- This is the total memory the process is using, not leaked memory.
- It includes stack, code, runtime libraries, and other allocations.
- Physical footprint is NOT the same as leaked memory.
- A process may have a large footprint but 0 leaks.

Comparison of tools:

Valgrind:
- Widely used on Linux.
- Not reliable on macOS ARM64 in this setup.
- Causes assertion failure.

leaks:
- Native macOS tool.
- Works on macOS ARM64.
- Reports leaked bytes and nodes.
- Use with --atExit to run the program and check at exit.
- May show warning about non-debuggable process, but still works.

AddressSanitizer (alternative):
- Compile with -fsanitize=address.
- Works well on macOS ARM64.
- Detects leaks, use-after-free, double-free, buffer overflows.
- Example:
    clang++ -g -fsanitize=address -std=c++26 file.cpp -o file

Key points:
- Valgrind is not the right tool on macOS ARM64.
- Use leaks or AddressSanitizer on macOS.
- 0 leaks confirms memory is properly released.
- Physical footprint is not the same as leaked memory.
- Custom deleter was called, confirming unique_ptr invokes it on destruction.

Interview traps:
- Valgrind is not portable to all platforms.
- On macOS, prefer leaks or AddressSanitizer.
- A process can have a large memory footprint but zero leaks.
- "Not debuggable" warning does not prevent leaks from analyzing.
- unique_ptr with custom deleter still calls the deleter at destruction.
- 0 leaks means no memory leaked, not that no memory was allocated.

One-line summary:
Valgrind fails on macOS ARM64. Use leaks or AddressSanitizer instead. leaks reports 0 leaks for unique_ptr with custom deleter, confirming proper cleanup. Physical footprint is not the same as leaked memory. A process can use 2288K of physical memory while still having 0 leaks.

58. UNIONS AND STD::VARIANT

UNION BASICS

What is a union:
- A union is a special class type where all members share the same memory location.
- Only one member can be active at a time.
- Size of union is the size of its largest member.
- Alignment of union is the alignment of its largest member.

Example:

    union U {
        int i;
        short s;
        float f;
    };

- int and float are 4 bytes each. short is 2 bytes.
- Size of U is 4 bytes.

Writing and reading:

    U u1;
    u1.i = 50;
    std::cout << u1.i << std::endl;   // prints 50

- u1.i = 50 writes 4 bytes at the union's address.
- Reading u1.i reads the same 4 bytes.

Assembly observation:
- Writing int: mov DWORD PTR [rbp-4], 0x32
- Writing short: mov WORD PTR [rbp-4], 0x28
- Reading int: mov eax, DWORD PTR [rbp-4]
- Reading short: movzx eax, WORD PTR [rbp-4]; cwde

Key points from assembly:
- Both writes go to the same address.
- Writing short only writes 2 bytes, leaving other 2 bytes unchanged.
- Reading a different member than the one written is undefined behavior.
- Union does not track which member is active. This is the programmer's responsibility.

UNION SIZE VS VARIANT SIZE

    union U { int i; short s; float f; };
    std::variant<int, float> data;

    sizeof(u1)   // 4
    sizeof(data) // 8

Why variant is larger:
- Union stores only the value. Size = 4 bytes.
- Variant stores value plus a type tag (index). Size = 8 bytes.
- The extra 4 bytes in variant is the index that tracks which alternative is active.
- Variant is safer because it knows the active type.
- Union is smaller but requires manual tracking of the active member.

STD::VARIANT BASICS

What is std::variant:
- A type-safe union.
- Holds exactly one value from a list of alternatives.
- Remembers which alternative is active.
- Defined in header <variant>.
- Introduced in C++17.

Example:

    std::variant<int, float> data;
    data = 7.0f;
    std::cout << std::get<float>(data) << std::endl;  // 7

Key point:
- Variant holds one active alternative at a time.
- The active alternative is set by assignment.

ACCESSING VARIANT VALUES

std::get<T>:
    std::get<float>(data)
- Returns the value if T is the active alternative.
- Throws std::bad_variant_access if T is in the variant but not active.
- Compile-time error if T is not in the variant's type list.

std::get_if<T>:
    if (auto attempt = std::get_if<float>(&data)) {
        std::cout << "we found a float" << std::endl;
    }
- Returns a pointer to the value if T is active.
- Returns nullptr if T is not active.
- Compile-time error if T is not in the variant's type list.
- Safe: does not throw.

std::variant::index():
    std::cout << data.index() << std::endl;
- Returns the zero-based index of the active alternative.
- For std::variant<int, float>:
    index 0 = int
    index 1 = float
- data = 7.0f sets float active, so index() returns 1.
- If valueless_by_exception, returns variant_npos.

ERRORS OBSERVED

Error 1: std::cout << data;
- std::variant has no built-in operator<< for std::ostream.
- To print, use std::get<T>, std::get_if<T>, or std::visit.

Error 2: std::get<float>(data) when data holds int.
- Throws std::bad_variant_access at runtime.
- Program terminates if not caught.

Error 3: std::get_if<long>(&data) where long is not in variant.
- Compile-time error: type not found in type list.
- static_assert failed.
- T must be one of the variant's alternatives.

Error 4: std::get << get<float>(data) << std::endl;
- std::get alone is a function, not a stream.
- Invalid syntax.

Error 5: std::edl typo instead of std::endl.
- Compile error: no member named 'edl'.

VISITING VARIANT VALUES

To print a variant without knowing the active type:

    std::visit([](auto&& arg) {
        std::cout << arg << std::endl;
    }, data);

- std::visit calls the lambda with the active alternative.
- The lambda must handle all possible alternatives.
- This is the safe, generic way to process a variant.

COMPARISON TABLE (in plain text)

Union:
- Size: largest member size (4 bytes for int/float).
- Type safety: no.
- Tracks active member: no.
- Access wrong member: undefined behavior.
- Access mechanism: direct member access.

Variant:
- Size: largest member size plus index (8 bytes for int/float).
- Type safety: yes.
- Tracks active member: yes.
- Access wrong member: throws bad_variant_access.
- Access mechanism: std::get, std::get_if, std::visit.

INTERVIEW TRAPS

- Union does not track the active member.
- Reading a different member than the one written is undefined behavior.
- Variant throws std::bad_variant_access if you request the wrong type.
- std::get_if returns nullptr instead of throwing.
- std::get_if and std::get require T to be in the variant's type list.
- std::variant has no operator<<. Use std::visit or std::get.
- std::variant index is zero-based and matches the order of alternatives.
- Union is smaller but less safe than variant.
- size of variant includes the index plus padding.

CORE RULE

- Union: shared memory, manual tracking, smallest size, unsafe.
- Variant: shared memory plus index, automatic tracking, slightly larger, safe.
- std::get: returns value or throws.
- std::get_if: returns pointer or nullptr.
- std::visit: calls a callable with the active alternative.
- std::variant::index(): returns the active alternative's index.

ONE-LINE SUMMARY

A union stores all members in the same memory but does not track which member is active. Reading a different member is undefined behavior. std::variant is a type-safe union that remembers the active alternative with an index. Use std::get to access a specific type, std::get_if for safe checking, std::visit to handle all alternatives, and index() to check the active alternative. Variant is slightly larger because of the index but much safer.

59. ASSERT VS STATIC_ASSERT

assert:

- Defined in header <cassert>.
- Works at runtime.
- Checks a condition while the program is running.
- If condition is true, nothing happens.
- If condition is false, prints an error message and calls abort().
- Error message includes the failed expression, function name, file name, and line number.

Example:

    int age = -7;
    assert(age > 0);

Output on failure:

    Assertion failed: (age > 0), function main, file cassert.cpp, line 9.

Adding a custom message:

    assert(age > 0 && "age was greater than 0");

- The string literal is always truthy.
- So the logic is still age > 0.
- The assertion message now includes the string.

Output:

    Assertion failed: (age > 0 && "age was greater than 0"), function main, file cassert.cpp, line 9.

- assert is disabled when NDEBUG is defined. Typically in release builds.
- assert can use runtime variables and any runtime expression.

static_assert:

- Built into the language. No header needed.
- Works at compile time.
- Checks a condition while the program is compiled.
- If condition is true, nothing happens.
- If condition is false, compilation fails with an error.

Example:

    static_assert(sizeof(int) == 4, "int must be 4 bytes");

- Condition must be a compile-time constant expression.
- Message is optional but recommended.

Trying to use a runtime variable:

    int age = -7;
    static_assert(age > 0 && "Message");

Error:

    static assertion expression is not an integral constant expression
    note: read of non-const variable 'age' is not allowed in a constant expression

Why it fails:

- static_assert requires a constant expression.
- age is a non-const int variable.
- Its value is not known at compile time.

Key differences:

assert:
- Checked at runtime.
- Can use runtime variables.
- Failure aborts the program.
- Disabled by NDEBUG.
- No compile-time cost.

static_assert:
- Checked at compile time.
- Must use constant expressions.
- Failure stops compilation.
- Not disabled by NDEBUG.
- No runtime cost because it is checked only at compile time.

Interview traps:

- assert is a macro, not a function. It can be disabled.
- static_assert is a keyword, not a macro. It cannot be disabled.
- static_assert requires a compile-time constant expression.
- assert can use runtime variables.
- Do not use assert for conditions that must always be true. Use static_assert for compile-time invariants.
- Do not use static_assert for runtime checks.

Core rule:

- assert: runtime check, abort on failure, disabled by NDEBUG.
- static_assert: compile-time check, compile error on failure, always active.

One-line summary:
assert checks conditions at runtime and aborts if false. It can use runtime variables. static_assert checks conditions at compile time and gives a compile error if false. It requires constant expressions. Use assert for runtime validation and static_assert for compile-time invariants.

60. TYPE CASTING IN C++

static_cast:
- Compile-time cast.
- Used for upcasting (Derived* to Base*) which is safe.
- Used for downcasting (Base* to Derived*) but unsafe: no runtime check.
- Used for numeric conversions like int to float, float to int.
- Does not require virtual functions.
- Fast, no runtime overhead.
- If downcasting incorrectly, undefined behavior can occur.

dynamic_cast:
- Runtime cast.
- Requires polymorphic base class (at least one virtual function).
- Used for safe downcasting.
- For pointers: returns nullptr if cast fails.
- For references: throws std::bad_cast if cast fails.
- Slower due to runtime type check.
- Relies on RTTI (Run-Time Type Information) and vtable.

reinterpret_cast:
- Most unsafe cast.
- No compile-time or runtime check.
- Reinterprets the bits at a memory location as a different type.
- Can cast between unrelated pointer types, pointer to integer, integer to pointer.
- Does not change the data, only the interpretation.
- Used in low-level programming: drivers, network programming, hardware registers.
- Dangerous because it can violate alignment, type safety, and lead to undefined behavior.
- Endianness matters when exchanging raw bytes between systems.

Object slicing:
- Happens when a derived object is assigned by value to a base object.
- The derived part is cut off, leaving only the base part.
- Example: Base b = derivedObj;
- Slicing loses polymorphism and derived-specific data.
- Avoid by using pointers or references to base class.

Pointers and references:
- Prevent slicing because only the address is copied, not the object.
- Allow polymorphism when virtual functions are used.
- Upcast with pointers: safe.
- Downcast with static_cast: unsafe without check.
- Downcast with dynamic_cast: safe, returns nullptr on failure.

Virtual functions:
- Enable runtime polymorphism.
- Required for dynamic_cast to work.
- Without virtual, dynamic_cast fails to compile ("Base class is not polymorphic").
- With virtual, the correct function is called based on the actual object type.

RTTI (Run-Time Type Information):
- Mechanism that stores type information for polymorphic classes.
- Used by dynamic_cast to determine the actual type at runtime.
- Enabled by having at least one virtual function in the class.
- Adds a small overhead to objects (vptr).

Endianness:
- Little endian: least significant byte first (x86, ARM).
- Big endian: most significant byte first (network protocols, some mainframes).
- reinterpret_cast on raw bytes can give wrong results if endianness differs.
- When sending data over network, use byte-swapping functions or serialization.

Dangers of reinterpret_cast:
- If you cast a bool* to int*, you read 4 bytes instead of 1, getting garbage.
- If data layout changes (e.g., struct member order), reinterpret_cast reads wrong values.
- Misalignment can cause crashes on some architectures.
- No type safety, no bounds checking.

Best practices:
- Use static_cast for upcasting and numeric conversions.
- Use dynamic_cast for safe downcasting when you are unsure of the actual type.
- Avoid reinterpret_cast unless absolutely necessary (low-level, hardware).
- Use serialization libraries (Protobuf, JSON) for data exchange.
- Use static_assert to enforce sizes and layouts at compile time.
- Prefer pointers/references over values to avoid slicing.

Comparison of casts:

static_cast:
- Checked at: compile-time
- Class relation required: yes (inheritance)
- Data conversion: yes, if needed
- Safety: medium (programmer must be sure)

dynamic_cast:
- Checked at: run-time
- Class relation required: yes (inheritance + virtual function)
- Data conversion: no
- Safety: safe (returns nullptr on failure)

reinterpret_cast:
- Checked at: none
- Class relation required: no
- Data conversion: no (just reinterprets bits)
- Safety: dangerous (undefined behavior possible)

Interview traps:
- static_cast cannot convert Base object to Derived object.
- dynamic_cast requires polymorphic base (virtual function).
- reinterpret_cast does not change data, only interpretation.
- Object slicing occurs with value assignment, not with pointers/references.
- Endianness affects reinterpret_cast when exchanging raw bytes.
- Always check dynamic_cast result for nullptr before use.

One-line summary:
static_cast is compile-time and fast but unsafe for downcasting. dynamic_cast is runtime and safe but requires virtual functions. reinterpret_cast is the most dangerous, reinterpreting bits without checks. Use pointers/references to avoid slicing. Always prefer safer casts and use serialization for data exchange.

61. FUNCTION OBJECTS AND OPERATOR OVERLOADING

Function Object (Functor)

- A function object is an object of a class that overloads operator().
- It can be called like a function.
- Also called a functor.

Example:

    struct Value {
        int m_result{0};

        int operator()(int res) {
            m_result = res;
            return res;
        }
    };

    int main() {
        Value v;
        v(42);   // calls v.operator()(42)
        std::cout << v.m_result << std::endl;  // 42
    }

Key points:

- operator() is the function call operator.
- When you write v(42), the compiler calls v.operator()(42).
- Unlike a regular function, a functor can store state.
- Here, m_result stores the last value passed.
- Functors are often used with STL algorithms like sort, for_each, transform.

Why use functors instead of functions:

- They can hold state between calls.
- They can be passed to templates and inlined by compiler.
- They work well with STL algorithms.
- They can be customized per instance.

Operator< for Custom Sorting

- std::sort uses operator< by default.
- To sort custom types, overload operator<.
- The comparison should define a strict weak ordering.

Example:

    struct Goblin {
        int health;
        Goblin(int h) : health(h) {}

        bool operator<(const Goblin& rhs) const {
            return this->health < rhs.health;
        }
    };

    int main() {
        std::vector<Goblin> goblin{Goblin(5), Goblin(3), Goblin(100)};
        std::sort(std::begin(goblin), std::end(goblin));

        for (auto g : goblin) {
            std::cout << g.health << std::endl;
        }
    }

Output (sorted by health):

    3
    5
    100

Key points:

- operator< takes a const reference to another object.
- Returns true if this object is less than rhs.
- Must be declared const because sorting does not modify objects being compared.
- std::sort uses this operator to compare and reorder elements.
- Without operator<, std::sort fails to compile for custom types.

Interview traps:

- operator() makes an object callable.
- operator< is used by std::sort by default.
- Both are member functions, not free functions.
- operator() can be overloaded with different parameter lists.
- operator< must satisfy strict weak ordering: no self-comparison true, transitive, etc.
- A functor can have state. A free function cannot.
- Lambdas are syntactic sugar for functors.

Core rule:

- Functor: class with operator().
- v(42) is v.operator()(42).
- State can be stored in the functor.
- std::sort uses operator< by default.
- Overload operator< to sort custom types.

One-line summary:

A function object (functor) is a class with operator() overloaded. It can be called like a function and can store state. std::sort uses operator< by default, so overload operator< to sort custom types. Both operator() and operator< are member functions. Lambdas are shorthand for functors.

63. CAPTURING THIS IN LAMBDAS

The problem:
- Inside a member function, accessing a data member like counter means this->counter.
- A lambda with empty capture list cannot access this.
- Compiler error: 'this' cannot be implicitly captured in this context.

Code that fails:

    struct MyStruct {
        void fun() {
            auto f = []() {
                std::cout << counter << std::endl; // error
            };
        }
        int counter{0};
    };

Error:

    'this' cannot be implicitly captured in this context
    note: explicitly capture 'this'

How to fix:

Fix 1: Capture this explicitly.

    auto f = [this]() {
        std::cout << counter << std::endl;
    };

- this is captured as a pointer.
- The lambda can access all members through this.
- If the object is destroyed before the lambda is called, this becomes dangling.

Fix 2: Capture by copy in C++17 and earlier.

    auto f = [=]() {
        std::cout << counter << std::endl;
    };

- In C++17, [=] implicitly captures this.
- In C++20 and later, [=] does NOT implicitly capture this.
- Deprecated in C++20. Use [this] instead.

Fix 3: In C++20 and later, use [this] or [&, this].

Key points:
- Inside a member function, data members are accessed through this.
- Lambdas need this captured to access members.
- Use [this] to capture the pointer.
- The captured this points to the enclosing object.
- If the lambda outlives the object, this becomes dangling.
- In C++20, [=] no longer captures this implicitly. Use [this] instead.
- [&] also does not capture this implicitly in C++20.

Danger:
- Capturing this and calling the lambda after the object is destroyed gives undefined behavior.
- This is a common source of bugs in async or callback code.

Alternative:
- Capture individual members by copy:

    auto f = [counter = counter]() {
        std::cout << counter << std::endl;
    };

- This copies the value at capture time.
- The lambda does not depend on this.
- Safe if the object is destroyed, because the copy is independent.

Interview traps:
- [=] no longer captures this in C++20.
- [this] captures the pointer, not a copy of the object.
- Capturing this can lead to dangling this if the object is destroyed.
- Capture individual members by copy to avoid dangling this.
- Lambdas in member functions often need this or explicit capture.

Core rule:
- Inside member functions, lambdas need this captured to access members.
- Use [this] to capture this explicitly.
- [=] worked in C++17 but is deprecated in C++20.
- Capturing this can cause dangling this.
- Prefer capturing individual members by copy when possible.

One-line summary:
Lambdas inside member functions need access to this to reach data members. Use [this] to capture this explicitly. In C++20, [=] no longer captures this. Capturing this can lead to dangling pointers if the object is destroyed. To be safe, capture individual members by copy.

67. CAPTURING THIS IN LAMBDAS — SUMMARY AND EXPLICIT CAPTURE

Capture types overview:

- [this]: captures the this pointer.
- [*this]: captures a copy of the enclosing object.
- [&]: captures all used local variables by reference. In member functions, it also captures this implicitly by reference.
- [=]: captures all used local variables by copy. In member functions, it used to capture this implicitly by copy, but this is deprecated in C++20.

Behavior of [this]:

- Modifies the original object.
- counter++ affects the real member.
- Each call sees the updated value.
- Example output for three calls: 1, 2, 3.
- Dangerous if the lambda outlives the object: this becomes dangling.

Behavior of [*this]:

- Captures a copy of the object at lambda creation time.
- Modifications affect only the copy inside the lambda.
- The original object is unchanged.
- Without mutable, the copy is read-only. counter++ would give a compile error.
- With mutable, the copy can be modified.
- Each fun() call creates a fresh copy, so output repeats.
- Example with mutable: inside lambda counter becomes 1, original remains 0. Output: 1, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0.
- Example without mutation (counter++ commented): output 0, 0.
- Safe from dangling this because it owns its own copy.

Behavior of [&]:

- Captures this implicitly by reference.
- Modifies the original object.
- Output identical to [this]: 1, 2, 3.
- Lifetime concerns same as [this].

Behavior of [=] and C++20 deprecation:

- In C++17, [=] captured this implicitly by copy (so modifications affected the copy, not the original).
- In C++20, implicit capture of this with [=] is deprecated.
- Compiler warning: implicit capture of 'this' with a capture default of '=' is deprecated.
- The fix is to be explicit:
    [=, this]  captures all locals by copy and this by pointer.
    [=, *this] captures all locals by copy and *this by copy.
    [&, this]  captures all locals by reference and this by pointer.
- Without explicit capture, the code still compiles but warns.

Comparison table (plain text):

Capture form | Modifies original? | Safe if object destroyed? | C++20 status
[this]       | Yes                | No                        | OK
[*this]      | No (modifies copy) | Yes                       | OK (C++17)
[&]          | Yes                | No                        | OK
[=]          | Depends            | No                        | Deprecated for this

- [=] is deprecated because it was ambiguous: did it capture this or *this? C++20 clarifies: use [=, this] or [=, *this].

Interview traps:

- [this] captures the pointer, not a copy. Modifications affect the original.
- [*this] captures a copy. Modifications affect only the copy. Needs mutable to modify.
- [&] captures this by reference. Modifications affect the original. Dangerous if object destroyed.
- [=] in C++20 no longer implicitly captures this. Use [=, this] explicitly.
- Capturing this can lead to dangling pointers if the lambda outlives the object.
- [*this] is safer but has different semantics (independent copy).
- Always prefer explicit captures for clarity.
- Remember that in member functions, member access implies this->member.

Core rule:

- [this]: shared state, modifies original, lifetime risk.
- [*this]: independent copy, no modification to original, safe from dangling.
- [&]: shared state, modifies original, lifetime risk.
- [=]: in C++20, capture this explicitly with [=, this] or [=, *this].
- Choose based on whether you want shared state or independent copy, and whether the lambda may outlive the object.

One-line summary:
Capturing this in lambdas can be done with [this], [*this], [&], or [=, this]. [this] and [&] modify the original and can dangle if the object dies. [*this] copies the object and is safe but modifications stay in the copy. In C++20, [=] no longer implicitly captures this; be explicit with [=, this] or [=, *this]. Choose based on desired semantics and lifetime safety.

69. ENVIRONMENT VARIABLES AND COMMAND-LINE ARGUMENTS

Code:

    #include <iostream>

    int main(int argc, char* argv[], char* env[]) {
        std::cout << "argc " << argc << std::endl;

        for (size_t i = 0; i < argc; i++) {
            std::cout << "[" << i << "]" << std::endl;
        }

        std::cout << "\nenv " << *env << std::endl;
        std::cout << std::getenv("PATH") << std::endl;

        int i = 0;
        for (;;) {
            std::cout << env[i++];
            if (env[i] == nullptr) {
                break;
            }
            break;
        }
        return 0;
    }

Command and output:

    ./program_arg "some string"
    argc 2
    [0]
    [1]

    env COLORTERM=truecolor
    /opt/anaconda3/bin:/opt/anaconda3/condabin:/Users/...:/Applications/Warp.app/Contents/Resources/bin
    COLORTERM=truecolor%

Observations:

Command-line arguments:
- argc = 2.
- argv[0] = "./program_arg" (program name).
- argv[1] = "some string" (first argument).
- Loop prints indices only, not values.

Environment variables:
- *env is the first environment variable: COLORTERM=truecolor.
- env[0] and *env are the same.
- std::getenv("PATH") returns the PATH environment variable's value.
- std::getenv is from <cstdlib>.

Loop bug in the code:
    int i = 0;
    for (;;) {
        std::cout << env[i++];     // prints env[0], i becomes 1
        if (env[i] == nullptr) {   // checks env[1]
            break;
        }
        break;                      // unconditional break
    }
- Prints env[0] only.
- The unconditional break at the end stops after one iteration.
- So only COLORTERM=truecolor is printed.
- No newline after the print, so the next output runs into it. The shell shows % because of missing newline.

Key points about argc and argv:
- argc is the number of command-line arguments including the program name.
- argv is an array of C-style strings.
- argv[0] is the program name.
- argv[1] to argv[argc-1] are the actual arguments.
- argv[argc] is guaranteed to be nullptr.
- The last element is nullptr, which allows loops to detect the end.

Key points about env:
- env is a non-standard third parameter to main.
- Supported by GCC and Clang on Unix-like systems.
- Not part of the C++ standard.
- env is an array of C-style strings in NAME=VALUE format.
- The array is terminated by nullptr.
- Use getenv("NAME") for portable access.

Portable environment variable access:

    #include <cstdlib>

    const char* path = std::getenv("PATH");
    if (path != nullptr) {
        std::cout << "PATH = " << path << std::endl;
    }

- std::getenv returns nullptr if the variable is not found.
- std::getenv returns a pointer to the value. Do not modify it.
- To list all environment variables portably, use extern char** environ; (also non-standard but widely supported).

Interview traps:
- argv[0] is the program name, not necessarily the full path.
- argv[argc] is nullptr. This is guaranteed.
- env is non-standard. Use getenv() for portable code.
- argc is always at least 1 (the program name).
- Command-line arguments are C-style strings, not std::string.
- env[i] is char*, not std::string.
- Modifying env or argv is allowed but rarely needed.
- getenv returns a pointer to internal memory. Do not free it.
- Env variables can be modified at runtime with setenv or unsetenv on POSIX systems.

Core rule:
- argc and argv are standard for command-line arguments.
- env is a non-standard extension for environment variables.
- Use getenv() for portable environment variable access.
- argv[argc] is nullptr. Loop until you hit nullptr to iterate all arguments.
- env[i] is a C-style string. Terminated by nullptr at end of list.

One-line summary:
main receives command-line arguments via argc and argv. argc is the count, argv[0] is the program name, argv[argc] is nullptr. Environment variables can be accessed via the non-standard env parameter or portably via std::getenv. The env array is terminated by nullptr. Use std::getenv for portable access to individual variables.

70. PREPROCESSOR AND g++ -E

What is the preprocessor:

- The preprocessor is the first stage of compilation.
- It runs before the compiler.
- It handles preprocessor directives: #include, #define, #ifdef, #ifndef, #endif, #pragma, etc.
- It performs textual substitution.
- It does not understand C++ syntax. It only processes text.

Command:

    g++ -E main.cpp

What -E does:

- Tells g++ to run only the preprocessor.
- Stops before compilation, assembly, and linking.
- Outputs the preprocessed source to standard output.
- The output is the original source after all preprocessor directives have been applied.

What the output contains:

- All #include files expanded inline.
- All macros expanded.
- All comments removed.
- All #if/#ifdef branches resolved and inactive branches removed.
- Line markers like # 1 "main.cpp" and # 1 "/usr/include/..." for debugging.
- Line markers tell the compiler where each line came from originally.

Example line markers:

    # 1 "main.cpp"
    # 1 "<built-in>"
    # 1 "<command-line>"
    # 1 "/usr/include/stdc-predef.h" 1 3 4
    # 1 "main.cpp" 2

Common uses:

- See what the compiler actually sees.
- Debug macro expansions.
- Check which headers are included.
- Inspect template instantiation dependencies.
- Understand error messages that reference generated code.
- Check conditional compilation results.

Variations:

- g++ -E main.cpp              output to standard output
- g++ -E main.cpp > out.i      save preprocessed output to file
- g++ -E -P main.cpp           omit line markers
- g++ -E -dM main.cpp          output only #define macros
- g++ -E -dD main.cpp          output macros and normal output
- g++ -E -C main.cpp           keep comments (opposite of default)

Related compiler flags for stages:

- -E: preprocess only
- -S: compile to assembly (.s)
- -c: compile to object file (.o)
- (no flag): compile and link to executable

Key points:

- Preprocessor is textual. It does not know types, functions, or classes.
- #include literally pastes the header file content.
- #define replaces text.
- Macros can be dangerous because they do not respect scopes or types.
- Prefer const, constexpr, inline, and templates over macros.
- The preprocessed output is usually very large because headers include many other headers.
- The -E flag is useful for debugging build issues and understanding header dependencies.

Interview traps:

- -E runs only the preprocessor. No compilation occurs.
- Preprocessor does not understand C++.
- #include is not a linker or loader. It is textual inclusion.
- Line markers in output are for error reporting, not part of the program.
- Macros are not type-safe.
- Modern C++ prefers constexpr, inline, and templates over macros.
- Use -E to see macro expansion and header inclusion.

Core rule:

- Preprocessor runs first.
- -E stops after preprocessing.
- Output is expanded source code.
- Use -P to suppress line markers.
- Use -dM to see only macros.
- Prefer modern C++ features over macros when possible.

One-line summary:
g++ -E runs only the preprocessor. It expands #include, #define, and conditional directives. The output shows what the compiler actually sees, with all headers pasted in and all macros expanded. Line markers show original file and line numbers. Use -P to remove line markers and -dM to list only macros. Preprocessing is textual and does not understand C++ syntax. Prefer constexpr, inline, and templates over macros in modern C++.

72. STD::SOURCE_LOCATION

What is std::source_location:
- C++20 feature.
- Defined in header <source_location>.
- Represents information about the source code location.
- Captures file name, function name, line number, and column number.
- Evaluated at compile time.

Basic usage:

    #include <iostream>
    #include <source_location>

    int add(int a, int b) {
        std::source_location location = std::source_location::current();
        std::cout << location.line() << std::endl;
        return a + b;
    }

Key methods:

- location.file_name() returns the source file name.
- location.function_name() returns the function name.
- location.line() returns the line number.
- location.column() returns the column number.

How it works:

- source_location::current() is evaluated at compile time.
- The compiler generates static data containing file name, function name, line, and column.
- The source_location object holds a pointer to this static data.
- No runtime overhead to capture the location. It is baked into the binary.
- line() dereferences the internal pointer to get the line number.
- If the pointer is null, line() returns 0.

Assembly observations:

- The source_location object is stored on the stack.
- It is initialized with a pointer to a static data section (.data).
- line() is a simple function:
    - Reads the internal pointer.
    - If null, returns 0.
    - Otherwise reads the line number at offset 0x10 in the pointed-to structure.
- No runtime string operations. All data is in the binary.

Use cases:

- Logging with automatic file and line information.
- Assertion macros with location.
- Error reporting.
- Debugging helpers.
- Replacing __FILE__ and __LINE__ macros.

Comparison with __FILE__ and __LINE__:

- __FILE__ and __LINE__ are preprocessor macros.
- std::source_location is a proper C++ type.
- source_location can be passed as a function argument, stored, and copied.
- __FILE__ and __LINE__ are text substitutions.
- source_location is type-safe.
- source_location also gives column and function name.
- source_location::current() default argument captures the caller's location.

Example with default argument:

    void log(const std::string& msg,
             const std::source_location& loc = std::source_location::current()) {
        std::cout << loc.file_name() << ":" << loc.line()
                  << " " << msg << std::endl;
    }

    int main() {
        log("hello");   // captures main.cpp and line where log is called
    }

- When source_location::current() is used as a default argument, it captures the call site.
- This is a powerful feature: the caller's file and line are automatically recorded.

Key points:

- source_location is a small object. Usually a single pointer.
- It is trivially copyable.
- It is constexpr.
- Use it instead of __FILE__, __LINE__, __func__ in modern C++.
- Available in C++20 and later.

Interview traps:

- source_location::current() must be called at the location you want to capture.
- If used as a default argument, it captures the caller's location.
- If stored and passed around, it keeps the original location.
- line() returns unsigned int (32-bit).
- column() returns unsigned int.
- The object is small and cheap to pass.
- source_location does not dynamically allocate.
- Not available before C++20. Use __FILE__ and __LINE__ for older standards.

Core rule:

- source_location::current() captures the current location at compile time.
- Evaluated at compile time, no runtime cost.
- Provides file, function, line, and column.
- Use as default argument to capture call site.
- Replaces __FILE__ and __LINE__ in modern C++.
- Requires C++20.

One-line summary:
std::source_location is a C++20 type that captures file name, function name, line, and column at compile time. source_location::current() captures the location where it is called. When used as a default argument, it captures the caller's location. The compiler bakes the data into the binary. No runtime overhead. Use it instead of __FILE__ and __LINE__ for type-safe, modern location tracking in logging, assertions, and error reporting.

73. STATIC DATA MEMBERS, INLINE, AND CONSTEXPR

The linker error:

    Undefined symbols for architecture arm64:
      "Struct::someVal", referenced from _main

This happens when a static data member is declared inside a class but not defined.

Variations of static data members:

1. Declaration only (no definition, no initializer):

    struct Struct {
        static const int someVal;   // declaration
    };

- Error if used: linker cannot find the symbol.

2. const static integral with in-class initializer:

    struct Struct {
        static const int someVal = 8;
    };

- Compiles and works if only used as a constant.
- If you take its address or bind to a reference (ODR-used), linker may still fail.
- Pre-C++17: needs out-of-line definition in exactly one .cpp file.
- C++17 and later: still okay, but if ODR-used, needs definition unless it is inline.

3. Non-const static with in-class initializer:

    struct Struct {
        static int someVal = 8;   // error
    };

- Error: non-const static data member must be initialized out of line.

4. Correct way for non-const static (pre-C++17):

    // header
    struct Struct {
        static int someVal;   // declaration
    };

    // one .cpp file
    int Struct::someVal = 63;   // definition

- This allocates storage and links correctly.

5. inline static (C++17 and later):

    struct Struct {
        inline static int someVal = 63;
    };

- inline keyword allows in-class initialization for non-const static members.
- No out-of-line definition needed.
- The linker merges definitions across translation units.

6. constexpr static (C++17 and later):

    struct Struct {
        static constexpr int someVal = 63;
    };

- constexpr implies const.
- In C++17 and later, constexpr static members are implicitly inline.
