- No out-of-line definition needed.
- Can be used as a constant expression.

Key rules:

- A static data member must be defined exactly once if it is ODR-used.
- ODR-used means its address is taken, a reference is bound, or it is used in a context that requires an lvalue.
- Using it as a constant expression (e.g., in an array size) does not ODR-use it.
- Pre-C++17: static const integral members can be initialized in-class, but still need out-of-line definition if ODR-used.
- C++17 and later: inline variables solve the ODR problem for static data members.
- constexpr static members are implicitly inline in C++17 and later.
- Non-const static members cannot be initialized in-class unless they are inline.

Comparison table:

    static const int someVal;               // declaration only, needs definition if ODR-used
    static const int someVal = 8;           // in-class init, may need definition if ODR-used (pre-C++17)
    static int someVal = 8;                 // error, must be initialized out of line
    static int someVal; + int Struct::someVal = 63;  // correct pre-C++17
    inline static int someVal = 63;         // correct C++17, no out-of-line definition
    static constexpr int someVal = 63;      // correct C++17, implicitly inline

Observed outputs:

- With static const int someVal = 8; and using it only in cout, output: 8 SomeValue.
- With static int someVal; and out-of-line definition int Struct::someVal = 63;, output: 63 SomeValue.
- With inline static int someVal = 63;, output: 63 SomeValue.
- With static constexpr int someVal = 63;, output: 63 SomeValue.

Interview traps:

- static const integral members can be initialized in-class, but may still need out-of-line definition if ODR-used.
- In C++17, inline variables allow in-class initialization for non-const static members.
- In C++17, constexpr static members are implicitly inline.
- Non-const static members must be defined out of line (unless inline).
- The linker error for undefined static member is common and fixed by providing a definition.
- Use inline or constexpr in modern C++ to avoid separate definitions.

One-line summary:
Static data members need a definition if they are ODR-used. In-class initialization is allowed for const integral and constexpr static members. In C++17 and later, inline variables and implicitly inline constexpr static members remove the need for separate out-of-line definitions. For non-const static members, use inline (C++17) or provide an out-of-line definition.

74. STD::STRING_VIEW

What is std::string_view:

- A non-owning view over a sequence of characters.
- Introduced in C++17.
- Defined in header <string_view>.
- Just a pointer to the data plus a size.
- Does not own the data it points to.
- Does not allocate memory.
- Trivially copyable and cheap to pass.

Basic usage:

    #include <iostream>
    #include <string_view>

    void print_string(const std::string_view& param) {
        std::cout << param << std::endl;
    }

    int main() {
        const char* s = "This is really a long string...........";
        print_string(s);
        return 0;
    }

How it works:

- string_view stores two fields: a pointer and a size.
- Layout: size at offset 0, pointer at offset 8 (on 64-bit systems).
- Total size: 16 bytes.
- Constructing from a const char* calls char_traits<char>::length (which uses strlen) to compute the length.
- The pointer and the length are stored in the string_view object.

Assembly observations:

- In main:
    - The C-string literal is in .rodata.
    - A string_view object is constructed on the stack.
    - Constructor string_view(char const*) calls char_traits::length -> strlen.
    - Then stores size and pointer in the string_view object.
    - Calls print_string with the address of the string_view.
    - No destructor needed because string_view is trivially destructible.

- In print_string:
    - Reads size at offset 0 and pointer at offset 8.
    - Calls operator<< for string_view.
    - That operator calls string_view::size() and string_view::data().
    - Then calls __ostream_insert(stream, data, size) to write the characters.

Key differences from std::string:

std::string:
- Owns its data.
- May allocate heap memory for long strings.
- Constructing from const char* creates a temporary std::string.
- The temporary involves allocation, copying, and destruction.
- After the function call, the temporary is destroyed.
- Has overhead for construction and destruction.

std::string_view:
- Does not own its data.
- Does not allocate memory.
- Constructing from const char* just records pointer and length.
- No temporary std::string created.
- No destructor to call.
- Much cheaper for read-only scenarios.
- Passing string_view does not copy the string.

Assembly comparison:

For std::string version:
- Calls to _M_construct, _M_create, _M_dispose, ~basic_string.
- Heap allocation if the string is long.
- Temporary object created and destroyed.

For std::string_view version:
- Only calls char_traits::length (strlen).
- No _M_construct, _M_create, _M_dispose, ~basic_string.
- No heap allocation.
- No destructor.
- Trivially destructible.

What string_view avoids:

- Heap allocation for long strings.
- Copying the characters into a new buffer.
- Calling the destructor on a temporary.
- Constructing a std::string object.

When to use string_view:

- When you only need to read a string.
- When passing string data to functions that do not need to own it.
- When you want to avoid temporary std::string creation.
- When working with C-strings, std::string, and char buffers uniformly.

When NOT to use string_view:

- When the function needs to store the string beyond the call.
- When the function needs to modify the string.
- When the underlying data might be destroyed or modified while the view is in use.
- When you need a null-terminated string (string_view is not null-terminated in general).

Danger:

- string_view does not own the data.
- If the underlying data is destroyed, the string_view becomes dangling.
- Example:
    std::string_view sv;
    {
        std::string s = "hello";
        sv = s;
    }
    std::cout << sv;   // undefined behavior: s is destroyed
- Always ensure the underlying data outlives the string_view.

Conversion:

- std::string -> std::string_view: implicit.
- const char* -> std::string_view: implicit (calls strlen).
- std::string_view -> std::string: explicit (std::string(sv)).

Example with both:

    void process(const std::string_view sv) {
        std::cout << sv << std::endl;
    }

    int main() {
        std::string s = "hello";
        const char* cs = "world";
        process(s);     // no copy
        process(cs);    // no temporary std::string
    }

Key points:

- string_view is a non-owning view.
- Size is 16 bytes (pointer + size).
- Constructing from const char* calls strlen.
- No allocation, no copy, no destructor.
- Trivially destructible.
- Use for read-only string parameters.
- Beware of dangling views if the underlying data dies.
- Prefer string_view over const std::string& when you do not need ownership.

Interview traps:

- string_view does not own data.
- string_view is not null-terminated in general.
- Constructing from const char* calls strlen.
- string_view can dangle if the underlying data is destroyed.
- const std::string& can bind to a temporary; string_view does not extend lifetime.
- std::string_view is cheaper than const std::string& for function parameters.
- Use std::string when you need ownership, string_view when you need a view.

Core rule:

- string_view = pointer + size, non-owning.
- No allocation, no copy, no destructor.
- Use for read-only function parameters.
- Underlying data must outlive the view.
- Prefer string_view over const std::string& for read-only parameters.
- Prefer std::string when ownership is needed.

One-line summary:
std::string_view is a non-owning view over a character sequence. It stores a pointer and a size, and does not allocate or copy. Constructing from const char* calls strlen. Using string_view avoids temporary std::string creation and heap allocation. Use it for read-only string parameters when the underlying data outlives the view. Beware of dangling views.

75. ITERATOR INVALIDATION WITH VECTOR ERASE

Code:

    #include <iostream>
    #include <vector>

    int main() {
        std::vector<int> v = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6};

        for (std::vector<int>::iterator it = v.begin(); it < v.end(); it++) {
            std::cout << *it << std::endl;
        }

        std::cout << std::endl;

        for (auto x : v) {
            v.erase(v.begin());
            std::cout << x << std::endl;
        }

        return 0;
    }

Output:

    1
    2
    3
    4
    5
    6

    1
    3
    5
    6
    6
    6

Observations:

First loop (explicit iterator):
- Uses v.begin() and v.end() directly.
- No modification to the vector during iteration.
- Prints all 6 elements correctly: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6.
- This loop is safe.

Second loop (range-based for with erase):
- Range-based for uses internal iterators: begin() and end() captured at loop start.
- Inside loop, v.erase(v.begin()) removes the first element.
- This invalidates the internal iterator and changes the size.
- The loop's end iterator is from the original vector, so it does not match the new size.
- Result: iterator invalidation, undefined behavior.
- Output is corrupted: 1, 3, 5, 6, 6, 6.

Why the second output is 1, 3, 5, 6, 6, 6:

Step by step trace:
- Loop 1: x = v[0] = 1, then erase v[0]. Vector is now {2,3,4,5,6}.
- Loop 2: internal iterator advanced. Reads v[1] = 3 (2 was skipped). Then erase v[0]. Vector is now {3,4,5,6}.
- Loop 3: reads v[2] = 5 (4 was skipped). Erase v[0]. Vector is now {4,5,6}.
- Loop 4: reads v[3] = 6 (past end of vector). But iterator still within old bounds. Erase v[0]. Vector is now {5,6}.
- Loop 5: reads garbage or undefined. Prints 6 again.
- Loop 6: reads more garbage. Prints 6 again.

The exact behavior depends on the implementation. It is undefined behavior.

Key points:

- Range-based for loop captures begin() and end() once at the start.
- Modifying the container inside the loop invalidates iterators.
- erase() invalidates all iterators at or after the erased position.
- The internal iterator of the range-based for is invalidated.
- The internal end iterator may refer to memory that no longer belongs to the vector.
- Reading through invalid iterators is undefined behavior.
- Output may appear to work but is unreliable.

Safe ways to erase while iterating:

Method 1: Use erase-remove idiom.

    v.erase(std::remove(v.begin(), v.end(), value), v.end());

- remove moves elements to be kept to the front.
- erase removes the leftover elements at the end.
- No iterator invalidation issue.

Method 2: Use explicit iterator and erase's return value.

    for (auto it = v.begin(); it != v.end(); ) {
        if (condition) {
            it = v.erase(it);   // erase returns next valid iterator
        } else {
            ++it;
        }
    }

- erase returns an iterator to the next element.
- Do not increment it when erasing.

Method 3: Iterate backwards.

    for (auto it = v.rbegin(); it != v.rend(); ) {
        if (condition) {
            it = std::make_reverse_iterator(v.erase(std::next(it).base()));
        } else {
            ++it;
        }
    }

- Erasing from the back does not invalidate earlier iterators.

Interview traps:

- Range-based for does not protect against container modification.
- erase invalidates iterators.
- Range-based for internally uses begin() and end() that are captured once.
- Modifying size during iteration can cause out-of-bounds reads.
- Undefined behavior may produce "reasonable-looking" but wrong output.
- Use erase's return value or erase-remove idiom for safe removal.

Core rule:

- Do not modify a container's size while iterating with a range-based for.
- erase invalidates iterators at or after the erased position.
- Use explicit iterator with erase's return value for safe removal.
- Use erase-remove idiom for removing all elements matching a value.
- Undefined behavior can look like it works, but it is still wrong.

One-line summary:
Range-based for loops capture begin and end once. Erasing elements inside the loop invalidates the internal iterators, leading to undefined behavior. The output 1, 3, 5, 6, 6, 6 shows the corruption. Use erase's return value with an explicit iterator, or use the erase-remove idiom for safe element removal during iteration.

76. STD::LIST OPERATIONS

What is std::list:

- Doubly-linked list.
- Defined in header <list>.
- Elements are not stored contiguously in memory.
- Each element has pointers to previous and next element.
- Fast insertion and deletion anywhere in the list.
- No random access. You cannot use [] or at().
- Slower traversal than vector due to pointer chasing.
- No iterator invalidation on insertion or deletion (except the erased element).

Initialization and capacity:

    std::list<int> list1 = {10, 20, 30, 40};

- list1.empty() returns true if list has no elements.
- list1.size() returns number of elements.

Element access:

    list1.front()   // first element
    list1.back()    // last element

- No random access. Cannot use list1[2] or list1.at(2).
- To reach the nth element, use std::advance(it, n) with an iterator.

Iterators:

    for (auto it = list1.begin(); it != list1.end(); ++it) { }
    for (auto it = list1.rbegin(); it != list1.rend(); ++it) { }

- begin() points to first element.
- end() points past last element.
- rbegin() points to last element.
- rend() points before first element.
- Iterators are bidirectional. You can use ++ and --.

Modifiers:

    list1.push_front(5);       // add to front
    list1.push_back(50);       // add to back
    list1.emplace_back(60);    // construct in place at back
    list1.emplace_front(4);    // construct in place at front

- emplace_back constructs the element directly, avoiding a temporary.
- emplace_front likewise.

Insert:

    auto it = list1.begin();
    std::advance(it, 2);       // move iterator to 3rd position
    list1.insert(it, 15);      // insert 15 before position

- insert does not invalidate other iterators.
- Returns iterator to the inserted element.

Remove from ends:

    list1.pop_front();
    list1.pop_back();

- pop_front removes first element.
- pop_back removes last element.
- Both have no return value.

Erase from middle:

    it = list1.begin();
    std::advance(it, 1);
    list1.erase(it);           // erase element at it

- erase returns iterator to next element.
- Only the erased element's iterator is invalidated.

List-specific operations:

std::list provides special member functions that std::vector does not:

sort():

    list2.sort();

- Sorts the list in ascending order.
- std::sort does NOT work on std::list because std::sort requires random access iterators.
- Use list's own sort() member function.

unique():

    list2.unique();

- Removes consecutive duplicate elements.
- Keeps only the first of each consecutive run.
- Sort first if you want to remove all duplicates.

reverse():

    list2.reverse();

- Reverses the list in place.

remove(value):

    list2.remove(35);

- Removes all elements equal to 35.
- Uses operator== for comparison.

remove_if(predicate):

    list2.remove_if([](int n) { return n % 2 != 0; });

- Removes all elements for which the predicate returns true.
- Predicate can be a lambda, function, or functor.
- This is safe and efficient for lists.

splice():

    std::list<int> source = {100, 200, 300};
    std::list<int> dest = {1, 2, 3};
    auto dest_it = dest.begin();
    std::advance(dest_it, 1);
    dest.splice(dest_it, source);

- Moves elements from source into dest without copying.
- No memory allocation, no element copy.
- Only pointer adjustments.
- After splice, source becomes empty.
- Elements are inserted before dest_it.
- This is O(1) for the whole list.

merge():

    std::list<int> m1 = {10, 30, 50};
    std::list<int> m2 = {20, 40, 60};
    m1.merge(m2);

- Merges two sorted lists into one sorted list.
- m2 becomes empty.
- Elements are moved, not copied.
- Both lists must be sorted before merging.

clear():

    m1.clear();

- Removes all elements.
- Size becomes 0.

Comparison with std::vector:

std::vector:
- Contiguous memory.
- Random access ([] and at()).
- Fast push_back and pop_back.
- Slow insert and erase in the middle (shifts elements).
- Iterators invalidated on reallocation.
- std::sort works.

std::list:
- Non-contiguous memory.
- No random access.
- Fast insert and erase anywhere (O(1) if iterator is known).
- No iterator invalidation on insert/erase (except erased iterator).
- std::sort does not work. Use list::sort().
- Has splice, merge, remove, remove_if, unique, reverse.

Key points:

- std::list is a doubly-linked list.
- No random access. Use iterators and std::advance.
- Insert and erase do not invalidate other iterators.
- Use list's own sort, not std::sort.
- remove_if with a lambda is safe and efficient.
- splice moves elements between lists without copying.
- merge combines two sorted lists without copying.
- unique removes consecutive duplicates.
- Prefer vector unless you need frequent middle insertion/removal.

Interview traps:

- std::sort does not work on std::list. Use list::sort.
- std::list has no operator[] or at().
- Iterators are bidirectional, not random access.
- Insert and erase are O(1) if you have the iterator, but finding the position is O(n).
- splice is O(1) for the whole list.
- merge requires both lists to be sorted.
- remove and remove_if traverse the whole list: O(n).
- unique removes only consecutive duplicates.

Core rule:

- std::list = doubly-linked list.
- No random access. Use iterators.
- Use list's own sort, reverse, unique, remove, remove_if.
- Use splice and merge for efficient list-to-list operations.
- Prefer vector unless middle insertion/removal is frequent.

One-line summary:
std::list is a doubly-linked list with fast insertion and deletion anywhere, but no random access. It provides member functions like sort, unique, reverse, remove, remove_if, splice, and merge. std::sort does not work on lists; use list::sort. splice moves elements between lists without copying. merge combines two sorted lists. Use list when you need frequent middle insertion or removal, and vector when you need random access and cache-friendly traversal.

77. STD::SET OPERATIONS

What is std::set:
- Associative container that stores unique elements in sorted order.
- Defined in header <set>.
- Implemented as a balanced binary search tree, usually red-black tree.
- Elements are always sorted.
- No duplicates allowed.
- Fast lookup, insertion, deletion: O(log n).
- No random access. No operator[] or at().

Basic initialization:

    std::set<int> s1 {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6};

Insert:

    s1.insert(-4);

- Inserts -4 into the set.
- If element already exists, insert does nothing.
- Elements remain sorted after insert.

Iteration:

    for (const auto& x : s1) {
        std::cout << x << std::endl;
    }

- Iterates in sorted order.

count:

    s1.count(4);

- Returns 1 if element exists, 0 otherwise.
- For set, count is always 0 or 1.

contains (C++20):

    s1.contains(4);

- Returns true if element exists, false otherwise.
- Cleaner alternative to count.

find:

    auto found = s1.find(3);

- Returns iterator to element if found.
- Returns s1.end() if not found.

erase by iterator:

    if (found != s1.end()) {
        s1.erase(found);
    }

- Removes element at iterator.
- Only erased iterator is invalidated.

erase by value:

    s1.erase(3);

- Removes element with value 3.
- Returns number of elements removed (0 or 1 for set).

lower_bound and upper_bound:

    auto lower = s2.lower_bound(4);    // first element >= 4
    auto upper = s2.upper_bound(10);   // first element > 10

- lower_bound(x): first element not less than x.
- upper_bound(x): first element greater than x.

Range iteration:

    for (auto it = lower; it != upper; ++it) {
        std::cout << *it << ", ";
    }

- Prints elements in range [lower, upper).

erase range:

    s2.erase(lower, upper);

- Removes all elements in range [lower, upper).

merge:

    s1.merge(s2);

- Moves elements from s2 into s1.
- Duplicates not moved. They remain in s2.
- s1 remains sorted.
- Efficient operation.

Key points:
- set stores unique elements in sorted order.
- insert, find, erase are O(log n).
- count returns 0 or 1.
- contains returns true or false.
- lower_bound and upper_bound define ranges.
- erase by iterator, value, or range.
- merge moves elements, leaves duplicates behind.
- Iterators are bidirectional, not random access.
- Elements are const. Cannot modify in place.

Comparison with vector:
- vector has random access, no sorting, duplicates allowed.
- set has no random access, always sorted, no duplicates.

Comparison with unordered_set:
- set is sorted, O(log n).
- unordered_set is unsorted, O(1) average.

Interview traps:
- set does not allow duplicates. Use multiset for duplicates.
- count returns 0 or 1 for set.
- insert does not overwrite.
- lower_bound returns first element >= x.
- upper_bound returns first element > x.
- erase range is half-open [lower, upper).
- merge does not move duplicates.
- Iterators are bidirectional.
- Elements are const.

Core rule:
- set = sorted unique elements.
- O(log n) insert, find, erase.
- count returns 0 or 1.
- contains returns true or false.
- lower_bound and upper_bound define ranges.
- erase by value, iterator, or range.
- merge moves elements, leaves duplicates in source.

One-line summary:
std::set is a sorted associative container with unique elements. Insert, find, and erase are O(log n). Use count or contains to check existence. Use lower_bound and upper_bound for ranges. Erase by iterator, value, or range. Merge moves elements and leaves duplicates behind. Iterators are bidirectional. No random access. Elements are always sorted. Use unordered_set for faster lookup when ordering is not needed.


78. STD::PAIR AND STD::MAKE_PAIR

What is std::pair:

- A simple container holding two values of possibly different types.
- Defined in header <utility>.
- Has two public members: first and second.
- Member types: first_type and second_type.
- Two values are stored in a single object.
- Used heavily in maps, sets, and algorithms.

Basic usage:

    std::pair<int, std::string> p1(200, "OK");
    std::cout << p1.first << " " << p1.second << std::endl;

- p1.first is int with value 200.
- p1.second is std::string with value "OK".

Class Template Argument Deduction (CTAD):

    std::pair p2(404, std::string("Not Found"));

- C++17 allows CTAD. Types are deduced from constructor arguments.
- p2 becomes std::pair<int, std::string>.

Conversion constructor:

    mystd::pair<double, std::string> p3 = p1;

- A pair can be converted to another pair if the elements are convertible.
- int -> double, string -> string.

std::make_pair:

    auto newpair = std::make_pair(val, 10.0F);

- Creates a pair with types deduced from arguments.
- val is int, 10.0F is float.
- newpair is std::pair<int, float>.
- Returns by value.
- Uses decay to remove references and cv-qualifiers from the deduced types.

std::ref and std::reference_wrapper:

    int val = 100L;
    auto newpair = std::make_pair(std::ref(val), 10.0F);

- std::ref returns a std::reference_wrapper<int>.
- newpair is std::pair<std::reference_wrapper<int>, float>.
- newpair.first refers to val, not a copy.
- Changing val later affects newpair.first.

    val = 150L;
    std::cout << newpair.first << std::endl;   // prints 150

Without std::ref:

    auto newpair = std::make_pair(val, 10.0F);

- newpair.first is an int copy.
- Changing val later does NOT affect newpair.first.
- Output: 100.

Difference:

    std::make_pair(val, ...)       // copies val
    std::make_pair(std::ref(val), ...)  // refers to val

Accessing pair elements:

    std::get<0>(p)    // first
    std::get<1>(p)    // second
    p.first           // first
    p.second          // second

Access by type:

    std::get<float>(newpair);   // returns the float element

- std::get<T> works when T appears exactly once in the pair.
- If T appears more than once, std::get<T> is ambiguous and causes compile error.
- For pairs, T1 and T2 are usually different, so std::get<T> is often usable.

Comparing pairs:

    p1 == p2   // true if both first and second are equal
    p1 < p2    // lexicographic comparison: first first, then second

C++20 spaceship operator:

    p1 <=> p2  // returns std::strong_ordering, etc.
    // Compares first first, then second.

Pair in vector with emplace_back:

    using student_t = std::pair<int, std::string>;
    std::vector<student_t> students;

    for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
        students.emplace_back(i, "student" + std::to_string(i));
    }

    for (const auto& student : students) {
        std::cout << student.first << " " << student.second << "\n";
    }

- emplace_back forwards arguments directly to the pair constructor.
- Avoids creating a temporary pair, then copying into the vector.
- More efficient than push_back.
- Range-based for with const auto& avoids copying pairs.

Output:

    0 student0
    1 student1
    2 student2
    ...
    9 student9

Tuple interface:

- std::pair supports structured bindings:
    auto [id, name] = student;
- Uses std::tuple_size, std::tuple_element, and std::get under the hood.
- These can be specialized for custom pair-like types.

Custom pair-like types:

- You can define your own pair type and specialize:
    std::tuple_size<MyPair<T1,T2>>
    std::tuple_element<I, MyPair<T1,T2>>
    std::get<I>(MyPair<T1,T2>&)
- This enables structured bindings for your type.

C++20 std::formatter for custom pair:

    template <typename T1, typename T2, typename CharT>
    struct formatter<mystd::pair<T1, T2>, CharT> : formatter<basic_string<CharT>, CharT> {
        template <typename FormatContext>
        auto format(const mystd::pair<T1, T2>& p, FormatContext& ctx) const {
            return format_to(ctx.out(), "({}, {})", p.first, p.second);
        }
    };

- Allows std::format to print custom pair types.
- C++23 feature.

Key points:

- std::pair holds two values, accessible via first and second.
- std::make_pair deduces types and returns by value.
- std::ref wraps a variable in std::reference_wrapper so it is not copied.
- std::get<0>, std::get<1>, std::get<T> access pair elements.
- Comparison operators work lexicographically: first, then second.
- C++20 provides <=> for pairs.
- Structured bindings work with pairs: auto [a, b] = pair;
- emplace_back constructs pairs in place, avoiding temporaries.
- Pair is used in maps, multimaps, and algorithms like std::sort.

Interview traps:

- std::make_pair decays types. Use std::ref to keep a reference.
- std::get<T> fails if T appears twice in the pair.
- CTAD works with std::pair in C++17 and later.
- Pair comparison is lexicographic: first compared first, then second.
- emplace_back is more efficient than push_back for pairs.
- Structured bindings are the modern way to unpack pairs.

Core rule:

- pair = two values, first and second.
- make_pair = create a pair with deduced types.
- std::ref = avoid copying by wrapping in reference_wrapper.
- std::get<0> = first, std::get<1> = second, std::get<T> = element of type T.
- Comparison is lexicographic.
- Structured bindings unpack pairs.
- emplace_back avoids temporaries.

One-line summary:
std::pair is a simple two-element container with first and second members. std::make_pair deduces types and returns a pair by value. Use std::ref to keep a reference instead of a copy. Access elements by index with std::get<0>, std::get<1>, or by type with std::get<T>. Comparisons are lexicographic. C++20 adds <=>. Structured bindings like auto [a, b] = pair work out of the box. Use emplace_back to avoid temporary pairs. Custom pair-like types can support structured bindings by specializing tuple_size, tuple_element, and get.

79. STD::MAP REQUIREMENTS AND OPERATOR<

What std::map requires:

- Keys must be comparable with a strict weak ordering.
- By default, std::map uses std::less<Key>, which calls operator<.
- If operator< is not defined, std::map fails to compile.

Error observed:

    invalid operands to binary expression ('const Edge' and 'const Edge')

This happens when you use std::map<Edge, ...> without operator< for Edge.

Fixes:

Fix 1: Define operator< inside the class:

    bool operator<(const Edge& rhs) const {
        return v1 + v2 < rhs.v1 + rhs.v2;
    }

- The function must be const.
- It must define a strict weak ordering.

Fix 2: Use C++20 defaulted operator<=>:

    auto operator<=>(const Edge&) const = default;

- Generates all comparison operators automatically.
- Requires all members to be comparable.
- Must be inside the class.

Fix 3: Provide a custom comparator to std::map:

    struct EdgeCompare {
        bool operator()(const Edge& a, const Edge& b) const {
            if (a.v1 != b.v1) return a.v1 < b.v1;
            return a.v2 < b.v2;
        }
    };

    std::map<Edge, std::string, EdgeCompare> mymap2;

- Comparator is passed as the third template argument.
- Does not require modifying Edge.

Strict weak ordering rules:

A valid operator< must satisfy:

1. Irreflexivity: a < a is always false.
2. Asymmetry: if a < b is true, then b < a is false.
3. Transitivity: if a < b and b < c, then a < c.
4. Transitivity of equivalence: if a and b are equivalent and b and c are equivalent, then a and c are equivalent.

Problem with the observed operator<:

    bool operator<(const Edge& rhs) const {
        return v1 + v2 < rhs.v1 + rhs.v2;
    }

This is problematic:

- Edge(5, 0) and Edge(3, 2) both sum to 5.
- Neither is less than the other.
- So they are considered equivalent by std::map.
- If you insert Edge(5, 0) with key "a" and then insert Edge(3, 2) with key "b", the second insert finds the key as "equal" to the first, so it does not insert a new element.
- You lose one of the two edges.
- Worse: Edge(1, 4) and Edge(4, 1) are also considered equivalent.
- This means many distinct edges collapse into the same map key.

Correct operator<:

    bool operator<(const Edge& rhs) const {
        if (v1 != rhs.v1) return v1 < rhs.v1;
        return v2 < rhs.v2;
    }

- This is lexicographic comparison: first by v1, then by v2.
- Two edges are equivalent only when v1 and v2 are both equal.
- This is a proper strict weak ordering.
- No data loss in std::map.

Why the observed output still looks correct:

- In this specific test, all edges are of the form Edge(k, k).
- So v1 + v2 uniquely identifies each edge in the test.
- Edge(0,0) sum 0, Edge(1,1) sum 2, Edge(2,2) sum 4, Edge(3,3) sum 6.
- The sums are all distinct.
- So the test passes, but the operator< is still semantically wrong.

Key points:

- std::map keys need a strict weak ordering.
- By default, this comes from operator<.
- The operator< must give a total order over the keys (or at least an equivalence that matches logical equality).
- The observed operator< using v1 + v2 does not satisfy this for Edges with different v1, v2 but same sum.
- This is a classic bug that can cause data loss in std::map.
- Use lexicographic comparison or a proper hash-and-equality approach.

Interview traps:

- std::map<Key, Value> requires operator< or a custom comparator.
- operator< must be a strict weak ordering.
- Do not use sums or hashes as the only comparison.
- Sum-based comparison can collapse distinct keys.
- Prefer lexicographic comparison.
- Use operator<=> = default in C++20 for automatic correct comparison.

Core rule:

- std::map needs a strict weak ordering on keys.
- Use lexicographic comparison, not sums.
- Default operator<=> in C++20 gives a correct ordering.
- Custom comparators can be used as an alternative.
- Sum-based operator< is a bug, even if it works in limited tests.

One-line summary:
std::map requires a strict weak ordering on its keys. The default uses operator<. A sum-based operator< is a bug because it collapses distinct keys into the same equivalence class. Use lexicographic comparison or C++20 defaulted operator<=>. Custom comparators can also be used.

80. CUSTOM COMPARATORS VS OPERATOR< IN STD::MAP

Two ways to provide ordering for std::map keys:

1. Define operator< inside the key class.
2. Provide a separate comparator functor as the third template argument.

Example code:

    struct Edge {
        unsigned long v1;
        unsigned long v2;

        bool operator<(const Edge& rhs) const {
            return v1 + v2 < rhs.v1 + rhs.v2;
        }
    };

    struct EdgeCompare {
        bool operator()(const Edge& lhs, const Edge& rhs) const {
            return lhs.v1 + lhs.v2 < rhs.v1 + rhs.v2;
        }
    };

    std::map<Edge, std::string> mymap2;                     // uses Edge::operator<
    std::map<Edge, std::string, EdgeCompare> mymap3;        // uses EdgeCompare

Key points:

- std::map's default comparison is std::less<Key>, which calls operator< on the key.
- If operator< is defined inside the key type, std::map can use it directly.
- If you want a different comparison, pass a comparator as the third template argument.
- The comparator must be a callable that takes two keys and returns bool.
- The comparator must satisfy strict weak ordering.
- Common comparator forms: function pointer, functor, lambda (only for local usage, not as template argument before C++20).

Strict weak ordering rules:

1. Irreflexivity: comp(a, a) is false.
2. Asymmetry: if comp(a, b) is true, comp(b, a) is false.
3. Transitivity: if comp(a, b) and comp(b, c), then comp(a, c).
4. Transitivity of equivalence: if a and b are equivalent and b and c are equivalent, then a and c are equivalent.

Problem with sum-based comparison:

    return v1 + v2 < rhs.v1 + rhs.v2;

- Edge(1, 4) and Edge(4, 1) both sum to 5. Neither is less than the other. They are considered equivalent.
- Edge(3, 2) and Edge(5, 0) are also equivalent.
- Distinct edges collapse into the same key in std::map.
- This is a bug. It can cause data loss.

Correct comparison:

    return (v1 < rhs.v1) || (v1 == rhs.v1 && v2 < rhs.v2);

Or more simply:

    if (v1 != rhs.v1) return v1 < rhs.v1;
    return v2 < rhs.v2;

This is lexicographic comparison. It is a proper strict weak ordering.

When to use operator<:

- When there is a natural ordering for the key type.
- When you want std::map to work without extra template arguments.
- Example: std::map<int, std::string>, std::map<std::string, int>.
- When the ordering should be the default for all uses of the type.

When to use a custom comparator:

- When you want a different ordering from operator<.
- When you cannot modify the key type (e.g., it's from a library).
- When different maps need different orderings.
- When you want to avoid polluting the type's global operator<.

Comparator as functor:

    struct EdgeCompare {
        bool operator()(const Edge& lhs, const Edge& rhs) const {
            if (lhs.v1 != rhs.v1) return lhs.v1 < rhs.v1;
            return lhs.v2 < rhs.v2;
        }
    };

    std::map<Edge, std::string, EdgeCompare> mymap;

- EdgeCompare is a stateless functor.
- It is default-constructible and const-callable.
- std::map stores a copy of the comparator.
- The comparator must be copyable.

Comparator as lambda (C++20 and later):

    auto cmp = [](const Edge& a, const Edge& b) {
        return std::tie(a.v1, a.v2) < std::tie(b.v1, b.v2);
    };

    std::map<Edge, std::string, decltype(cmp)> mymap(cmp);

- Lambda can be used as a comparator.
- In C++20, you can use decltype(cmp) as the template argument.
- The lambda must be passed to the constructor.

Defaulted operator<=> (C++20):

    struct Edge {
        unsigned long v1;
        unsigned long v2;
        auto operator<=>(const Edge&) const = default;
    };

- Generates all comparison operators including <.
- Automatically correct lexicographic ordering.
- Simplest solution when members are comparable.

Key differences summary:

operator< inside class:
- Defined once for the type.
- Used by default in std::map.
- Cannot have multiple orderings.
- Simple and convenient.

Custom comparator:
- Defined separately.
- Passed as third template argument.
- Can have multiple orderings for the same type.
- Useful when the type's operator< is not suitable.
- Requires more boilerplate.

Interview traps:

- std::map requires a strict weak ordering.
- Sum-based comparison is a bug.
- operator< must be a const member function.
- Comparator must be a callable with operator()(const Key&, const Key&) const.
- Comparator must be copyable.
- In C++20, defaulted <=> is often the simplest correct choice.
- Do not use operator< for sorting if it doesn't define a true total order.

Core rule:

- std::map keys must have a strict weak ordering.
- Provide it via operator< or a custom comparator.
- Lexicographic comparison is correct. Sum-based comparison is wrong.
- Use operator< for natural ordering.
- Use custom comparator for alternative orderings or when you cannot modify the type.
- In C++20, defaulted operator<=> is the easiest correct solution.

One-line summary:
std::map needs a strict weak ordering on its keys. You can provide this via operator< inside the key class or via a custom comparator as the third template argument. Lexicographic comparison is correct; sum-based comparison is a bug that collapses distinct keys. Use operator< for a natural default, custom comparator for alternative orderings or unmodifiable types, and defaulted <=> in C++20 for the simplest correct ordering.

81. CUSTOM MULTIMAP IMPLEMENTATION

Overview:
- A custom_multimap class template was implemented from scratch.
- Mimics the behavior of std::multimap.
- Built using a Binary Search Tree (BST).

Data Structure:
- Nodes contain: value_type (pair), left, right, parent pointers.
- Root pointer, size, and insertion counter are tracked.
- Uses std::allocator_traits and rebind_alloc for node memory management.

Stable Ordering:
- std::multimap must keep equivalent keys in insertion order (C++11 and later).
- The custom map achieves this using an insertion_id counter.
- When two keys are equivalent, it compares insertion_id to keep the older insertion first.
- node_less function checks key comparison first, then insertion_id.

Custom Iterator:
- Implements a bidirectional iterator.
- Uses successor() and predecessor() helper functions for tree traversal.
- Handles end() safely by keeping a reference to the root.
- Increment and decrement operators move to the next or previous node.
- begin() returns the leftmost node. end() returns a null iterator with root reference.

Key Operations:
- insert: Traverses the tree, places the new node based on comparison, updates parent pointers.
- erase(key): Finds the key, then loops to erase all matching elements.
- erase(iterator): Removes a specific node. Uses transplant to reconnect left/right children to the parent.
- find(key): Traverses left even after finding a match to return the very first element with that key.
- count(key): Counts occurrences by traversing the tree.
- lower_bound(key): Finds the first element not less than key.
- upper_bound(key): Finds the first element greater than key.
- equal_range(key): Returns a pair of iterators (lower_bound and upper_bound).

Memory Management:
- clear(): Recursively destroys and deallocates all nodes.
- Destructor calls clear().
- Allocates nodes using the provided allocator rebound to the Node type.

Key Caveat:
- The custom implementation uses an unbalanced BST.
- If elements are inserted in sorted order, it degrades into a linked list.
- Operations become O(N) in the worst case.
- std::multimap uses a Red-Black tree, guaranteeing O(log N) time complexity.

Interview Traps:
- std::multimap does not allow modifying keys in place. The custom implementation reflects this.
- Stability of equivalent keys is guaranteed since C++11.
- The find() function must return the first matching element, not a random one.
- Unbalanced BST can cause performance issues (stack overflow on deep recursion, slow lookups).
- Custom allocators are complex. std::allocator_traits is the standard way to handle rebinding and construction.

One-line summary:
A custom_multimap was implemented using an unbalanced BST with an insertion_id counter for stable ordering, custom bidirectional iterators, and allocator-based memory management. It implements the full standard API including insert, erase, find, count, lower_bound, and upper_bound. The main limitation is the lack of tree balancing, which can degrade performance compared to std::multimap's Red-Black tree.

82. STD::UNORDERED_MAP OPERATIONS

Overview:
- std::unordered_map is an associative container that stores key-value pairs.
- Keys are unique.
- Uses a hash table, not a tree.
- Fast average lookup, insertion, and deletion: O(1).
- Worst case: O(N) if many hash collisions occur.
- Elements are NOT stored in any sorted order.
- Defined in header <unordered_map>.

Member Types:
- key_type: Type of the key (e.g., std::string).
- mapped_type: Type of the value (e.g., std::string).
- value_type: std::pair<const Key, T>.
- size_type: Unsigned integer type for sizes.
- iterator / const_iterator: Forward iterators.
- hasher: Hash function type.
- key_equal: Equality comparator type.
- allocator_type: Allocator type.

Constructors:
- Default constructor: unordered_map u1;
- Initializer list: unordered_map u = {{"RED", "#FF0000"}, {"GREEN", "#00FF00"}};
- Range constructor: unordered_map u2(u.begin(), u.end());
- Copy constructor: unordered_map u3 = u;
- Assignment operator: u1 = u;
- get_allocator(): Returns the allocator.

Iterators:
- begin(), end(): Forward iterators.
- cbegin(), cend(): Const forward iterators.
- Note: Unordered_map does not have reverse iterators (rbegin/rend) because there is no meaningful reverse order.

Capacity:
- empty(): Returns true if size is 0.
- size(): Number of elements.
- max_size(): Maximum possible number of elements.

Modifiers:
- insert(): Adds a key-value pair.
- insert_or_assign() (C++17): Inserts if key is new, otherwise assigns new value to existing key.
- emplace(): Constructs element in-place.
- emplace_hint(): Same as emplace but with an iterator hint (ignored in unordered_map, but present for API compatibility).
- try_emplace() (C++17): Inserts only if key does not exist. Does NOT overwrite existing value.
- extract() (C++17): Removes a node from the map and returns a node handle. Allows modifying the key and re-inserting without reallocation.
- merge() (C++17): Moves elements from another unordered_map into this one. Duplicates are not moved.
- erase(): Removes by iterator, key, or range.
- swap(): Swaps contents of two maps.
- clear(): Removes all elements.

Lookup:
- at(): Access value with bounds checking. Throws std::out_of_range if key not found.
- operator[]: Access value. If key does not exist, inserts a default-constructed value.
- count(): Returns 1 if key exists, 0 otherwise.
- find(): Returns iterator to element, or end() if not found.
- contains() (C++20): Returns true if key exists, false otherwise.
- equal_range(): Returns pair of iterators. Since keys are unique, the range will have size 0 or 1.

Bucket Interface:
- bucket_count(): Number of buckets in the hash table.
- max_bucket_count(): Maximum possible buckets.
- bucket(key): Returns the index of the bucket where the key would be placed.
- bucket_size(n): Number of elements in bucket n.
- begin(n), end(n), cbegin(n), cend(n): Iterators for a specific bucket.

Hash Policy:
- load_factor(): Average number of elements per bucket (size / bucket_count).
- max_load_factor(): Threshold. If load_factor exceeds this, rehashing occurs.
- rehash(n): Sets the number of buckets to at least n.
- reserve(n): Pre-allocates enough buckets for at least n elements.

Observers:
- hash_function(): Returns the hash function object.
- key_eq(): Returns the key equality comparator object.

Non-Member Functions:
- operator==: Compares two unordered_maps. Order does not matter, but contents must be equal.
- std::swap(u1, u2): Swaps two maps.
- std::erase_if(u, pred) (C++20): Erases all elements for which the predicate returns true.

Key Differences from std::map:
- std::map: Balanced binary search tree. Elements are sorted by key. Lookup is O(log N).
- std::unordered_map: Hash table. Elements are unordered. Average lookup is O(1).
- std::unordered_map requires a hash function (default: std::hash<Key>).
- std::map requires operator< (default: std::less<Key>).

Interview Traps:
- operator[] inserts a default value if key does not exist. at() throws an exception.
- unordered_map does not have reverse iterators.
- Iterators are forward only.
- Rehashing invalidates iterators, but references and pointers to elements remain valid (since nodes are not moved in memory, only the bucket array is rebuilt).
- extract() is useful for modifying keys without memory allocation.

One-line summary:
std::unordered_map is a hash table-based associative container with unique keys. It provides fast average O(1) lookup, insertion, and deletion. Key operations include insert, emplace, try_emplace, extract, merge, at, operator[], find, and count. It also provides bucket management functions for fine-tuning hash performance. Use unordered_map when performance is critical and ordering does not matter.

83. STD::UNORDERED_MULTIMAP OPERATIONS

What is std::unordered_multimap:
- A hash table based associative container.
- Allows multiple elements to have the same key (duplicate keys).
- Elements are NOT sorted.
- Average lookup, insertion, and deletion is O(1).
- Defined in the header unordered_map.

Differences from std::unordered_map:
- unordered_map allows only unique keys.
- unordered_multimap allows duplicate keys.
- Because keys are not unique, operator[] and at() are NOT available. You cannot use a key to retrieve a single mapped value.
- try_emplace and insert_or_assign are NOT available because they assume unique keys.

Member types and constructors:
- key_type: Type of the key.
- mapped_type: Type of the value.
- value_type: std::pair of const Key and T.
- Can be initialized with an initializer list containing duplicate keys:
    MapType m = { {"Apple", "Fruit"}, {"Apple", "Tech Company"} };
- Supports default, range, copy constructors, and assignment.
- get_allocator() returns the allocator.

Iterators:
- Forward iterators only.
- No reverse iterators, because the order is not meaningful.
- begin(), end(), cbegin(), cend().

Capacity:
- empty(): Returns true if the container has no elements.
- size(): Returns the number of elements.
- max_size(): Returns the maximum possible number of elements.

Modifiers:
- insert(): Adds a new element. Always succeeds because duplicates are allowed.
- insert_range() (C++23): Inserts a range of elements from another container.
- emplace(): Constructs the element in place.
- emplace_hint(): Uses a hint for insertion.
- extract() (C++17): Removes a node from the container and returns a node handle. You can modify the node and re-insert it without allocating new memory.
- merge() (C++17): Moves elements from another unordered_multimap into this one.
- erase(key): Removes ALL elements with the specified key.
- erase(iterator): Removes only the element pointed to by the iterator.
- swap(): Swaps contents with another container.
- clear(): Removes all elements.

Lookup:
- count(key): Returns the number of elements matching the key (can be greater than 1).
- find(key): Returns an iterator to the first matching element found. Since duplicates exist, it might not be the "first" in insertion order, but it points to one of the matching elements.
- contains(key) (C++20): Returns true if at least one matching element exists.
- equal_range(key): Returns a pair of iterators defining the range of elements matching the key. This is the most reliable way to iterate over all duplicate values for a specific key.
    auto range = m.equal_range("Apple");
    for (auto it = range.first; it != range.second; ++it) {
        std::cout << it->second << std::endl;
    }

Bucket Interface:
- bucket_count(): Number of buckets in the hash table.
- max_bucket_count(): Maximum possible buckets.
- bucket(key): Returns the index of the bucket where the key is or would be placed.
- bucket_size(n): Number of elements in bucket n.
- Local iterators: begin(n), end(n), cbegin(n), cend(n) iterate only through elements in a specific bucket.

Hash Policy:
- load_factor(): Average number of elements per bucket (size divided by bucket_count).
- max_load_factor(): The threshold that triggers rehashing.
- max_load_factor(float): Sets a new maximum load factor.
- rehash(n): Changes the number of buckets to at least n.
- reserve(n): Pre-allocates space for at least n elements.

Observers:
- hash_function(): Returns the hash function object.
- key_eq(): Returns the equality comparator object.

Non-member functions:
- operator==: Compares two unordered_multimaps. They are equal if they contain the same elements, regardless of order.
- std::swap(m1, m2): Swaps two maps.
- std::erase_if(m, predicate) (C++20): Erases all elements satisfying the predicate.

Interview traps:
- unordered_multimap does not have operator[] or at().
- count() can return values greater than 1.
- find() does not guarantee which of the duplicate keys it returns.
- equal_range() is the standard way to retrieve all elements for a specific key.
- Iterators are forward only. There are no reverse iterators.
- Rehashing invalidates iterators, but pointers and references to elements remain valid because the nodes are not moved in memory.

One-line summary:
std::unordered_multimap is a hash table container that allows duplicate keys. It provides O(1) average lookup and insertion. Use count(), find(), and equal_range() for lookup. It does not support operator[] or at(). Iterators are forward only. Use erase(key) to remove all duplicates of a key, or erase(iterator) to remove a single element. Use the bucket interface and hash policy to tune performance.

84. STD::STACK

What is std::stack:
- A container adaptor that provides a LIFO (Last-In, First-Out) data structure.
- It is not a standalone container; it wraps another underlying container.
- Defined in the <stack> header.
- The last element pushed is the first element popped.
- You can only access the top element. You cannot iterate through the stack.

Underlying Containers:
- The default underlying container is std::deque.
- It can be changed to std::vector or std::list by passing it as the second template argument.
- Example:
    std::stack<int> s1;                        // uses std::deque
    std::stack<int, std::vector<int>> s2;      // uses std::vector
    std::stack<int, std::list<int>> s3;        // uses std::list

Member Types:
- value_type: The type of elements (e.g., int).
- size_type: Unsigned integer type for sizes.
- reference: Reference to the element type.
- const_reference: Const reference to the element type.

Constructors:
- Default constructor: std::stack<int> s;
- Construct from underlying container:
    std::deque<int> d = {10, 20, 30};
    std::stack<int> s(d);
- Copy constructor: std::stack<int> s2 = s;
- Note: std::stack does NOT have a constructor that takes an initializer list directly.
- To initialize with values, you must pass an underlying container:
    std::stack<int> s({0, 9, 8, 7, 6}); // Works because {0,9,8,7,6} creates a deque.

Capacity:
- empty(): Returns true if the stack has no elements.
- size(): Returns the number of elements.

Element Access:
- top(): Returns a reference to the top element (the last element pushed).
- It is the only way to access elements. There is no operator[] or at().
- Calling top() on an empty stack is undefined behavior.

Modifiers:
- push(value): Adds an element to the top.
- emplace(args...): Constructs an element in-place at the top.
- push_range(range) (C++23): Pushes a range of elements onto the stack.
- pop(): Removes the top element. Returns void. Does not return the removed element.
- swap(other): Swaps the contents of two stacks.
- Note: To get the value and then remove it, you must do:
    int val = s.top();
    s.pop();

Non-member Functions and Operators:
- Comparison operators: ==, !=, <, <=, >, >= compare the underlying containers lexicographically.
- Spaceship operator (C++20): <=>
- std::swap(s1, s2): Swaps two stacks.
- std::uses_allocator: Checks if the stack uses an allocator.

Key Limitations:
- No iterators: You cannot use a range-based for loop or standard algorithms directly on a stack.
- No initializer list constructor: std::stack<int> s{1, 2, 3}; is an error. Use s({1, 2, 3}) or push them one by one.
- No random access: You cannot access elements in the middle.
- No reverse iterators.

Interview Traps:
- pop() returns void, not the removed element. You must call top() before pop() to get the value.
- std::stack is an adaptor, not a container.
- The underlying container is std::deque by default.
- Iterating through a stack requires popping elements, which empties the stack.
- Calling top() or pop() on an empty stack is undefined behavior.

Core Rule:
- LIFO: Last In, First Out.
- Only top(), push(), pop(), empty(), size().
- No iterators, no direct initializer list.
- Underlying container is std::deque by default.

One-line summary:
std::stack is a LIFO container adaptor. It wraps an underlying container (default std::deque) and restricts access to the top element. It provides push, emplace, pop, top, size, and empty. It has no iterators and cannot be initialized directly with an initializer list. Use top() to read the value and pop() to remove it.

STD::STACK - DEEP DIVE AND INTERVIEW TRAPS

What is std::stack (Broad Perspective):

It is a Container Adaptor, not a container itself.

It does not own its own memory management logic; it relies entirely on the underlying container.

It restricts the interface of the underlying container to only allow LIFO (Last-In, First-Out) operations.

TWIST 1: WHY DOES POP() RETURN VOID? (Exception Safety)

This is the most common interview question.

Students often wonder why pop() does not return the removed element.

If pop() returned the value by value (e.g., T pop();), it would need to make a copy of the top element before removing it.

If that copy constructor throws an exception, the element would be removed from the stack but lost during the copy process (data loss).

By separating top() and pop(), C++ guarantees exception safety.

You can safely read the top element, do whatever you need, and if it succeeds, then call pop() to remove it.

TWIST 2: THE UNDERLYING CONTAINER WARS (Deque vs Vector vs List)

By default, std::stack uses std::deque.

Why deque? Because it grows efficiently at the ends without invalidating references to existing elements (it allocates in chunks). It provides O(1) push and pop at the back.

What if you use std::vector? You can: std::stack<int, std::vector<int>> s;

Vector vs Deque for Stack: Vector has better cache locality (contiguous memory) and might be faster for smaller stacks. But when a vector grows, it reallocates and copies/moves all elements to a new memory location.

What if you use std::list? You can, but it is terrible for a stack. It has pointer overhead and terrible cache locality. Never use list for a stack unless you have a very specific memory constraint.

Interview Answer: Choose std::vector for small, fast stacks. Choose std::deque (default) for large or unpredictable stacks to avoid reallocation costs.

TWIST 3: THE ITERATION PROBLEM (How to print/iterate a stack?)

std::stack has no iterators. You cannot use a range-based for loop.

Question: How do you print all elements of a stack without destroying it?

Approach 1: Pop everything into another stack and print. (Destroys original order, requires O(N) extra space).

Approach 2: Make a copy of the stack. std::stack<int> temp = original; Then pop temp until empty.

Approach 3: Inherit from std::stack and expose the protected container member (c). (Not recommended, but shows deep knowledge).

TWIST 4: MEMORY MANAGEMENT (Call Stack vs std::stack)

Interviewers often confuse the Call Stack (memory used by function calls) with std::stack (the C++ data structure).

The C++ std::stack is allocated on the heap (usually via deque), whereas the Call Stack is a fixed-size memory region managed by the OS.

Stack Overflow (the error, not the website): If a recursive function is called infinitely, the Call Stack fills up and crashes the program. std::stack can grow until system memory is exhausted (heap exhaustion).

TWIST 5: THE MONOTONIC STACK PATTERN (Algorithmic Gold)

In coding interviews (like LeetCode), std::stack is heavily used to solve O(N) problems that look like O(N^2).

Examples: Next Greater Element, Daily Temperatures, Largest Rectangle in Histogram.

The idea is to maintain a stack where elements are always sorted (monotonic). When a new element comes, you pop elements that violate the order.

If an interviewer asks about stack, they likely want to see this pattern.

TWIST 6: COMPLEXITY AND SPACE

Push, Pop, Top, Empty: All are O(1) time complexity.

Search: O(N) (because you have to pop everything).

Space: O(N) based on the number of elements.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (SUMMARY):

Calling top() or pop() on an empty stack is Undefined Behavior. It will crash your program. Always check !st.empty() first.

std::stack does not allow initializer lists. st = {1, 2, 3}; is wrong. Use st({1, 2, 3}); or push elements manually.

std::stack is not thread-safe. If two threads push/pop simultaneously, you need a std::mutex.

The pop() function returns void. If you need the value, you must save top() first: int val = st.top(); st.pop();

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):

Undo/Redo mechanisms in editors.

Browser back/forward history.

Expression evaluation (Infix to Postfix conversion in compilers).

Backtracking algorithms (Solving mazes, N-Queens, Sudoku).

Parsing syntax (e.g., checking if HTML tags or parentheses are balanced).

Core Rule for Interviews:
A stack is not just a data structure; it is a concept of "Reversing order" and "Backtracking". If a problem asks you to look at the most recent element or match pairs, think of a stack immediately.

Here is the complete deep dive into std::queue, including all previous observations, twists, and interview traps, written in plain English.

85. STD::QUEUE - COMPLETE DEEP DIVE, OBSERVATIONS, AND INTERVIEW TRAPS

Basic Definition:
- std::queue is a Container Adaptor.
- It follows the FIFO (First-In, First-Out) principle. The first element inserted is the first one to be removed.
- It does not manage its own memory; it relies entirely on an underlying container.

Observations From Your Code (API Breakdown):
1. Member Types and Underlying Containers:
   - The default underlying container is std::deque.
   - You demonstrated compatibility with std::deque and std::list.
   - Constructors include default, copy, and initialization from an underlying container (like std::deque).
2. Capacity and Access:
   - empty() and size() check the state of the queue.
   - front() accesses the first element (the one next in line to be removed).
   - back() accesses the last element (the most recently added).
3. Modifiers:
   - push() and emplace() insert elements at the back.
   - pop() removes the element at the front.
   - push_range() (C++23) inserts multiple elements at once.
   - swap() exchanges the contents of two queues.
4. Non-Member Functions:
   - Supports comparison operators (==, <, <=> in C++20).
   - Supports std::swap and std::format (C++23).

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (The "Why" Behind the Design):

Twist 1: Why Can't We Use std::vector as the Underlying Container? (The Biggest Trap)
- In our previous discussions about std::stack, we saw that std::vector can be used because a stack only needs push_back() and pop_back().
- However, a queue requires push_back() (to insert at the back) and pop_front() (to remove from the front).
- std::vector does not have a pop_front() method. Removing from the front of a vector requires shifting all subsequent elements, which takes O(N) time.
- Therefore, std::vector is invalid for std::queue. The only valid containers are std::deque (default) and std::list.

Twist 2: Why Does pop() Return void?
- Just like std::stack, std::queue::pop() does not return the removed element.
- If pop() returned the element by value, it would have to make a copy of the front element before removing it.
- If that copy constructor throws an exception, the element is removed from the queue but lost during the copy process (data loss).
- By separating front() and pop(), C++ guarantees strong exception safety.
- To safely remove an element and keep its value: int val = q.front(); q.pop();

Twist 3: How to Iterate a Queue? (The Iterator Problem)
- std::queue does not provide iterators. You cannot use range-based for loops or standard algorithms like std::find.
- Interview Question: "How do you print all elements of a queue without destroying it?"
- Answer: You must make a copy of the queue. Then, you pop elements from the copy while printing them.
- Popping from the original queue would permanently destroy the data.

Twist 4: The Initializer List Trap
- Writing std::queue<int> q{1, 2, 3}; will result in a compile error.
- A queue does not accept an initializer list directly.
- The correct way is to initialize the underlying container: std::queue<int> q(std::deque<int>{1, 2, 3}); or push elements one by one.

Twist 5: Deque vs List (Cache Locality)
- The default container is std::deque. A deque allocates memory in chunks, providing excellent cache locality and fast O(1) operations at both ends.
- std::list is a doubly-linked list. While it also provides O(1) push_back and pop_front, its cache locality is terrible because nodes are scattered across memory.
- Interview Answer: std::deque is always the best choice for a queue unless you have a highly specific memory constraint.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. std::vector is invalid for std::queue because it lacks pop_front().
2. Calling front() or back() on an empty queue causes undefined behavior (a crash). Always check q.empty() first.
3. pop() returns void. You must use front() to read the value before popping.
4. No iterators exist. You cannot use range-based loops.
5. Thread safety: std::queue is not thread-safe. If multiple threads push and pop concurrently, you need a mutex or a lock-free queue.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- BFS (Breadth-First Search): Traversing graphs or trees level by level.
- Task Scheduling: CPU scheduling, printer queues, or OS message queues.
- Network Buffers: Handling incoming data packets in the exact order they arrive.
- Web Servers: Processing incoming requests on a first-come, first-served basis.

Core Rule for Interviews:
A queue represents "Fairness and Ordering." If a problem involves processing items in the exact order they arrive, or finding the shortest path in an unweighted graph, a queue is your default choice. Always remember the underlying container trap, as interviewers love to test if you know why std::vector cannot be used.

Here is the complete deep dive based on your two code blocks. It integrates your observations about the API, the custom comparator, and connects them to all the previous concepts we have discussed (like underlying containers, iterators, and interview traps).

87. STD::PRIORITY_QUEUE - API, CUSTOM COMPARATORS, AND DEEP DIVE

Basic Definition:
- std::priority_queue is a container adaptor.
- It provides constant time access to the largest element (by default).
- It is implemented as a Max-Heap.
- Insertion and removal take O(log N) time. Accessing the top takes O(1) time.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. Member Types and Containers:
   - value_type, size_type, reference, container_type (the underlying container), and value_compare.
   - The default underlying container is std::vector (unlike stack and queue which use std::deque).
2. Min-Heap vs Max-Heap:
   - Default: std::priority_queue<int> is a Max-Heap (largest at top).
   - Custom: By passing std::greater<int> as the third template argument, you create a Min-Heap (smallest at top).
3. Constructors:
   - Default, range (from iterators), and copy constructors.
4. Modifiers:
   - push(), emplace(), and push_range() (C++23).
   - pop() removes the top element.
   - swap() exchanges two priority queues.
5. Custom Comparators:
   - In your second code block, you created a struct EdgeCompare.
   - It takes two Edge objects, calculates their sums, and compares them.
   - It satisfies the strict weak ordering requirement, allowing std::priority_queue to figure out which Edge has the highest priority.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: Why is std::vector the Default Underlying Container?
- In std::stack and std::queue, the default is std::deque.
- But std::priority_queue uses std::vector. Why?
- Because a heap is essentially a binary tree stored in an array. The heap algorithms (std::push_heap and std::pop_heap) need to jump to parent and child indices (e.g., 2*i + 1, 2*i + 2). This requires random access iterators.
- std::vector has the fastest random access and the best cache locality. std::list would be terrible here because you cannot jump to arbitrary indices.

Twist 2: The Custom Comparator Logic
- Your EdgeCompare uses e1Sum < e2Sum.
- std::priority_queue puts the "largest" element at the top based on the comparator.
- Because your comparator says "e1 is less than e2 if e1's sum is smaller than e2's sum", the queue will put the Edge with the largest sum at the top.
- Output expectation: e3(3,3) sum=6 will be printed first, then e2(2,2) sum=4, then e1(1,1) sum=2.
- If you wanted a Min-Heap based on the sum, you would change the comparator to return e1Sum > e2Sum.

Twist 3: The Iterator Problem (Again!)
- Just like std::stack and std::queue, std::priority_queue has NO iterators.
- You cannot use a range-based for loop.
- In your second code block, you correctly used a while(!edges.empty()) loop and popped elements to print them. This is the only way to traverse a priority queue, and it destroys the queue in the process.

Twist 4: Exception Safety and pop()
- Just like stack and queue, pop() returns void.
- You must use Edge topEdge = edges.top(); before calling edges.pop();.
- This guarantees strong exception safety. If the copy constructor throws, the element is not lost.

Twist 5: Heapification Time Complexity
- Pushing N elements one by one takes O(N log N) time.
- However, if you use the range constructor or push_range(), it builds the heap in O(N) time using the Floyd's build-heap algorithm. This is a crucial optimization for large datasets.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Missing Comparator / operator<: The compiler will throw a massive error if the type does not support comparison.
2. Can you use std::list as the underlying container? No, because std::list does not support random access, which is required for heap algorithms.
3. Max-Heap vs Min-Heap: Remember that default is Max-Heap. Use std::greater for Min-Heap.
4. No iterators exist. You must pop elements to read them.
5. Does priority_queue allow duplicates? Yes, it allows duplicates (unlike std::set).

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Dijkstra's Shortest Path Algorithm: To efficiently get the node with the shortest distance.
- Task Scheduling: OS process scheduling where higher priority tasks run first.
- Huffman Coding: Data compression algorithms.
- Kth Largest Element: Finding the top K elements in a massive stream of data (e.g., Top K frequent elements in a dataset).

Core Rule for Interviews:
A priority queue represents "Priority over Fairness." Whenever a problem requires you to repeatedly extract the maximum or minimum element efficiently (like scheduling or pathfinding), use a priority queue. Always pay attention to the comparator logic, because getting the heap ordering backwards is a very common mistake.

91. THE C++ ITERATOR OMNIBUS - DEEP DIVE & INTERVIEW TRAPS

Overview of this Omnibus:

This code is an architectural masterpiece. It validates every single era of C++ iterators, from C++98 legacy tags to C++20 Concepts and C++23 adaptors.

It proves that iterators are not just pointers; they are a strict compile-time contract.

If an algorithm requires a specific category (like Random Access), the compiler uses these tags to reject invalid containers (like a list) before the program even runs.

MODULE BREAKDOWN & TWISTS:

Custom Contiguous Iterator

You built a custom iterator that satisfies the C++20 std::contiguous_iterator concept.

Twist: Notice the difference between iterator_category (legacy C++98) and iterator_concept (C++20). Modern C++ algorithms use iterator_concept first. A contiguous iterator guarantees that *(it + n) is exactly equivalent to *(std::addressof(*it) + n).

Trap: A custom iterator MUST define all associated types (value_type, difference_type, pointer, reference) or it will fail to work with standard algorithms.

Range Access Generics

std::size and std::ssize: std::ssize returns a signed integer (ptrdiff_t). Why? To prevent integer underflow bugs when subtracting iterators.

std::data: Gives you a raw pointer to the underlying memory.

Trap: std::data() will not compile for non-contiguous containers like std::list. It only works for vectors, arrays, and strings.

Runtime Navigation

std::advance: Modifies the iterator in place.

std::next and std::prev: Return a new iterator, leaving the original untouched.

Twist: The time complexity of std::advance depends entirely on the container. If you use it on a std::list, it takes O(N) time because it must step node by node. If you use it on a std::vector, it takes O(1) time because it just does pointer arithmetic.

Modern Trap: C++20 introduced std::ranges::advance. Why? Because the old std::advance could go past the end() and cause undefined behavior. The new ranges version takes a sentinel (boundary) and stops safely.

Traits and Metaprogramming

std::iterator_traits: This is the bridge between iterators and templates. It allows algorithms to know the types associated with an iterator.

Twist: In C++20, this is largely replaced by std::iter_value_t and std::iter_difference_t, which use Concepts under the hood.

Iterator Adaptors (The Proxy Pattern)

Reverse Iterator: rbegin() actually holds the end() iterator of the container. When you dereference a reverse iterator, it secretly does *(it - 1).

Inserter Iterators (back_inserter, front_inserter, inserter):

Trap: You cannot use back_inserter on a std::set because sets do not have a push_back() method. You must use std::inserter.

Trap: You cannot use front_inserter on a std::vector because vectors do not have push_front().

Move Iterator: std::make_move_iterator casts the dereferenced value to an rvalue. This is crucial for std::vector<std::string>. Instead of copying heavy strings during a std::copy, it moves them, leaving the source strings empty.

Counted Iterator (C++20): Wraps an iterator and a count. It stops iterating after N steps, acting as a safe sentinel.

I/O Stream Iterators
std::istream_iterator: Reads data from a stream (like std::cin or std::stringstream). By default, it skips whitespace.

std::ostream_iterator: Writes data to a stream, adding a delimiter (like a space or newline) after each element.

Twist: Stream iterators are single-pass (InputIterator). Once you read a character from the stream, you cannot go back.

C++20 Concepts Validation

This is the modern way to write template code. Instead of relying on cryptic error messages, we use std::contiguous_iterator or std::random_access_iterator.

Twist: Concepts are evaluated at compile time. If the concept is not satisfied, the compiler stops immediately, giving a clear error message.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):

Assuming std::advance is O(1): It is only O(1) for Random Access Iterators. For lists, it is O(N).

Using the wrong inserter: Using back_inserter on a std::set or front_inserter on a std::vector causes a compile error.

Trying to modify a const_iterator: const_iterator enforces read-only access. Attempting to assign to *it is a compile error.

Dereferencing a move_iterator source: After moving elements with move_iterator, the original container elements are in a "valid but unspecified" state (often empty strings).

Mixing iterator categories: Trying to pass a list iterator to std::sort fails because std::sort strictly requires random_access_iterator.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):

Custom Containers: If you write your own library, you must implement iterators and satisfy these concepts so your container works with std::sort, std::copy, and ranges.

Zero-Copy Parsing: std::string_view combined with istream_iterator allows parsing large files without allocating new memory.

High-Performance Pipelines: std::move_iterator is the engine behind moving data efficiently between memory buffers.

Core Rule for Interviews:
Iterators are the universal glue of the STL. They define a strict contract of capabilities (read, write, move, jump). Always match the iterator category to the algorithmic requirement, prefer ranges and concepts in modern C++, and remember that adaptors like back_inserter and move_iterator allow you to change the behavior of an iterator without writing a new class.

92. THE C++ ALGORITHMS OMNIBUS - COMPARISON, COPY, COUNT, AND PREDICATES

Basic Definition:
- std::equal compares two ranges element-wise and returns true if they match.
- std::lexicographical_compare compares two ranges in dictionary order.
- std::copy copies elements from a source range to a destination.
- std::count counts how many elements equal a given value.
- std::count_if counts how many elements satisfy a predicate.
- std::all_of, std::any_of, std::none_of answer yes/no predicate questions.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. Return Types:
   - std::equal returns bool
   - std::lexicographical_compare returns bool
   - std::copy returns the destination end iterator (not void)
   - std::count and std::count_if return difference_type (signed)
   - std::all_of, any_of, none_of return bool
2. Version History:
   - std::equal: 3-arg (C++98), 4-arg (C++14), ranges::equal (C++20)
   - std::copy: basic, copy_if (C++11), copy_n, copy_backward, ranges::copy (C++20)
   - Predicate queries: added C++11, ranges versions in C++20
3. Short-circuit Behavior:
   - equal stops at first mismatch
   - all_of stops at first false
   - any_of stops at first true
   - none_of stops at first true
   - count and count_if do NOT short-circuit
4. Empty Range Behavior:
   - all_of returns true (vacuous truth)
   - any_of returns false
   - none_of returns true

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: The 3-argument std::equal Trap
The C++98 version takes only the first range's end and the second range's start. It does NOT check the second range's length. If the second range is shorter, it reads past the end, causing undefined behavior. Always prefer the C++14 4-argument version or the C++20 std::ranges::equal. Pre-C++14 codebases are full of this bug.

Twist 2: Vacuous Truth on Empty Ranges
On an empty range, all_of returns true (all zero elements satisfy the predicate — vacuously true). any_of returns false (no element exists to satisfy it). none_of returns true (no element violates it). This is the single most common interview gotcha with these algorithms. If you can explain why all_of returns true on an empty range, you understand the contract.

Twist 3: Prefix Logic in lexicographical_compare
If one range is a prefix of the other, the shorter is considered less. So "abc" is less than "abcd". An empty range is less than everything. People expect equal-length comparison or equality check — it does neither. It returns at the first mismatch.

Twist 4: Overlapping Ranges in std::copy
If source and destination overlap, behavior is undefined. Use std::copy_backward for overlapping shifts, or std::move. Also, d_first must have enough space. If not, buffer overflow and undefined behavior. When the destination size is unknown, use std::back_inserter to grow dynamically.

Twist 5: Signed Return Type of std::count
std::count returns difference_type (typically ptrdiff_t, signed), NOT size_t. This is deliberate. It keeps arithmetic consistent with iterator subtraction and avoids unsigned underflow bugs. People who assume size_t get caught.

Twist 6: memmove Optimization in std::copy
For trivially copyable types (int, char, PODs), std::copy internally uses memmove, making it extremely fast. This is why copying a vector<int> is nearly as fast as a raw array copy. Also, std::copy returns the destination end iterator, which enables chaining and tells you exactly where writing stopped.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Using 3-argument std::equal with a shorter second range: undefined behavior.
2. Expecting all_of to return false on an empty range: it returns true (vacuous truth).
3. Expecting any_of to return true on an empty range: it returns false.
4. Thinking std::copy returns void: it returns the destination end iterator.
5. Assuming std::count returns size_t: it returns a signed difference_type.
6. Using std::copy on overlapping ranges: undefined behavior. Use copy_backward or move.
7. Forgetting that lexicographical_compare treats prefixes as less: "abc" < "abcd" is true.
8. Assuming std::count short-circuits: it always scans the full range. Use any_of for early exit.
9. Passing a too-small destination to std::copy without back_inserter: buffer overflow.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Validation pipelines: all_of and any_of are the backbone of config and input validation. They short-circuit, so they stop at the first failure.
- Log scanning: count_if answers "how many ERROR-level logs are there?" in a single pass, without allocating a filtered copy.
- Dynamic data copying: std::copy with back_inserter is the standard pattern for building result vectors when the output size is unknown upfront.
- Version and string ordering: lexicographical_compare handles version string ordering, but remember it compares as strings, not numbers, so "1.2.10" sorts before "1.2.9". Numeric version comparison needs a custom comparator.
- Diff algorithms: std::equal's short-circuit behavior is the first layer of any diff tool.
- Zero-allocation predicate checks: all_of, any_of, none_of let you answer questions about a range without building intermediate containers.

Core Rule for Interviews:
Algorithms are contracts, not just functions. Every algorithm has three things you must know: the return type, the empty-range behavior, and the overlapping or length-mismatch safety. count returns signed, copy returns an iterator, equal returns bool. all_of is true on empty, any_of is false, none_of is true. The 3-arg equal and overlapping copy are UB traps. In modern C++, prefer the std::ranges:: versions — they are length-safe, cleaner, and avoid the classic pre-C++20 bugs.


93. STD::COPY IN ACTION - BACK_INSERTER, REVERSE ITERATORS, RESERVE, AND THE I/O BOTTLENECK

Basic Definition:
- std::copy copies elements from a source range to a destination iterator.
- std::back_inserter creates an insert_iterator that calls push_back on the container.
- rbegin() and rend() are reverse iterators that traverse a container backwards.
- reserve() pre-allocates capacity without changing size.
- std::iota fills a range with sequential values starting from a seed.
- std::endl flushes the stream, while "\n" does not.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - copy with back_inserter, no reserve:
   - v1_copy starts empty, has no capacity reserved.
