6. Confusing partition_point with lower_bound: partition_point works on a boolean predicate, lower_bound works on a sorted order.
7. Expecting partition_copy to modify the source: it does not, it writes to two separate destinations.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Quickselect and median finding: partition followed by partition_point is the core of quickselect and nth_element implementations.
- Data triage: partition_copy splits a stream into accepted and rejected records without touching the source - validation pipelines, spam filters, alert routers.
- Binary search over predicate boundaries: partition_point is the binary search for "the last row that satisfies X" or "the first event after timestamp Y", given that the data is ordered or grouped.
- Sorted-then-partitioned pipelines: after sort, use partition_point to find the boundary of a value range in O(log N) instead of scanning.
- Guarded mutation: the is_partitioned check before partition is the pattern for "normalize this range only if needed", saving the cost of a partition call on already-partitioned data.
- Stable splitting: partition_copy when downstream consumers depend on the original relative order of records within each group.

Core Rule for Interviews:
The partition family has three members plus one guard: is_partitioned verifies, partition mutates in place (unstable), stable_partition mutates in place (stable), partition_copy copies into two destinations (stable), and partition_point binary searches the boundary. Never call partition_point without is_partitioned. Never dereference *point without checking point != end. partition is unstable; partition_copy is stable; distance is O(1) only for random access iterators. And remember the guard pattern from test0: is_partitioned first, partition only if needed, partition_point only after the range is verified. That is the difference between a correct pipeline and one that silently returns the wrong answer on unsorted input.

107. STD::SORT, STD::GREATER, AND ALIAS TEMPLATES - COMPARATOR CONTRACTS AND ITERATOR REQUIREMENTS

Basic Definition:
- std::sort(first, last) sorts a range in non-decreasing order by default.
- std::sort(first, last, comp) sorts using a custom comparator comp.
- std::greater<int>() is a functor that returns a > b, producing descending order.
- std::sort requires RandomAccessIterator.
- std::sort is not stable; equal elements may be reordered.
- It uses introsort internally: quicksort with heapsort and insertion-sort fallbacks.
- A template alias (template<class T> using dType = std::vector<T>) creates a reusable generic shorthand.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. Alias template at the top:
   - template<class T> using dType = std::vector<T>; declares a generic alias.
   - dType<int> is identical to std::vector<int> at compile time.
   - This is the generic version of the using DataType = std::vector<int> alias from Note 98.
2. test0 - descending sort with std::greater:
   - c = {1, 7, 5, 2, -7, 4, -100, 500}.
   - std::sort(c.begin(), c.end(), std::greater<int>()) sorts in descending order.
   - Expected output: 500 7 5 4 2 1 -7 -100.
3. Comparator is a functor:
   - std::greater<int> is a callable type from <functional>.
   - std::greater<int>() constructs an instance, which sort invokes as comp(a, b).
   - It returns a > b. sort interprets comp(a, b) == true as "a comes before b".
4. Iterator requirement is RandomAccess:
   - c is a vector, so random access is available.
   - The same call on std::list<int> would fail to compile.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: sort's Comparator Defines "Comes Before", Not "Compare"
The comparator comp(a, b) returns true if a should come before b in the sorted output. With std::greater<int>, comp(5, 3) returns true because 5 > 3, so 5 is placed before 3 - hence descending. With the default std::less<int>, comp(3, 5) returns true because 3 < 5, so 3 is placed before 5 - hence ascending. The direction of the comparator flips the direction of the sort. This is why std::greater produces descending order, not "greater than" in the sense of equality. Interviewers use this to check whether you understand that comp is a "should a come before b" predicate, not a numeric comparison.

Twist 2: The Comparator Must Be a Strict Weak Ordering
The standard requires comp to be a strict weak ordering. This means:
- Irreflexive: comp(a, a) must be false.
- Asymmetric: if comp(a, b) is true, comp(b, a) must be false.
- Transitive: if comp(a, b) and comp(b, c) are true, comp(a, c) must be true.
- Transitivity of equivalence: if a is equivalent to b and b is equivalent to c, then a is equivalent to c.

Writing comp as <= instead of < violates irreflexivity (comp(a, a) is true) and is undefined behaviour. Writing a comparator whose result depends on shared mutable state also breaks the ordering. The classic interview trap is a comparator that returns true for equal elements - it compiles, runs, and can crash with out-of-bounds access inside sort.

Twist 3: sort Is Not Stable
Equal elements have no guaranteed relative order after sort. If you sort records by one field and expect the original order of records with the same key to be preserved, you need std::stable_sort. It costs extra memory in the general case but preserves the original order of equivalent elements. The common idiom is: sort by the primary key with stable_sort, then sort by the secondary key with sort - the two-pass pattern that produces a lexicographic order without a compound comparator.

Twist 4: sort Requires RandomAccessIterator
This is the classic companion to the iterator category lesson from Note 91. sort needs constant-time jumps, so it works on vector, deque, array, and raw arrays. It does not work on list, forward_list, or set (which is sorted by design). Trying to call sort on a list fails to compile. The fix is std::list::sort, a member function that runs in O(N log N) with BidirectionalIterator. This is the same category rule that governed reverse in Note 97, rotate in Note 101, and shuffle in Note 102. If the iterator cannot jump, sort refuses.

Twist 5: introsort Guarantees O(N log N) Worst Case
Pure quicksort has O(N²) worst case on adversarial input. std::sort uses introsort: it starts with quicksort, switches to heapsort when the recursion depth exceeds 2*log(N), and finishes with insertion sort on small ranges. The result is O(N log N) worst case, average, and best case. This is why std::sort is safe to use on untrusted input, whereas a hand-written quicksort is not. The cost is that std::sort cannot guarantee stability, because heapsort and quicksort are both unstable.

Twist 6: Alias Templates vs Type Aliases
using dType = std::vector<int> is a non-template type alias: it names one concrete type. template<class T> using dType = std::vector<T> is an alias template: it names a family of types. The two produce identical code for the instantiation dType<int>, but the alias template is reusable across T. This is the same mechanism the standard library uses for std::remove_cv_t and std::enable_if_t. Interviewers like it because it shows the difference between naming a type and naming a pattern.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Using <= in a comparator instead of <: violates strict weak ordering, undefined behaviour.
2. Writing comp(a, b) as a numeric comparison instead of a "comes before" predicate: reverses the intended order.
3. Expecting sort to be stable: it is not, use stable_sort.
4. Calling sort on std::list or std::forward_list: compile error, use std::list::sort.
5. Assuming sort is O(N²) worst case: introsort guarantees O(N log N) worst case.
6. Using a stateful comparator that depends on external mutable state: breaks strict weak ordering, undefined behaviour.
7. Forgetting that std::greater is a functor, not a function: it needs () to be called, or use std::greater<> for transparent comparison.
8. Confusing a type alias with an alias template: the first names one type, the second names a family.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Leaderboards and rankings: sort with std::greater to produce descending order for scores, prices, or timestamps.
- Ranking by multiple keys: stable_sort by primary key, then sort by secondary key - the two-pass idiomatic pattern.
- Data preprocessing: sort is the first step in deduplication (sort plus erase-unique, Note 103), in binary search (sort plus lower_bound), and in partition-based algorithms (sort plus partition_point, Note 106).
- Large dataset ordering: introsort's O(N log N) worst case makes sort the safe default for adversarial or unpredictable input.
- Modern code shape: std::sort(v.begin(), v.end(), std::greater<int>()) is shorter and more expressive than a hand-written descending loop.
- Generic library code: alias templates reduce boilerplate when building containers over parameter types, and std::greater<> (C++14) removes the template argument via transparent comparison.

Core Rule for Interviews:
std::sort sorts in place using a comparator that answers "should a come before b?" - not "is a greater than b?" std::greater<int>() flips the default order to descending. The comparator must be a strict weak ordering: irreflexive, asymmetric, transitive. Never use <=. sort is unstable; stable_sort is stable. sort requires RandomAccessIterator, so it works on vector and deque but not list or forward_list - for those, use the container's own sort member. introsort guarantees O(N log N) worst case, which is why std::sort is safe on untrusted input while hand-written quicksort is not. Alias templates (template<class T> using dType = ...) name families of types; type aliases name one. Same iterator-category rule from Notes 91, 97, 101, and 102 applies here: sort only accepts random access, and the compiler enforces it before the program runs.

108. STD::IS_SORTED AND STD::IS_SORTED_UNTIL - ORDER QUERIES, FORWARD ITERATOR REQUIREMENT, AND THE STD::END TYPO

Basic Definition:
- std::is_sorted(first, last) checks whether a range is in non-decreasing order.
- std::is_sorted(first, last, comp) checks order according to a custom comparator.
- std::is_sorted_until(first, last) returns an iterator to the first element that breaks the sorted order.
- If the entire range is sorted, is_sorted_until returns last.
- Both require only ForwardIterator, not RandomAccessIterator.
- Both short-circuit at the first violation.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - is_sorted on two vectors:
   - v1 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8} is fully ascending.
   - is_sorted returns true, printed as 1 without boolalpha.
   - v2 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, -1} has -1 at the end, breaking the order.
   - is_sorted returns false, printed as 0.
2. test1 - is_sorted_until with a typo:
   - v1 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, -1}.
   - is_sorted_until returns an iterator to the element -1, the first one that violates sorted order.
   - *it correctly dereferences to -1.
   - std::end was written instead of std::endl, causing a compile error.
3. Compile error analysis:
   - error: reference to overloaded function could not be resolved; did you mean to call it?
   - std::end is an overloaded function (initializer_list, array, container overloads).
   - The compiler listed every operator<< candidate that failed to match.
   - The fix is std::endl, the newline-and-flush manipulator.
   - One missing character turned a working line into a hundred-line error.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: is_sorted Checks Non-Decreasing, Not Strictly Increasing
The default comparator is operator<, but is_sorted actually verifies that for every adjacent pair, the earlier element is not greater than the later one. In other words, it checks a[i] <= a[i+1], allowing equal neighbours. So {1, 1, 2, 2, 3} is sorted. This is the same as std::sort's notion of ascending: duplicates are allowed. If you need strictly increasing, pass std::greater_equal-style custom logic or use is_sorted with a comparator that treats equals as violations. Interviewers use this to see if you conflate "sorted" with "strictly increasing".

Twist 2: is_sorted_until Returns the Boundary, Not a Boolean
is_sorted gives a yes/no answer. is_sorted_until gives you the position where the sorted prefix ends. If the range is fully sorted, it returns last. This makes it the natural companion to partition_point (Note 106) and the boundary-search family: is_sorted_until is the "where does the good prefix stop" query. distance(begin, it) gives the length of the sorted prefix. In test1, the sorted prefix is {1,2,3,4,5,6,7} and the boundary is at index 7, where -1 lives.

Twist 3: Dereference Guard Is Mandatory
In test1, *it is safe because -1 exists and breaks the order, so it != end. In general code, the safe pattern is:

    auto it = std::is_sorted_until(v.begin(), v.end());
    if (it != v.end()) {
        std::cout << *it << std::endl;
    }

If the range were fully sorted, is_sorted_until would return v.end(), and *it would be undefined behaviour. This is the same trap family as *find without checking find != end, *partition_point without checking point != end (Note 106), and all_of on empty ranges (Note 92). The algorithm returns a well-defined iterator; the caller still has to guard the dereference.

Twist 4: ForwardIterator, Not RandomAccess
This is the direct contrast with Note 107. std::sort requires RandomAccessIterator and refuses to compile on list and forward_list. std::is_sorted and std::is_sorted_until require only ForwardIterator, so they compile and run correctly on forward_list and list. The reason: they walk the range one step at a time, comparing neighbours, never jumping. This is the same reason std::fill (Note 95) and std::unique (Note 103) work on forward_list while std::sort does not. The family rule is: linear scans need ForwardIterator, reordering needs RandomAccessIterator.

Twist 5: Short-Circuit Means Best Case Is O(1)
is_sorted walks from the first element forward. The moment it finds a[i] > a[i+1], it returns false. So on an unsorted range, it can return in constant time. On a sorted range, it walks all N elements and returns true. Best case O(1), worst case O(N). is_sorted_until always walks up to the boundary, so its complexity is O(k) where k is the length of the sorted prefix. This is the same short-circuit discipline as std::equal (Note 92) and std::is_partitioned (Note 105).

Twist 6: The std::end Typo Is a Manifestation of Overload Resolution
std::end is not a variable and not a manipulator. It is an overloaded function template, with versions taking initializer_list, arrays, and containers with .end() members. Passing std::end to operator<< asks the compiler to print a function, which has no overload. The compiler tries every operator<< it can find - int, double, char*, string, nullptr_t, wchar_t, char8_t, and more - and lists each as "not viable". This is the C++ compiler's overload-resolution error style, and it is why small typos produce huge error walls. The C++20 constrained overloads (Note 99) give shorter errors for erase; the operator<< overloads still produce this style because they are legacy.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Confusing sorted with strictly increasing: is_sorted allows equal adjacent elements.
2. Dereferencing the result of is_sorted_until without checking it != end: undefined behaviour on a fully sorted range.
3. Assuming is_sorted needs RandomAccessIterator: it needs only ForwardIterator, so it works on list and forward_list.
4. Writing std::end instead of std::endl: overloaded function printed, compiler produces a huge error.
5. Assuming is_sorted is O(N) always: it short-circuits on the first violation.
6. Using is_sorted with a comparator that is not a strict weak ordering: undefined behaviour on the ordering check.
7. Confusing is_sorted with is_sorted_until: one gives a boolean, the other gives a boundary iterator.
8. Using is_sorted_until on a range and treating it as a boolean: it is an iterator, not a bool.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Precondition checks before binary search: std::binary_search and lower_bound assume sorted input. is_sorted verifies it in one O(N) call before the O(log N) search.
- Debug assertions: assert(std::is_sorted(v.begin(), v.end())) in tests catches pipeline bugs where a sort step was skipped.
- Sorted prefix detection: is_sorted_until finds where a run of sorted data ends, useful in streaming merge logic where a sorted chunk is followed by fresh unsorted input.
- Order monitoring in telemetry: check whether a time-series buffer is still in order before running algorithms that assume sorted input.
- Boundary search companions: is_sorted_until plus partition_point (Note 106) plus distance gives a complete "where does the well-ordered prefix stop" toolkit.
- Longest sorted run: is_sorted_until is the inner primitive for finding runs in sorting algorithms like natural merge sort.

Core Rule for Interviews:
is_sorted answers "is this range non-decreasing?" in O(N) worst case with early exit. is_sorted_until returns the boundary iterator, the first element that breaks order, or end() if fully sorted. Both need only ForwardIterator, so they work on forward_list while std::sort (Note 107) does not. is_sorted allows equal neighbours - sorted does not mean strictly increasing. Never dereference the iterator from is_sorted_until without checking it != end. And finally: std::end is an overloaded function, std::endl is a manipulator. One character difference, and the compiler prints every operator<< candidate as not viable. The two-question checklist for order queries: do you need the boolean (is_sorted) or the boundary (is_sorted_until), and have you guarded the dereference?

109. STD::NTH_ELEMENT AND STD::PARTIAL_SORT - MEDIAN SELECTION, TOP-K SORTING, AND THE PERFORMANCE LADDER

Basic Definition:
- std::nth_element(first, nth, last) rearranges the range so that the element at nth is the one that would be there if the range were fully sorted.
- All elements before nth are less than or equal to *nth, all elements after are greater than or equal to *nth.
- The order inside the left and right partitions is unspecified.
- Average complexity is O(N), worst case O(N log N) in practice (introselect).
- std::partial_sort(first, middle, last) sorts the first distance(first, middle) elements fully and leaves the rest unspecified.
- Complexity is O(N log k) where k = distance(first, middle).
- Both require RandomAccessIterator.
- Both are unstable.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - nth_element for median:
   - v1 = {5, 1, 3, 9, 7, 2, 4, 6, 8}.
   - m = v1.begin() + v1.size()/2, which is index 4, the middle position.
   - std::nth_element(v1.begin(), m, v1.end()) puts the median at index 4.
   - After the call, *m is 5, which is the 5th smallest element of the 9-element vector.
   - Left side contains {1,2,3,4} in some order, right side contains {6,7,8,9} in some order.
2. test1 - partial_sort for top-k in descending order:
   - Same input v1.
   - m = index 4.
   - std::partial_sort(v1.begin(), m, v1.end(), std::greater<int>()) sorts the first 5 elements in descending order.
   - After the call, the first 5 positions hold {9, 8, 7, 6, 5}.
   - The remaining positions hold {1, 2, 3, 4} in unspecified order.
3. Iterator position for both:
   - m = v1.begin() + v1.size()/2 gives index 4 in a 9-element vector.
   - For nth_element, index 4 is the median position (5th smallest).
   - For partial_sort with greater, index 4 means "top 5 largest".
   - The middle iterator is the contract boundary in both calls.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: nth_element Guarantees One Position, Not a Sorted Side
This is the most commonly misunderstood property of nth_element. It does not sort the left half, it does not sort the right half, it only guarantees that the element at the nth position is correct and that everything to the left is <= and everything to the right is >=. The left and right partitions are internally unordered. In test0, index 4 holds 5, but indexes 0 to 3 may be {1,3,2,4} in any order, not {1,2,3,4}. If you need both sides sorted, use sort. If you need only the kth element, use nth_element - it is average O(N) instead of O(N log N).

Twist 2: Median Is nth_element, Not a Special Algorithm
The median of a range is the element at index size()/2 after sorting, but you never have to sort to get it. nth_element(begin, begin + size/2, end) produces the median in average O(N) time. This is the standard idiom and it is faster than sort. If the size is even, size()/2 gives the upper median; (size()-1)/2 gives the lower median. For statistical use, choose the definition deliberately. For quickselect algorithms and median-of-medians, this is the core primitive.

Twist 3: partial_sort Is Top-K, Not Partial Nth
partial_sort(first, middle, last) fully sorts the prefix [first, middle) and leaves [middle, last) unspecified. The guarantee is stronger than nth_element's for the sorted prefix: not just the boundary element, but the entire prefix is in sorted order. The cost is O(N log k) vs nth_element's O(N). If you need the top k elements in sorted order - a leaderboard, a search result page, top-k frequent elements - partial_sort is the right algorithm. If you only need the kth element itself, nth_element is cheaper.

Twist 4: std::greater Flips the Meaning of "Top K"
With the default std::less, partial_sort puts the smallest k elements in sorted ascending order at the front. With std::greater, it puts the largest k elements in sorted descending order at the front. The middle iterator still means the same thing - "the first k positions are sorted". But the meaning of k flips from "smallest k" to "largest k". In test1, the middle is index 4, so the first 5 positions hold the largest 5 elements in descending order: 9 8 7 6 5. This is the exact idiom for top-k queries and leaderboards.

Twist 5: The Performance Ladder - sort vs partial_sort vs nth_element
Three algorithms, three contracts, three complexities:
- sort: O(N log N), full sorted range, unstable.
- partial_sort: O(N log k), first k sorted, rest unspecified, unstable.
- nth_element: O(N) average, one position correct, sides partitioned, unstable.
If you need everything sorted, use sort. If you need the top k in order, use partial_sort. If you only need the kth element, use nth_element. Interviewers love this ladder because it shows whether you can pick the right tool for the precision required. Choosing sort for a top-10 query is the classic performance mistake.

Twist 6: Both Require RandomAccessIterator
This is the same family rule from Notes 91, 101, 102, 107, and 108. nth_element and partial_sort both need RandomAccessIterator because they jump around the range. They work on vector, deque, array. They do not work on list, forward_list, or set. This contrasts directly with is_sorted (Note 108) and unique (Note 103), which only need ForwardIterator because they scan linearly. The rule is simple: reordering algorithms require RandomAccess, scanning algorithms require Forward.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Assuming nth_element sorts the left half: it does not, only the nth position is guaranteed.
2. Using sort when nth_element would do: O(N log N) instead of O(N) for a median query.
3. Using nth_element when partial_sort is needed: nth_element does not give the top k in sorted order.
4. Forgetting std::greater to flip to "top k largest": default partial_sort gives "smallest k first".
5. Passing a middle iterator outside [first, last]: undefined behaviour.
6. Trying nth_element or partial_sort on a list: does not compile, RandomAccessIterator required.
7. Dereferencing nth or middle without checking it is within range: undefined behaviour, though the middle is always valid here by construction.
8. Expecting stability: both algorithms are unstable, use stable_sort if you need order preservation (and pay the extra cost).

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Median and percentile: nth_element at position size()*p for any percentile p, in average O(N), no full sort needed.
- Top-K queries: partial_sort with greater for leaderboards, search ranking, top-N frequent elements in a stream.
- Quickselect core: nth_element is the STL's quickselect - partitioning plus recursion until the nth position is placed.
- Filtering large datasets: find the top 100 from a million records with partial_sort in O(N log 100) instead of O(N log N).
- Streaming medians: nth_element on a sliding window buffer is the standard sub-routine for maintaining a running median.
- Performance interviews: the sort/partial_sort/nth_element ladder is one of the clearest examples of "choose the algorithm based on the exact guarantee you need, not just the biggest hammer".

Core Rule for Interviews:
nth_element fixes one position, partial_sort fixes the first k in sorted order, sort fixes everything. Complexities follow the contracts: O(N) for nth_element, O(N log k) for partial_sort, O(N log N) for sort. Use nth_element for medians and kth-element queries, partial_sort for top-k lists, sort for full ordering. Add std::greater to flip partial_sort from "smallest k" to "largest k". Both nth_element and partial_sort require RandomAccessIterator - the same category rule from Notes 91, 101, 102, 107, and 108 - so they work on vector and deque but not on list or forward_list. And remember the general ladder: if you do not need the full sorted range, do not pay for it.

110. STD::MERGE AND THE PARTIAL_SORT-TWICE TRICK - SORTED PRECONDITIONS AND HALF-SORT PATTERNS

Basic Definition:
- std::merge(first1, last1, first2, last2, d_first) merges two sorted ranges into one sorted destination range.
- Both inputs must already be sorted by the same ordering, otherwise behaviour is undefined.
- The result is stable: equal elements from the first range come before equal elements from the second.
- Complexity is O(N + M), where N and M are the two input lengths.
- std::merge requires InputIterator for both sources and OutputIterator for the destination.
- std::partial_sort(first, middle, last) sorts the smallest distance(first, middle) elements into [first, middle) and leaves [middle, last) unspecified.
- Calling partial_sort twice, once on the first half and once on the second half, produces a fully sorted range.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - guarded merge of two vectors:
   - v1 = {1, 3, 5, 7, -5}.
   - v2 = {2, 4, 6, -8}.
   - print before sort shows the unsorted state.
   - check_sort(v1) and check_sort(v2) run the is_sorted guard and call sort if needed.
   - After the guard, both vectors are fully sorted: v1 = {-5, 1, 3, 5, 7}, v2 = {-8, 2, 4, 6}.
   - std::merge with std::back_inserter(result) appends the merged sequence into result.
   - Output: -8 -5 1 2 3 4 5 6 7.
2. test0 - check_sort helper:
   - It reads: if (!std::is_sorted(v.begin(), v.end())) { std::sort(begin(v), end(v)); }.
   - This is the guard pattern from Note 108: only sort if the precondition is not already satisfied.
   - It avoids the O(N log N) cost when the input is already sorted.
3. test1 - partial_sort twice on two halves:
   - v = {5, 7, 2, 6, 9, 8}, m = v.size()/2 = 3.
   - First call: partial_sort(begin(v), begin(v) + m, end(v)).
     - Postcondition: the smallest 3 elements of the entire range are placed, in sorted order, at positions [0, 3).
     - That is {2, 5, 6}.
     - Positions [3, 6) hold the remaining 3 elements {7, 8, 9} in unspecified order.
   - Second call: partial_sort(begin(v) + m, end(v), end(v)).
     - Here middle equals last, so partial_sort is just a full sort of [begin(v) + m, end(v)).
     - That sorts the remaining 3 elements into {7, 8, 9}.
   - Combined output: {2, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9} - a fully sorted vector.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: merge Assumes Sorted Input, It Does Not Sort
This is the most common misuse of std::merge. The algorithm does not sort the inputs, it only interleaves two already-sorted ranges into a single sorted range. If either input is unsorted, the output is meaningless and the behaviour is undefined because the merge logic compares the current heads of each range. In test0, both vectors are unsorted at the start, so check_sort is mandatory. Remove the guard and the merge produces garbage. This is the same precondition discipline as binary_search and partition_point (Note 106) - if you skip the precondition check, you get an answer that looks plausible but is wrong.

Twist 2: The check_sort Guard Pattern
check_sort is a small but powerful idiom:

    void check_sort(std::vector<int>& v) {
        if (!std::is_sorted(v.begin(), v.end())) {
            std::sort(begin(v), end(v));
        }
    }

It uses is_sorted from Note 108 as a precondition guard. On already-sorted input, the cost is O(N) for the check, with no sort. On unsorted input, the cost is O(N) for the check plus O(N log N) for the sort. This is the same pattern as the is_partitioned guard before partition in Note 106 - verify, only mutate if the verification fails. It is the safe way to write merge-based pipelines where some inputs come pre-sorted and some do not.

Twist 3: merge Is Stable
When equal elements exist in both inputs, the ones from the first range come out before the ones from the second. This is the definition of stability for merge. It matters when the elements carry secondary data - for example, merging two lists of records sorted by timestamp, where ties should prefer one list over the other. std::merge guarantees this order; an unstable merge would not.

Twist 4: The partial_sort-Twice Trick
test1 is unusual and worth understanding precisely. partial_sort(first, middle, last) has a strong postcondition:

    [first, middle) contains the smallest (middle - first) elements of the whole range, in sorted order.
    [middle, last) contains the remaining (largest) elements, in unspecified order.

So the first partial_sort puts the smallest half at the front. The remaining half contains the largest half. Sorting that remaining half - which is just a full sort of [middle, last) - completes the sort. The final output is identical to std::sort's output. But the intent is completely different: this is not a general sorting algorithm, it is a demonstration of partial_sort's postcondition. If you need a full sort, call std::sort; the partial_sort-twice pattern is only interesting as an interview discussion point.

Twist 5: merge Does Not Shrink, merge Does Not Mutate
std::merge copies the interleaved result into the destination. The sources are untouched. This is the same in-place versus copy distinction from Notes 97, 98, 101, 103, and 104: reverse/reverse_copy, remove/remove_copy, rotate/rotate_copy, unique/unique_copy. merge always writes to a destination - there is no in-place variant. For in-place merging of two consecutive sorted halves of the same container, use std::inplace_merge. That is the algorithm that std::sort itself uses internally for its merge step.

Twist 6: merge and the Set Algorithms
merge is the simplest of the "sorted input" family. The others are:
- set_union, set_intersection, set_difference, set_symmetric_difference.
- inplace_merge for two consecutive sorted halves in the same container.
- includes for "is one sorted range a subset of another".

All of them require sorted inputs, all are stable, and all use the same two-pointer merge logic under the hood. If you understand merge, the rest of the family is a variation on the same skeleton. Interviewers use this cluster to test whether you know that "sorted precondition" is a family-wide contract, not just a merge quirk.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Calling std::merge on unsorted inputs: undefined behaviour, output is garbage. Sort first or use check_sort.
2. Forgetting the destination has no room: an empty vector without back_inserter segfaults, same trap as Notes 93, 97, 98, 100, 103, and 104.
3. Assuming merge sorts the inputs: it does not, it only interleaves sorted ranges.
4. Confusing merge with inplace_merge: merge writes to a destination, inplace_merge merges two consecutive sorted halves in place.
5. Expecting merge to remove duplicates: it does not, use set_union for deduplication during merge.
6. Assuming merge is unstable: it is stable, first-range elements come before equal second-range elements.
7. Using partial_sort-twice instead of sort: it works by accident of partial_sort's postcondition, but it is not the intended idiom and is much slower than sort in general.
8. Forgetting partial_sort's postcondition when reasoning about the first call: [middle, last) holds the remaining largest elements, which is why the second partial_sort completes the sort.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Merging sorted event streams: two time-ordered logs merged into a single chronological timeline.
- External sort and merge sort: merge is the final step of external sorting, where sorted chunks are combined into a fully sorted result.
- Database join primitives: merge join of two sorted relations uses exactly this pattern, with stability as a real requirement.
- Multi-source pipelines: merging sorted search results from multiple shards into a single ranked list.
- Guarded preprocessing: check_sort is the pattern for "make sure this is sorted before merge" in production pipelines where some inputs come pre-sorted and some do not.
- Teaching tool: the partial_sort-twice trick is an excellent interview question because it forces the candidate to reason about partial_sort's postcondition precisely, not just recite "partial_sort sorts the first half".

Core Rule for Interviews:
merge interleaves two already-sorted ranges into one sorted destination, stably, in O(N + M). It does not sort, and it does not deduplicate - that is set_union's job. Both inputs must satisfy the sorted precondition; check_sort with is_sorted is the idiomatic guard. partial_sort's postcondition is stronger than "sort the first half": it guarantees the smallest k elements are in the prefix, in order, and the remaining largest elements are in the suffix, unordered. That is why the partial_sort-twice pattern produces a fully sorted range - but it is a demonstration, not an idiom; use std::sort for full sorts. Same lessons repeat from earlier notes: destination must be pre-sized or use back_inserter, preconditions must be verified before use, and the sorted-input family (merge, inplace_merge, set_union, set_intersection, includes) all share the same contract.

111. STD::BINARY_SEARCH, STD::LOWER_BOUND, AND STD::UPPER_BOUND - PRESENCE VERSUS POSITION AND THE EQUAL-RANGE BOUNDARY

Basic Definition:
- std::binary_search(first, last, value) returns true if value exists in a sorted range, false otherwise.
- std::lower_bound(first, last, value) returns an iterator to the first element that is not less than value.
- std::upper_bound(first, last, value) returns an iterator to the first element that is greater than value.
- All three require the range to be sorted by the same ordering used for comparison.
- All three do O(log N) comparisons.
- binary_search returns bool; lower_bound and upper_bound return iterators.
- The pair [lower_bound, upper_bound) is the range of elements equal to value.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - binary_search on a sorted vector:
   - v = {1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 12, 13, 14}.
   - std::binary_search(begin(v), end(v), 7) returns true because 7 is present.
   - boolalpha is used so the output prints "true" instead of "1".
   - The answer is a boolean - no position information is provided.
2. test1 - lower_bound and upper_bound on duplicates:
   - v = {1, 3, 5, 5, 5, 5, 5, 7, 9, 11, 12, 13, 14}.
   - lower_bound for 5 returns an iterator to the first 5, at index 2.
   - upper_bound for 5 returns an iterator to the first element greater than 5, which is 7 at index 7.
   - *lower prints 5, *upper prints 7.
   - upper - lower equals 5, the count of 5s in the vector.
3. Same value, two different boundaries:
   - lower_bound points at the start of the equal run.
   - upper_bound points just past the end of the equal run.
   - [lower, upper) is exactly the range of elements equal to the searched value.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: binary_search Returns bool, Not an Iterator
This is the first thing interviewers catch. std::binary_search answers "is it there?" in O(log N) comparisons. It does not return an iterator. If you need the position, use lower_bound and compare its result to end():

    auto it = std::lower_bound(v.begin(), v.end(), value);
    bool found = (it != v.end() && *it == value);

That gives you both presence and position in one O(log N) call. Using binary_search followed by a separate lower_bound doubles the work. Interviewers use this to test whether you know the difference between a query and a point lookup.

Twist 2: lower_bound Is the Start of the Equal Run
lower_bound(first, last, value) returns the first element that is not less than value. If value is present, this is the first occurrence. If value is absent, this is the position where it would be inserted to keep the range sorted. That makes lower_bound the standard insertion point query. It is also the right primitive for set operations on sorted ranges - std::set_union and std::set_difference are built on lower_bound and upper_bound internally.

Twist 3: upper_bound Is the End of the Equal Run
upper_bound(first, last, value) returns the first element strictly greater than value. If value is present with duplicates, upper_bound points just past the last duplicate. If value is absent, lower_bound and upper_bound return the same iterator - the insertion point. The critical relationship is:

    count of value == std::distance(lower_bound, upper_bound)

That distance is O(1) for RandomAccessIterator and O(N) for ForwardIterator. In test1, upper - lower equals 5, which is exactly the number of 5s in the vector.

Twist 4: [lower_bound, upper_bound) Is the Equal Range
The pair of iterators from the two bounds is the definitive description of "all elements equal to value". std::equal_range(first, last, value) returns this pair in one call, internally calling lower_bound and upper_bound. Using equal_range is idiomatic when you want both boundaries; using lower_bound and upper_bound separately is fine and clearer when you only need one. For count queries on sorted data, std::distance(lower_bound, upper_bound) is the standard O(log N) + O(distance) pattern.

Twist 5: Iterator Requirement Is ForwardIterator, but There Is a Hidden Cost
All three algorithms require only ForwardIterator since C++11. That means they compile and run correctly on std::list and std::forward_list. But the O(log N) complexity is for comparisons, not for iterator steps. On a list, advancing the iterator inside the binary search costs O(N) steps total, making the effective cost O(N) even though the number of comparisons is O(log N). On a vector, iterator advance is pointer arithmetic, O(1) per step, so the comparison count dominates: O(log N) overall. This is the same category-versus-complexity trap as std::advance (Note 91). Interviewers use this to see if you understand that O(log N) comparisons only means O(log N) time for random access iterators.

Twist 6: Sorted Precondition Is Mandatory
All three algorithms assume the range is sorted according to the same ordering used for comparison. If the range is not sorted, the binary search looks in the wrong half and returns a meaningless iterator. The behaviour is undefined, not just wrong. This is the same precondition discipline as std::merge (Note 110) and partition_point (Note 106). In practice, guard with is_sorted (Note 108) during development, or sort once at the source and keep the invariant. Also guard the dereference: *upper is undefined if upper equals end(), so always compare the iterator to the sentinel before dereferencing.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Using binary_search when you need the position: it returns bool, not an iterator.
2. Calling *upper_bound without checking upper != end(): undefined behaviour when all elements are <= value.
3. Calling *lower_bound without checking lower != end(): undefined behaviour when all elements are < value.
4. Passing an unsorted range to any of the three: undefined behaviour, garbage result.
5. Assuming O(log N) time on a list: comparisons are O(log N), iterator steps are O(N), effective cost is O(N).
6. Confusing lower_bound with "the value itself": it is the first element not less than value, which may be the value or a greater one.
7. Computing count of a value with a linear scan on sorted data: use distance(lower_bound, upper_bound).
8. Assuming upper_bound returns the same position as lower_bound: they differ when duplicates exist, that is the whole point.
9. Using binary_search plus lower_bound to get both presence and position: use lower_bound once and compare with end().

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Sorted lookup with position: lower_bound plus comparison to end() gives presence and insertion point in one O(log N) call, useful for ordered inserts into a sorted vector.
- Counting occurrences in sorted data: distance(lower_bound, upper_bound) is the O(log N) way to count duplicates, far faster than a linear scan on large sorted buffers.
- Range extraction: [lower_bound, upper_bound) is the exact slice of equal keys, ready for further processing.
- Set algorithms on sorted ranges: set_union, set_intersection, set_difference, and includes all rely on lower_bound and upper_bound internally.
- Time-series range queries: find all events in [start, end] by lower_bound(start) to upper_bound(end) in O(log N), then iterate the slice.
- Precondition checking: combine with is_sorted (Note 108) for safe guard-and-search pipelines, mirroring the is_partitioned plus partition_point pattern from Note 106.

Core Rule for Interviews:
binary_search answers presence in O(log N) and returns bool; lower_bound and upper_bound answer position and return iterators. lower_bound gives the first element not less than value; upper_bound gives the first element greater than value; the pair [lower, upper) is the range of equal keys, and distance(lower, upper) is the count. All three require a sorted range and undefined behaviour without it. All three accept ForwardIterator, so they compile on list, but the O(log N) complexity is for comparisons only - on a list the iterator walk makes the real cost O(N). Always guard the dereference: *lower and *upper are undefined if they equal end(). When you need both presence and position, use lower_bound once and compare the result to end(). When you need both boundaries, use equal_range. Same rules repeat from Notes 91, 106, 108, and 110: preconditions are contracts, iterator category determines real cost, and dereference guards are not optional.

112. STD::INCLUDES - SORTED SUBSET QUERY, MULTIPLICITY, AND THE UNSORTED PRECONDITION TRAP

Basic Definition:
- std::includes(first1, last1, first2, last2) returns true if every element of the second range is also present in the first range.
- Both ranges must be sorted by the same ordering, otherwise behaviour is undefined.
- It respects multiplicity: if v2 contains two 5s, v1 must contain at least two 5s.
- It performs a single merge-style walk, O(N + M) comparisons.
- It requires InputIterator on both sides - the weakest iterator requirement in the sorted-range family.
- It returns bool and produces no output range.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - includes with a sorted first range and an unsorted second range:
   - v1 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6} is sorted ascending.
   - v2 = {21, 2, 3} is not sorted: 21 comes before 2, which violates ascending order.
   - std::includes(v1.begin(), v1.end(), v2.begin(), v2.end()) is called.
   - The result printed with boolalpha is false.
   - Two independent reasons point to false: 21 is not in v1 at all, and v2 is not sorted.
2. Two separate violations in one call:
   - The value 21 has no counterpart in v1 - this alone means the answer must be false, regardless of sorting.
   - v2 is not sorted - this is the precondition violation that technically triggers undefined behaviour.
   - In this run the result is false, which is what a correct sort-aware algorithm would produce for a missing element, but the outcome on unsorted input is not guaranteed.
3. Empty and edge cases not exercised here but implied:
   - An empty v2 returns true vacuously - every element of an empty range is trivially in v1.
   - An empty v1 with a non-empty v2 returns false.
   - Both empty returns true.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: includes Requires Sorted Input, Not Just Any Order
The algorithm walks both ranges with two iterators, advancing whichever head is smaller. This is exactly the merge walk from Note 110, but it only reports whether every element of the second range was found. The walk is correct only if both ranges are sorted: if the second range is unsorted, the iterator can move past elements it should have matched, producing a false negative that looks plausible. In test0, v2 = {21, 2, 3} violates the sort order. The missing 21 makes the result false regardless, but the algorithm is still operating outside its contract. This is the same precondition discipline as merge (Note 110), binary_search and lower_bound (Note 111), and partition_point (Note 106). Interviewers use this exact snippet to check whether you spot the unsorted v2 on sight, not just whether you get the boolean right.

Twist 2: includes Is Merge-Walk, Not Per-Element Binary Search
A naive implementation would call binary_search for each element of v2, costing O(M log N). std::includes is smarter: it does a single two-pointer walk, O(N + M), because both ranges are already sorted. The two-pointer merge walk is the same skeleton used by std::merge, std::set_intersection, and std::set_union. Understanding this tells you why includes needs sorted input: the two-pointer walk relies on the merged order to make progress without backtracking.

Twist 3: Multiplicity Is Respected
includes is not a set-membership test, it is a multiset test. If v1 = {1, 2, 2, 3} and v2 = {2, 2}, the result is true. If v2 = {2, 2, 2}, the result is false, even though 2 is "present" in v1, because v1 only has two 2s. This is the same multiplicity semantics as a multiset containment check. If you need plain set semantics (ignore duplicates), wrap each range through std::unique after sorting before calling includes, or use std::set and std::set::count.

Twist 4: includes versus search
Two algorithms that both "find a range inside a range", but with different meanings. std::search looks for a contiguous subsequence: it requires the elements to appear next to each other, in order, and it does not require either range to be sorted. std::includes looks for a sorted subsequence: elements must appear in order, but they may be interleaved with other elements, and both ranges must be sorted. In test0, {2, 3} appears as a contiguous block in v1, but includes does not care about contiguity - it would still return true if v1 were {1, 2, 9, 3, 7}. That is the key distinction: search is about adjacency, includes is about sorted containment.

Twist 5: includes Is a Query, Not a Transform
Unlike merge, set_union, and set_intersection, includes produces no output range. It is a pure boolean query. The rest of the sorted-family set algorithms all write to a destination, and they all share the same sorted-input precondition. This makes includes the "is this a subset?" predicate to set_union's "produce the union". If you find yourself writing a merge into a temp vector only to check whether one range is a subset of another, you want includes.

Twist 6: Iterator Requirement Is InputIterator
This is the weakest requirement in the entire sorted-range family. InputIterator is enough because the walk only reads each element once and only moves forward. This means includes works on istream_iterator, on forward_list, on list, on any single-pass input range. The sorted precondition is still on the underlying value sequence, so streaming usage only makes sense when the stream is already sorted. But the algorithm's requirement itself is minimal.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Passing an unsorted range to includes: undefined behaviour. test0's v2 = {21, 2, 3} violates the precondition even though the answer happens to be false.
2. Assuming includes is a set-membership test that ignores duplicates: it respects multiplicity, use unique first for set semantics.
3. Using includes when the subsequence must be contiguous: use std::search instead.
4. Expecting includes to return an iterator or range: it returns bool only.
5. Writing a temp merge to answer "is this a subset?": includes is exactly that query in one call.
6. Assuming O(M log N) per-element binary search: includes is a single O(N + M) merge walk.
7. Forgetting the empty-range truth tables: empty v2 returns true, empty v1 with non-empty v2 returns false.
8. Assuming includes needs RandomAccessIterator: it needs only InputIterator.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Config validation: check that a set of required permissions is fully contained in the set of granted permissions, both kept sorted for O(N + M) checks.
- Dependency resolution: verify that a sorted list of build dependencies is a subset of the sorted list of installed packages.
- Inventory checks: confirm that every item in an order is present, with the right quantity, in the sorted inventory list - multiplicity respected.
- Multi-shard search: check whether the sorted result list of one shard is fully present in a merged sorted list from multiple shards.
- Streaming validation: includes works on input iterators, so it can validate sorted streams without materialising them.
- Teaching tool: this snippet is the cleanest way to demonstrate that "sorted precondition" is a family-wide contract in the merge-and-set algorithms, not just a merge quirk.

Core Rule for Interviews:
includes answers "is the second sorted range a multiset subset of the first sorted range?" in O(N + M) with a single merge walk. Both inputs must be sorted - the unsorted v2 in test0 is a precondition violation, not just a wrong answer. Multiplicity is respected: duplicates in v2 must be matched by duplicates in v1. Use includes for containment queries, search for contiguous subsequence queries, and set_union or set_intersection when you need an output range. Empty second range is vacuously included, empty first range with non-empty second is not. And remember the family rule from Notes 106, 108, 110, and 111: sorted-input algorithms are contracts, verify with is_sorted when the input source is untrusted, and never skip the precondition check.

113. STD::SET_UNION AND STD::SET_INTERSECTION - MULTISET SEMANTICS, SORTED PRECONDITION, AND THE SET-OPERATIONS FAMILY

Basic Definition:
- std::set_union(first1, last1, first2, last2, d_first) merges two sorted ranges into a multiset union.
- Each value x appears max(count1(x), count2(x)) times in the output.
- std::set_intersection(first1, last1, first2, last2, d_first) produces the multiset intersection.
- Each value x appears min(count1(x), count2(x)) times in the output.
- Both require both input ranges to be sorted by the same ordering.
- Both run in O(N + M) with a single merge walk.
- Both require InputIterator for sources and OutputIterator for the destination.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - set_union on sorted multisets:
   - v1 = {1, 3, 5, 5, 5, 5, 5, 7, 9} (five 5s), sorted.
   - v2 = {2, 4, 6, 5, 5}, sorted to {2, 4, 5, 5, 6}.
   - std::set_union writes into out via back_inserter.
   - Output: 1 2 3 4 5 5 5 5 5 6 7 9.
   - 5 appears five times, which is max(5, 2).
2. test1 - set_intersection on the same multisets:
   - Same inputs, same sorting step.
   - std::set_intersection writes into out via back_inserter.
   - Output: 5 5.
   - 5 appears two times, which is min(5, 2).
3. Destination is std::list<int> out with std::back_inserter:
   - No size known in advance.
   - back_inserter calls push_back on the list for each output element.
   - Safe across all container types, including list - the same pattern from Notes 93, 97, 98, 100, 103, 104, and 110.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: The "set_" Prefix Is Misleading - These Are Multiset Operations
Every std::set_* algorithm operates on multisets, not sets. Duplicates in the input are preserved in the output according to the multiplicity rule: max for union, min for intersection, difference for difference. In test0, v1 has five 5s and v2 has two 5s; the union keeps five 5s. If these were true set operations, the answer would contain a single 5. This is the most underappreciated detail of the family. If you actually need true set semantics, deduplicate each input with unique (Note 103) after sorting before calling set_union. Interviewers use this snippet specifically to see if you know the algorithm name does not match the container semantics.

Twist 2: Both Require Sorted Input - sort Is Mandatory
The code sorts both vectors before calling either algorithm. This is not optional. Both algorithms use the merge walk from Note 110 and Note 112: they advance whichever head is smaller and emit accordingly. If the input is unsorted, the walk looks in the wrong place and the output is wrong. This is the same precondition discipline as merge (Note 110), includes (Note 112), binary_search and lower_bound (Note 111), and partition_point (Note 106). The sort is O(N log N), the set operation is O(N + M); the sort dominates, which is why "sort once at the source and keep the invariant" is the right production pattern.

Twist 3: The Family - Four Output Algorithms and One Predicate
set_union, set_intersection, set_difference, and set_symmetric_difference are the four output producers. includes (Note 112) is the boolean predicate. All five share the same skeleton: two sorted ranges, two-pointer merge walk, InputIterator on the sources, OutputIterator on the destination for the four producers. Once you understand the walk, the family is a matter of one-line changes to the emission logic. The mental model:

    set_union:            emit head1, then head2, when both are equal emit both.
    set_intersection:     emit only when head1 == head2.
    set_difference:       emit only from range1, skip when head1 == head2.
    set_symmetric_difference: emit both when they differ, skip when equal.
    includes:             true only if every element of range2 is consumed from range1.

Twist 4: The Output Is Sorted and Stable
All four producers emit in sorted order by construction, because the two-pointer walk visits elements in sorted order. They are also stable in the sense that equal elements preserve their relative source range - the first range's duplicates come before the second range's duplicates when they are equal in value. This is what makes set_union's output "the sorted multiset union" rather than just "a union, in some order".

Twist 5: Destination Rules - back_inserter or Pre-Size
Both algorithms write to an OutputIterator destination. There are two safe ways to provide it:

- std::back_inserter(out): appends via push_back, grows the container, safe for empty destinations. Used in both tests.
- Pre-size the destination and pass begin(): e.g. out.resize(v1.size() + v2.size()) then set_union(..., out.begin()). The maximum possible output length is N + M, but the actual length depends on the data.

The unsafe way is passing begin() on an empty container: the algorithm writes past end() and the program crashes or corrupts memory. This is the same trap repeated from Notes 93, 97, 98, 100, 103, 104, and 110.

Twist 6: Iterator Requirement Is Weak
Source iterators need only InputIterator. The destination needs only OutputIterator. Neither needs random access. In test0 and test1, the destination is a std::list<int> with a back_insert_iterator - a container that has BidirectionalIterator, not RandomAccess. The algorithms compile and run correctly because they only ever advance forward. This contrasts sharply with std::sort (Note 107), nth_element (Note 109), and partial_sort (Note 109), which all require RandomAccessIterator. The rule holds across the family: linear scans need Input, output production needs Output, only reordering algorithms demand random access.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Assuming set_union returns a true mathematical set: it is a multiset operation that preserves duplicates by count.
2. Calling set_union or set_intersection on unsorted input: undefined behaviour, output is garbage.
3. Forgetting to deduplicate before set_union when true set semantics are wanted: use sort + unique first (Note 103).
4. Passing begin(out) on an empty destination: undefined behaviour, use back_inserter or pre-size.
5. Expecting set_intersection output to be empty when one range is a subset: it returns the smaller count, not zero.
6. Using std::set<int> as the destination and expecting no duplicates: std::set deduplicates at insertion, which silently changes the multiset semantics.
7. Assuming O(M log N) per-element search: the family is O(N + M) merge walk, not repeated binary search.
8. Trying to produce output into the same container as an input: overlapping source and destination is undefined behaviour, use a temp container.
9. Confusing set_difference direction: set_difference(A, B) removes B's elements from A, not the other way round.
10. Using includes when an output range is needed: includes is a bool predicate, not a producer.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Merging sorted inventory lists: set_union to combine two sorted stock lists, correctly accounting for duplicate quantities.
- Common-record detection: set_intersection to find records present in both sorted datasets, respecting multiplicity when the same record appears multiple times in each.
- Version graph analysis: set_difference to find dependencies present in version A but missing in version B.
- Symmetric diff for data sync: set_symmetric_difference to find records that are in one shard but not the other, in either direction.
- Multiset arithmetic: stats pipelines where counts matter - set_intersection gives the min count, set_union gives the max count, both in one linear pass.
- Pipeline building block: set_intersection is the "common keys" step in a sorted join, set_union is the "combined key set" step, both O(N + M) after sort.

Core Rule for Interviews:
set_union and set_intersection are multiset operations on two sorted ranges, not set operations on std::set. The multiplicity rules are max(count1, count2) for union and min(count1, count2) for intersection. Both require sorted input - sort first, always, and use is_sorted when the input source is untrusted (Note 108). Both run in O(N + M) via a merge walk, not per-element binary search. Sources need InputIterator, destinations need OutputIterator, so list and back_inserter are fine - only reordering algorithms demand RandomAccess. Destination must be pre-sized or use back_inserter; begin() on an empty container is undefined behaviour. Same four lessons as Notes 106, 110, 111, and 112: sorted precondition, multiplicity, merge-walk cost, and destination safety.

114. STD::SET_DIFFERENCE AND STD::SET_SYMMETRIC_DIFFERENCE - DIRECTIONAL VERSUS SYMMETRIC, AND THE COMPLETE SET-OPERATIONS FAMILY

Basic Definition:
- std::set_difference(first1, last1, first2, last2, d_first) produces the multiset difference A minus B.
- Each value x appears max(0, count1(x) - count2(x)) times in the output.
- std::set_symmetric_difference(first1, last1, first2, last2, d_first) produces the symmetric difference.
- Each value x appears abs(count1(x) - count2(x)) times in the output.
- Both require both input ranges to be sorted by the same ordering.
- Both run in O(N + M) with a single merge walk.
- Both require InputIterator for sources and OutputIterator for the destination.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - set_difference with v1 minus v2:
   - v1 = {1, 2, 3}, sorted.
   - v2 = {1}, sorted.
   - std::set_difference writes into out via back_inserter.
   - The value 1 appears in both, count1 - count2 = 0, so it is excluded.
   - The values 2 and 3 appear only in v1, so they are kept.
   - Output: 2 3.
2. test1 - set_symmetric_difference with partially overlapping ranges:
   - v1 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}, sorted.
   - v2 = {6, 7}, sorted.
   - std::set_symmetric_difference writes into out via back_inserter.
   - Values 1 through 5 appear only in v1, kept.
   - Value 6 appears in both, count1 - count2 = 0, excluded.
   - Value 7 appears only in v2, kept.
   - Output: 1 2 3 4 5 7.
3. Destination is std::list<int> out with std::back_inserter:
   - No size known in advance.
   - back_inserter calls push_back on the list for each output element.
   - Safe across all container types, including list - the same pattern from Notes 93, 97, 98, 100, 103, 104, 110, and 113.
4. Both inputs are correctly sorted:
   - test0's v2 = {1} is trivially sorted.
   - test1's v2 = {6, 7} is sorted ascending.
   - Unlike the unsorted v2 in Note 112's test0, there is no precondition violation here.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: set_difference Is Directional, Not Symmetric
This is the single most important distinction in the family. set_difference(A, B) removes B's contribution from A and emits the remaining A-only elements. set_difference(B, A) emits the remaining B-only elements. The results are different unless the two ranges are disjoint. In test0, set_difference(v1, v2) gives {2, 3}, but set_difference(v2, v1) would give an empty output because every element of v2 (just 1) is also in v1. The direction matters, and the algorithm name alone does not tell you which range is subtracted - you have to read the argument order. Interviewers use this to check whether you know that A minus B is not the same as B minus A.

Twist 2: set_symmetric_difference Is Commutative
This is the opposite of set_difference. set_symmetric_difference(A, B) equals set_symmetric_difference(B, A). The reason is the multiplicity rule: abs(count1 - count2) is symmetric in the two counts. In test1, swapping v1 and v2 would still produce {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7}, because the values only in either range are the same set, and the values in both (6) are excluded either way. The output order is sorted either way. This symmetry is the mathematical definition of symmetric difference, and it is why this algorithm does not have a "reversed" variant.

Twist 3: The Complete Family - One Skeleton, Five Algorithms
All five sorted-range set algorithms share the same two-pointer merge walk from Note 110 and Note 112. The only difference is the emission logic:

    set_union:                emit both heads on equality, emit the smaller otherwise.
    set_intersection:         emit only when heads are equal.
    set_difference:           emit head1 only when head1 < head2 (A minus B).
    set_symmetric_difference: emit the smaller head, skip on equality.
    includes:                 return bool: true only if every element of range2 was consumed from range1.

Every one of them requires sorted input, runs in O(N + M), needs InputIterator for sources, and (for the four producers) needs OutputIterator for the destination. Once you understand the skeleton, the family is a one-line change to the emission rule. This is why mastering one of them - typically set_union - makes the entire family trivial.

Twist 4: Multiplicity Arithmetic Across the Family
The four producer algorithms are exactly the four ways to combine two counts into one:

    set_union:                max(count1, count2)
    set_intersection:         min(count1, count2)
    set_difference:           max(0, count1 - count2)
    set_symmetric_difference: abs(count1 - count2)

These four formulas are the entire family. Notice that set_union and set_intersection both produce a count that is always non-negative; set_difference clamps to zero; set_symmetric_difference takes the absolute value. This is why set_difference is directional (count1 - count2 vs count2 - count1) while set_symmetric_difference is not. The multiplicity view makes the algorithms feel inevitable - they are just four ways to combine two non-negative integers.

Twist 5: Destination Rules - back_inserter or Pre-Size
Both algorithms write to an OutputIterator destination. The two safe patterns are the same as everywhere in the family:

- std::back_inserter(out): appends via push_back, grows the container, safe for empty destinations. Used in both tests.
- Pre-size and pass begin(): out.resize(v1.size() + v2.size()) then set_difference(..., out.begin()). The maximum possible output length is N + M, but the actual length depends on the data.

The unsafe pattern is begin() on an empty container: the algorithm writes past end() and the program crashes or corrupts memory. Same trap from Notes 93, 97, 98, 100, 103, 104, 110, and 113. Interviewers re-use this snippet because it is the most reliable way to see whether a candidate knows the destination rule.

Twist 6: set_difference Is the Building Block for Sorted Set Subtraction
In practice, set_difference is used to compute "what is in A that is not in B". This shows up in every kind of sorted-diff workload: comparing two sorted snapshots of a database, removing a blacklist from an allowlist, computing the missing dependencies for a build. set_symmetric_difference is used to compute "what is in exactly one of A and B", which shows up in "find all changes" workloads where you care about additions and removals but not the unchanged middle. Both are the O(N + M) version of a set-difference query, and both are far faster than the naive hash-set or scan-based approach when the inputs are already sorted.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Reversing set_difference arguments and expecting the same answer: set_difference(A, B) is not symmetric.
2. Assuming set_symmetric_difference needs a specific argument order: it is commutative, abs count difference is symmetric.
3. Passing an unsorted range: undefined behaviour, same precondition trap as Notes 110, 112, and 113.
4. Passing begin(out) on an empty destination: undefined behaviour, use back_inserter or pre-size.
5. Using std::set<int> as the destination and expecting duplicates in the symmetric difference: std::set deduplicates silently, which changes the multiset semantics.
6. Confusing set_symmetric_difference with set_intersection: symmetric difference excludes common elements, intersection keeps only common elements.
7. Forgetting that set_difference clamps to zero: if count1 < count2, that value does not appear in the output at all, not with a negative count.
8. Producing output into the same container as an input: overlapping source and destination is undefined behaviour.
9. Assuming set_difference output length equals v1.size(): it does not, it depends on how many elements of v1 were also in v2.
10. Using includes when the caller needs the "what is missing" list: includes returns bool, set_difference returns the actual elements.

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Access control: set_difference(all_permissions, granted_permissions) gives the missing permissions to report.
- Blacklist and allowlist filtering: set_difference(allowlist, blacklist) gives the surviving entries, sorted.
- Snapshot diff: set_difference(yesterday, today) gives deletions, set_difference(today, yesterday) gives additions, set_symmetric_difference gives all changes.
- Dependency resolution: set_difference(required, installed) gives the missing dependencies to fetch.
- Database replication: set_symmetric_difference(primary, replica) gives the records that differ in exactly one side - the reconciliation set.
- Version graph analysis: set_difference(version_A, version_B) identifies breaking changes in A's dependency set, and symmetric difference identifies every version in either graph that is not in the other.

Core Rule for Interviews:
set_difference is directional: A minus B is not the same as B minus A. set_symmetric_difference is commutative: abs(count1 - count2) does not care about argument order. Both are multiset operations, not set operations, so duplicates are respected by the count formulas. Both require sorted input, run in O(N + M) via a merge walk, and need only InputIterator on sources plus OutputIterator on the destination. The full family is exactly four count formulas - max, min, max(0, c1 - c2), abs(c1 - c2) - plus the includes predicate, all sharing the same skeleton. Destination must be pre-sized or use back_inserter; begin() on an empty container is undefined behaviour. Same four lessons as Notes 106, 110, 111, 112, and 113: sorted precondition, multiplicity, merge-walk cost, destination safety. If you can say the four multiplicity formulas from memory, you know the entire family.

115. STD::MAKE_HEAP, STD::POP_HEAP, AND STD::SORT_HEAP - RAW HEAP ALGORITHMS BEHIND PRIORITY_QUEUE

Basic Definition:
- std::make_heap(first, last) rearranges a range into a max-heap in O(N) using Floyd's build-heap.
- std::push_heap(first, last) restores heap order after a push_back, in O(log N).
- std::pop_heap(first, last) moves the max element to the back and reheapifies the rest, in O(log N).
- std::sort_heap(first, last) sorts a heap range into ascending order, in O(N log N).
- std::is_heap(first, last) checks heap order, std::is_heap_until(first, last) finds the first violation.
- All heap algorithms require RandomAccessIterator and default to max-heap ordering (std::less).
- These are the raw building blocks that std::priority_queue (Note 87) wraps with push, pop, and top.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - make_heap on an unsorted vector:
   - v = {1, 3, 8, 2, 9}.
   - std::make_heap(begin(v), end(v)) rearranges v into a max-heap.
   - After the call, v.front() is 9, the largest element.
   - The internal arrangement is heap-ordered, not fully sorted: children are always less than their parents, but siblings are unordered.
   - The printed layout is implementation-dependent, typically 9 3 8 1 2 on libstdc++, but the invariant is what matters, not the exact positions.
2. test1 - pop_heap extraction loop:
   - v = {1, 3, 8, 2, 9}, not yet a heap.
   - The loop prints v.front(), then calls pop_heap and pop_back.
   - The first print is 1, which is wrong for a heap - the vector was never heapified.
   - pop_heap treats v as a heap, but it is not, so the behaviour is undefined.
   - The corrected loop requires make_heap first. With make_heap, the print order would be 9 8 3 2 1, descending extraction.
   - After the loop, v is empty.
3. test2 - make_heap followed by sort_heap:
   - v = {1, 3, 8, 2, 9}.
   - make_heap establishes the heap invariant.
   - sort_heap repeatedly pops the max to the back, producing ascending order.
   - Output: 1 2 3 8 9, fully sorted.
   - sort_heap destroys the heap invariant in the process - the range is sorted, not a heap, after the call.

DEEP DIVE AND TWISTS (What Interviewers Want to Know):

Twist 1: make_heap Is O(N), Not O(N log N)
This is the most surprising complexity in the heap family. Floyd's build-heap algorithm starts from the middle of the range and sifts each node down. Because most nodes are near the leaves and sift down for very short distances, the total work sums to O(N), not the O(N log N) you would get by calling push_heap N times. In test0, building the heap from 5 elements costs at most 5 comparisons in the worst case, not 5 log 5. Interviewers use this to check whether you know the difference between "insert N times" and "build once".

Twist 2: pop_heap Does Not Shrink the Range
This is the second most common misconception. pop_heap(first, last) swaps the max element to the back of the range (position last-1) and reheapifies the prefix [first, last-1). The range itself does not change size. If you call pop_heap without pop_back, the max element is still in the vector at the back, and calling pop_heap again on the same range will treat it as a heap element, producing garbage. The correct idiom is:

    std::pop_heap(v.begin(), v.end());
    v.pop_back();

The two-step pattern is the raw version of std::priority_queue::pop(), which does both steps internally. Interviewers use this to see if you know that pop_heap and pop_back are two separate operations.

Twist 3: push_heap Does Not Grow the Range Either
The symmetric trap for push_heap. push_heap(first, last) assumes that [first, last-1) is already a heap and that the new element at last-1 needs to be sifted up. It does not push anything into the container. The correct idiom is:

    v.push_back(x);
    std::push_heap(v.begin(), v.end());

Without the push_back, push_heap on an unchanged range will produce undefined behaviour or a corrupted heap. This is the raw version of std::priority_queue::push(), which does both steps internally.

Twist 4: sort_heap Destroys the Heap
sort_heap(first, last) produces a fully sorted range in ascending order by repeatedly calling pop_heap on shrinking prefixes. After the call, the range is sorted, not a heap. If you call pop_heap on the result, behaviour is undefined because the heap invariant no longer holds. This is why sort_heap is the last step: build heap, use it, then sort it and move on. It is also why sort_heap is rarely used in practice - std::sort (Note 107) is faster on most hardware because introsort has much better cache locality than heap sort. sort_heap exists for the case where you already have a heap and want to sort it without re-sorting from scratch, at the cost of no cache optimisation.

Twist 5: Heap Algorithms Are the Engine Behind priority_queue
std::priority_queue<int> is a thin wrapper over these five algorithms plus a std::vector (Note 87). push() = push_back + push_heap. pop() = pop_heap + pop_back. top() = front(). The priority_queue stores the comparator and the container, and every public operation is a one-line delegation to the heap algorithms. If you understand the raw family, the priority_queue is transparent - it is a container adaptor, and the "adaptor" is exactly these function calls. Interviewers use this to check whether you see through the abstraction or only know the adaptor surface.

Twist 6: The Heap Family and Its Iterator Requirement
The full family:

    make_heap:     O(N) build, requires RandomAccessIterator
    push_heap:     O(log N), requires RandomAccessIterator
    pop_heap:      O(log N), requires RandomAccessIterator
    sort_heap:     O(N log N), requires RandomAccessIterator
    is_heap:       O(N) check, requires RandomAccessIterator
    is_heap_until: O(N) find violation, requires RandomAccessIterator

All six require RandomAccessIterator because heap sift-up and sift-down use index arithmetic (2*i + 1 for left child, 2*i + 2 for right child, (i-1)/2 for parent). A list iterator cannot do index arithmetic, so heap algorithms will not compile on list or forward_list. This is the same category rule from Notes 87, 91, 101, 102, 107, 109, and 111. Only random access containers - vector, deque, array - can be heaped.

COMMON INTERVIEW TRAPS (Summary):
1. Calling pop_heap without pop_back: the max element stays in the vector, the range does not shrink, and the next pop_heap is undefined.
2. Calling push_heap without push_back first: the new element does not exist, push_heap operates on stale data.
3. Calling pop_heap or push_heap on a range that was never heapified with make_heap: undefined behaviour.
4. Calling sort_heap on a range that is not a heap: undefined behaviour, the algorithm assumes the heap invariant.
5. Assuming make_heap is O(N log N): it is O(N) with Floyd's build-heap, not N insertions.
6. Assuming sort_heap is faster than sort: it is not, std::sort has better cache locality and wins in practice.
7. Trying to heapify a list or forward_list: does not compile, heap algorithms require RandomAccessIterator.
8. Expecting the heap's internal layout to be sorted: it is heap-ordered, not sorted. Siblings are unordered, only the parent-child invariant holds.
9. Reading v.front() on an empty heap after the extraction loop: undefined behaviour, always guard with !v.empty().
10. Assuming the default heap is min-heap: it is max-heap, use std::greater<int> for min-heap.
11. Dereferencing the returned iterator from is_heap_until without checking it != end: same trap as is_sorted_until (Note 108).

BROAD WAY TO SEE IT (Real-World Use Cases):
- Priority queues from scratch: the heap family is the entire machinery under std::priority_queue, useful when you need custom container or allocator behaviour without the adaptor.
- Heap sort: make_heap plus sort_heap is the classic heap sort, O(N log N) worst case, in-place, unstable. Rarely faster than introsort but useful as a reference implementation.
- Streaming top-K: maintain a heap of size K, use push_heap and pop_heap to keep the K largest or smallest elements while streaming. This is the raw version of the priority_queue top-K pattern from Note 87.
- Dijkstra and A*: the frontier is a min-heap, built and maintained with these algorithms. Understanding the raw family is what lets you tune the frontier for cache and allocation behaviour.
- Real-time scheduling: OS schedulers and task queues use max-heaps to always execute the highest-priority task next. The raw algorithms give you explicit control over when to heapify, when to extract, and when to sort.
- Merging K sorted streams: a min-heap of stream heads drives K-way merge. Build once, pop the smallest, push the next element from the same stream. The heap family is the engine.

Core Rule for Interviews:
Heap algorithms are a paired family: make_heap builds, push_heap and pop_heap maintain, sort_heap destroys, is_heap checks. make_heap is O(N), not O(N log N) - Floyd's build-heap is the reason. pop_heap and push_heap do not change the range size; the caller must pair them with pop_back and push_back respectively. This is exactly what std::priority_queue hides, and understanding the raw family makes the adaptor transparent. sort_heap requires a heap precondition, destroys the invariant, and produces ascending order - it is the raw form of heap sort, but std::sort usually wins on cache. All heap algorithms require RandomAccessIterator, so vector, deque, and array work but list and forward_list do not. The default ordering is max-heap; std::greater<int> flips it to min-heap. And the same rule from Notes 87 and 91: heap algorithms are the engine, priority_queue is the surface - know both.

116. STD::MIN_ELEMENT AND STD::MAX_ELEMENT - COMPARATOR CONTRACTS, TIE BEHAVIOR, AND THE EMPTY RANGE TRAP

Basic Definition:
- std::min_element(first, last) returns an iterator to the smallest element in a range.
- std::max_element(first, last) returns an iterator to the largest element in a range.
- Both accept an optional custom comparator as the third argument.
- Both return end() if the range is empty.
- Both run in O(N) - exactly N-1 comparisons.
- Both require ForwardIterator, not RandomAccessIterator.
- When ties exist, min_element and max_element return the first occurrence.

Observations from your Code (API Breakdown):
1. test0 - min_element and max_element on integers:
   - v = {9, 4, 2, 5, 1, 0, 8, 3}.
   - std::min_element returns an iterator to 0.
   - std::max_element returns an iterator to 9.
   - Output: 0, then 9, because the iterators are dereferenced.
2. test1 - custom comparator on a Monstar struct:
   - Monstar is a plain struct with int power, int strength.
